Hilton Food · HFG-GB · the new Memo conveyor's card of 28 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Hilton Food

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 47,217 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

F4429th of 36

Memo topic 1, Tailwinds in the operating environment: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external environment sets a ceiling on Hilton's earnings, not a floor under them. The one strong, durable external tailwind (grocers outsourcing own-label protein to scaled contract partners) shows up as new contracts and new countries, which arrive slowly and on the company's capital. The headwinds (a beef supply squeeze to 2028 with Australia worst from 2027, retailer volume and promotional decisions, a United States regulator holding a plant hostage, UK labour costs) act on profit per kilogram and on West volumes now. The cost-plus contract turns raw-material inflation into revenue rather than lost margin, which is why the business survives these forces, but four channels leak through it: the annual pricing lag, consumer mix, finance cost on inflated working capital, and supply availability. For the model: (1) do not let revenue growth drive profit; (2) hold West volumes at 0 to 1 percent and East at about 3 percent; (3) carry an Australian profit-per-kilogram risk from FY2027; (4) let the Australian dollar add to FY2026 but not compound; (5) treat external forces as capping operating profit growth at mid-single digits in the existing estate. The most likely external surprise in 18 months is negative (Australian cattle, a customer sourcing shift); the most likely positive surprise is a United States re-approval for Foppen.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0230C-0528C-0744C-0942C-3548C-3930
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

F3031st of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: intensity is rising, and the competition that matters is not other packers but the customers and the primary processors. It has tightened over the last 6 to 18 months on three fronts at once, and the next 6 months show no easing.

  • Customers competing for the margin: Tesco moved to 100 percent Scottish beef for Scottish stores and Hilton ceased supplying that stream inside a renewed partnership (H1 FY26), partly offset by lamb; Dutch retailers cut promotions and Dutch volumes fell; Lidl Ireland accepted "a significant financial hit" to hold a 15 to 20 percent price gap. West core meat adjusted operating profit fell 20.5 percent to £28.0m in H1 FY26, at a 2.8 percent margin.
  • Primary processors moving up the chain: ABP and Kepak (expert testimony); retailers favour suppliers with captive raw material since the pandemic; Edeka owns processing; Morrisons makes about a quarter of its own fresh food.
  • Transactional packers are weakening, which is mildly favourable: expert testimony describes "severe financial stress" among non-partnered suppliers unable to fund automation.
  • Against the last 12 to 18 months: in FY2025 the pressure came from inflation and pass-through lag; in 2026 it has moved to volume allocation (Scotland) and promotional withdrawal. That shift matters because volume losses fall straight onto a fixed cost base.
  • Peer contrast: Cranswick and Greencore report rational, utilisation-focused competition among processors and gain share from weaker rivals; Hilton's exposure is different because it competes for the customer's sourcing decision, not for shelf share.
C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

F2833rd of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: a headwind, and the most important external force in the next 18 months to three years. Hilton passes raw-material cost through, so the damage does not arrive as cost; it arrives through four channels the pass-through does not cover: the lag, consumer mix and volume, working-capital finance cost, and supply availability.

  • Beef: European retail beef prices over 50 percent higher year-on-year, UK cattle over 20 percent; Irish cattle throughput 16 percent below the prior year; global beef production projected -2 percent in 2027 and -3 percent in 2028; Australian cattle availability expected to fall sharply in 2027 and 2028 after two years of heavy cow slaughter (Morgan Stanley); JBS reports Australian cattle prices up about 10 percent quarter-on-quarter in late 2026. Australia is the critical point, because Oceania is Hilton's most profitable region and the earnings research names an Oceania margin below 3.0 percent as a thesis-breaker.
  • Fish: haddock up to 90 percent and cod 30 percent on quota cuts; white-fish demand remains depressed at Seachill; salmon raw-material inflation drove Foppen's loss.
  • The four channels: (1) the lag: UK processing costs are fixed annually (Tesco's open-book model), so energy and labour inflation are absorbed for up to 12 months; (2) mix and volume: trade-down to mince and lighter weights; (3) finance cost, the least-noticed channel: customer-provided supply-chain finance funds about £150m of working capital, and its interest rose to £9.6m in FY2025 from £4.5m as higher meat prices inflated inventory and receivables; (4) availability: retailers increasingly favour suppliers with captive supply.
  • Labour and energy: labour is the cost the contracts protect least (the Sector's weak point); Brent at about $111 lifts energy, packaging and freight.
  • Supplier concentration: rising; primary processing is consolidating and some suppliers (ABP, Kepak) are also competitors. Hilton owns no farms, feed or slaughter.
  • Offsets: pork is in European oversupply (African swine fever in Spain), which helps mixed-protein reformulation; sugar and some grains are easing (minor).
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

C+4411th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: macro acts on Hilton mainly through currency translation and interest cost, which bite at the company level harder than at the sector level; consumer weakness bites at the industry level through mix; the regime's defensive rotation is a sector-level tailwind that Hilton's small size and momentum partly forfeit.

  • Company level, currency: about two-thirds of revenue is outside the UK; Australian and New Zealand dollars are 36.78 percent of revenue and the largest profit pool. The stronger Australian dollar supported the FY2026 guidance raise. Sensitivity: a 10 percent sterling move is £2.2m of profit and £20.6m of equity (FY2025); forecasters expect moderate sterling weakness against the euro over 12 to 24 months (a mild further tailwind). Hedging is short (over 90 percent of forwards mature inside six months).
  • Company level, rates: the Bank of England is expected to raise to 4.00 percent on 5 November 2026 and about 4.25 percent by mid-2027; a 50 basis-point rate rise is about £2.1m of interest. Net bank debt peaks around FY2026 at about 1.4x.
  • Industry level, consumer: UK inflation 3.5 percent, 2027 growth cut to 1.0 percent, energy cap up about 16 percent in January 2027; this drives trade-down within beef rather than lower total protein volume.
  • Sector level, regime: risk-off, defensives leading; the Fitness for Fighting tilts are Overweight for Consumer staples and Food production, but Underweight for Momentum and Double Underweight for Fragility (small, multi-part cases).
  • Energy and geopolitics: Brent about $111 and the Strait of Hormuz affect energy, packaging and the Saudi venture's supply chain.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–42

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: three further dynamics matter, two negative and one positive: customer-side corporate change (consolidation and leadership change at Hilton's own customers), animal-disease and food-safety events across the chain, and the stronger competitive position of Hilton's anchor customers.

  • Negative, customer corporate change: a possible Sainsbury's and Morrisons merger is named in the Sector's threat list; Ahold Delhaize is replacing its group, Europe and United States chief executives at once (Bernstein), a moment when sourcing policies can change.
  • Negative, disease and safety: foot-and-mouth in Germany and African swine fever in Spain triggered export bans; any Listeria finding at a Dutch Foppen site is named by the risks research as a thesis-terminator; a third-party warehouse fire in California added to Foppen's H1 FY26 costs.
  • Positive, anchor customers are winners: Tesco gaining share, Woolworths growing own-brand, Żabka expanding into Romania, Walmart diversifying supply. A packer tied to the winning grocer in each country gains volume as the grocer gains share.
  • Neutral, climate: Queensland heat and water stress is the highest physical climate risk (Australian water-efficiency plan of 10 percent).
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

D4820th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the only real threat is the adjacent entrant (a primary processor or the retailer itself taking packing in-house), and it is a moderate and slowly rising threat. Standard and innovative entrants are not a threat.

  • Adjacent, moderate and rising: primary processors (ABP, Kepak, Vion) adding packing; retailers integrating (Edeka's processing network; Morrisons making about a quarter of its fresh food; Danish Crown testimony on retailers moving into slaughter). Against this, Greencore's research concludes structural insourcing risk "remains highly limited" because grocers spend capital on warehouses and stores, and Sainsbury's committed £5bn to contract farming rather than ownership.
  • Standard, low: an automated co-located plant needs a retailer partner and tens of millions of capital; there is no spot market to enter.
  • Innovative, nil: alternative protein retreated; cultivated meat banned in Italy and Hungary; fermentation a 2030s question.
  • A different kind of entrant: a competitor willing to accept lower returns bidding for a mature contract at renewal (the research's own caveat).
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

F3028th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: suppliers are a source of risk, rising through 2028. Hilton owns no animals, feed or slaughter; its suppliers are consolidating, some compete with it, and their raw material is becoming scarce. The cost-plus contract moves the price risk to the retailer, but availability, mix and the timing of pass-through stay with Hilton.

  • Scarcity: global beef production -2 percent in 2027 and -3 percent in 2028; Australian cattle availability falling sharply in 2027 to 2028; Irish throughput -16 percent; UK herd declining; white-fish quotas cut; Peruvian fishmeal up about 90 percent lifting salmon costs.
  • Supplier power: primary processors set initial cost structures and some move into packing; in APAC the retailer (Woolworths) owns the cattle, which protects Hilton's fee-per-kilogram margin there but also means Hilton's Australian volume depends on Woolworths' supply.
  • Mitigation: a global red-meat sourcing centre at Huntingdon; dairy-herd and imported beef; mixed-protein reformulation; hake substitution; 87 percent farm-assured livestock and 99.7 percent MSC-certified wild fish (a capability retailers value).
  • Hilton's own financing of suppliers: a £24.5m supplier finance programme paying suppliers early while Hilton settles 30 days later; this supports supplier relationships and flatters Hilton's payables.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0155C-0356C-0438C-0868C-3252C-3734C-4345
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

D5524th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: demand for Hilton's service (outsourced, automated retail packing) is growing structurally and is highly visible; demand for its product (red meat) is flat in volume and deteriorating in mix for cyclical reasons that persist to 2028. The net is low-single-digit volume growth with unusually high visibility, plus a small, fast structural niche in Central European convenience.

  • Three layers of demand move differently. (a) Retailers' demand to outsource own-label protein to a few scaled contract makers is the Sector's strongest theme, rated "Big" by the Assistant Portfolio Manager (15 October 2026); Hilton's new partners (Walmart Canada, NADEC in Saudi Arabia, Żabka into Romania) are this structural demand arriving. (b) Consumer volume demand for red meat is flat: group volumes +0.2 percent in FY2025, +2.1 percent in H1 FY26; West core meat +0.6 percent. (c) Mix is deteriorating cyclically: shoppers trade from steak (17 to 23 percent margin to the retailer chain) into mince (2 to 5 percent) and lighter packs because retail beef prices rose 30 to 50 percent.
  • The structural niche: Central European fresh prepared food volumes +26 percent in H1 FY26, underpinned by Żabka's 11,000 stores adding about 1,000 a year and a market growing about 8 percent a year. It is high-growth but small in a £4.2bn revenue base (its profit share is not disclosed).
  • Visibility is the strongest feature. Five-to-fifteen-year contracts, renewals in 2025-26 at Tesco, Albert Heijn, Coop and Salling, and Canada's offtake committing 100 percent of plant capacity to Walmart give two-plus years of volume visibility on most of the base. What visibility does not cover is the customer's right to move a stream (Tesco's Scottish beef) and consumer elasticity.
  • Cycle reading: the cyclical mix headwind has two-plus years of visibility in the wrong direction: Morgan Stanley projects global beef production down 2 percent in 2027 and 3 percent in 2028, with Australian cattle availability falling sharply from 2027.
  • For the model: volume growth of about 2 percent a year in the existing estate (East about 3 percent, West 0 to 1 percent), plus new-geography volume from FY2027; do not extrapolate the 26 percent Polish figure at group level.
C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

C+5610th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the disruption that threatened a red-meat packer (plant-based protein) has been disproved, and the disruption Hilton deploys (automation and machine-learning line control) is strengthening in its favour. The live structural threat is not technological but organisational: vertical integration by retailers and primary processors.

  • Disproved: plant-based demand is in retreat in Western markets; meat substitutes are under 1.5 percent of the UK market and unlikely to exceed 3 percent (Jefferies); Hilton sold Dalco for £5.4m after a £16.7m impairment; cultivated meat is banned in Italy and Hungary. Fermentation-derived protein is a 2030s question.
  • Strengthening in Hilton's favour: end-of-line robotics and machine-learning pack-weight control (Huntingdon, being rolled out) typically cut labour participation about 10 percent per site; UK National Insurance rises shortened paybacks. This widens the gap over transactional packers.
  • A possible new tailwind: weight-loss drugs push demand toward protein (the Sector's fourth story); this favours protein over snacks and is weakly positive for Hilton.
  • Strengthening against Hilton: the integration trend (the advantaged party is the one that owns the animal: Cranswick's 55 percent pig self-sufficiency and 18.5 percent return on capital).
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

F3828th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: regulation is a net, live cost now and a modest tailwind later. One regulator (the United States Food and Drug Administration) drives a material, unresolved loss; labour-cost policy in the United Kingdom is a steady headwind; Saudi food-security policy is a tailwind not yet in the numbers.

  • Foppen (Greek smoked salmon plant): United States export restrictions after Listeria detection in 2025 cost £27.6m of exceptional charges in FY2025 (£18.4m inventory, £9.2m relocation and airfreight) and £7.0m in H1 FY26 (including £3.0m production inefficiency and £2.1m first-quarter airfreight), plus a £2.2m adjusted operating loss at Foppen in H1 FY26. A re-approval petition followed a late-2025 audit that went "exceptionally well" by the company's account, but the restriction has outlasted every date the company gave. The timing is outside management's control.
  • Labour policy: UK National Living Wage up about 6.7 percent and employer National Insurance up 1.2 points; cost-plus contracts do not clearly pass labour through within the year.
  • European rules: the deforestation regulation applies from 30 December 2026 and covers cattle and soy, raising traceability cost on South American beef imports; the packaging regulation favours Hilton (82 percent recycled content; tray-to-tray trial with Tesco).
  • Tailwind: Saudi Vision 2030 food security backs the NADEC venture (launch Q4 2026); the shift from in-store butchery to pre-packed meat is policy-supported.
  • UK fiscal backdrop: the 28 October 2026 Budget left a stagflationary 2027 (inflation peaking about 3.75 percent, Bank Rate about 4.25 percent); consumer-facing, not company-specific.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

C6812th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: yes, one sector-level driver is accelerating Hilton's demand for new business, and it is the right one for this business model: grocers moving own-label protein and fresh food to a few large contract makers on long cost-plus contracts. It accelerates contract wins and geographic expansion more than it accelerates volume in existing plants.

  • Evidence: own-label above half of UK grocery sales; Tesco projected to gain about 40 basis points of volume share a year through FY2028 by investing in own-label value tiers (Hilton is Tesco's packer); Ahold Delhaize own-brand above 40 percent and targeting 45 percent of European food sales by 2028; Woolworths own and exclusive brands +5.5 percent in its 2026 year; Walmart wants supply diversification away from the four big North American packers and to replicate the dedicated-plant model in other geographies.
  • Second driver, convenience: fresh prepared food in Central Europe (Żabka) and the UK chilled consolidation (Greencore with Bakkavor) show the same outsourcing logic in prepared food.
  • Limits: Bernstein calls private label "a mainly cyclical economic threat", growing "only 8 basis points annually" in the UK from 2015 to 2024; the counter-trend is retailers and primary processors integrating, and Nomad cutting externally made volumes.
  • Translation to the model: the driver supports new-geography revenue (Canada from 2027, Saudi Arabia equity-accounted from late 2026, Romania) rather than higher growth in the mature UK and Dutch base.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

D5224th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: customer (retailer) demand supports the thesis in volume and is fragile in allocation. The retailers Hilton serves are gaining share and expanding own-label, which supports volume; each retains and uses the right to re-allocate streams, which is where the fragility sits.

  • Supportive: Tesco gaining share and investing in own-label value tiers; Woolworths own-brand +5.5 percent; Ahold own-brand above 40 percent; Żabka adding 1,000 stores a year; Walmart diversifying supply; NADEC moving Saudi consumers from in-store butchery to packaged meat. Renewals in 2025-26.
  • Consumer layer beneath: group volumes +2.1 percent (H1 FY26) through 20 to 30 percent beef inflation; UK core retail meat volumes grew 1.7 percent in H1 FY25 while the market fell 2.7 percent; but trade-down to mince and lighter packs erodes value mix; UK white-fish demand depressed by price.
  • Fragility: Tesco's Scottish beef switch (H1 FY26), Dutch promotional withdrawal, Tesco's tactical 5 to 7 percent shifts. A customer concentrated at 85 to 90 percent of UK sales means one decision moves a region.
  • For the model: existing-customer volume growth of about 2 percent, with a probability-weighted allowance for allocation shifts in the West of about 1 percent of West volume a year (our judgement, reflecting 5 to 7 percent tactical shifts that occur only occasionally), and new-customer volume from Canada and Poland.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

F3431st of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: regulators and political actors hurt more than they help today. The United States Food and Drug Administration holds Foppen's Greek plant out of its most profitable market; UK labour policy raises cost; European traceability and packaging rules add compliance cost; Saudi policy is the one supportive actor. The position is fragile because the largest item, the United States decision, is binary and outside the company's control.

  • Hurting: Foppen (£34.6m of exceptional cost over FY2025 and H1 FY26 plus operating losses); National Living Wage and National Insurance; the deforestation regulation from 30 December 2026 (cattle and soy in scope); fragmented unfair-trading-practices rules affecting payment terms (minor).
  • Neutral to helpful: the packaging regulation (Hilton ahead at 82 percent recycled content); animal welfare tightening (raises integrated farmers' costs more than a packer's).
  • Helping: Saudi Vision 2030 food security; Canadian and Saudi governments receptive to domestic processing.
  • Domestic audit regulators: the Financial Reporting Council's review of the 2024 audit and Deloitte's control findings are scrutiny, not cost.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

F4532nd of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the theoretical market (retail packed protein in developed and developing markets) is many times Hilton's size and does not constrain growth; the realistic market is the number of leading grocers willing to outsource packing to a partner, which yields about one new country every one to two years. That allows mid-single-digit profit growth plus occasional steps, not 12 to 20 percent.

  • Existing estate: volume growth of about 2 to 3 percent a year (category growth, retailer share gains, secondary customers).
  • New geographies: Saudi Arabia (food imports historically about 80 percent of demand), Canada (Walmart's 400 stores, initially Eastern Canada), Romania; Walmart's interest in other North American geographies is the largest realistic extension.
  • Category extension: fresh prepared food (Poland market growth about 8 percent a year; UK convenience a £50bn market where Greencore leads), bacon, frozen burgers.
  • Rate allowed: about 3 to 5 percent volume growth a year with a new-country step every one to two years, consistent with management's mid-single-digit operating profit target; faster only if Walmart replicates.
1c

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0658C-1043
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

D5819th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the company's profit cycle is at its trough in FY2026 and turning, but the turn is so far technical in the profit line and real only in volumes and cash. The revenue cycle is at a peak of inflation and will decelerate, which is harmless.

  • Profit cycle: adjusted profit before tax £66.0m (FY2023), £76.1m (FY2024), £73.2m (FY2025), guided £66m to £71m (FY2026), consensus £74.0m (FY2027) and £79.6m (FY2028). Adjusted EPS 61.0p (FY2024), 56.0p (FY2025), consensus 52.4p (FY2026), 57.6p (FY2027), 62.6p (FY2028).
  • What has turned: volumes (+2.1 percent in H1 FY26 against +0.2 percent in FY2025); adjusted free cash flow (+£10.4m against -£30.8m); revisions (+8 to 9 percent over three months); losses being removed (Dalco gone; Seachill improving).
  • What has not: constant-currency continuing profit before tax fell 8.9 percent in H1 FY26; profit per kilogram fell from 18.7p to 17.7p; the guidance raise was Dalco's exclusion (about £3.7m) and Australian-dollar translation.
  • Revenue: +15.3 percent reported in H1 FY26 on inflation; consensus +5.6, +2.4 and +4.3 percent for FY2026 to FY2028 on the brokers' basis.
  • What confirms the turn: H2 FY26 adjusted profit before tax at or above about £36m (the guidance midpoint), and FY2027 operating profit growth with West core meat volumes at least flat.
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

F4328th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the external picture is mildly negative for earnings over the next 18 months and roughly neutral over three years. Tailwinds act on Hilton's contract wins and geography; headwinds act on its margin per kilogram and volume in the mature West. The headwinds are larger in the near term, the tailwinds more durable.

Driver by driver:

  • Own-label moving to large contract makers (Sector story, rated Big): tailwind, strong and durable, at value-chain level. Delivers Canada, Saudi Arabia, Romania and secondary customers.
  • Beef to chicken and pork, taken from imports (Sector story): mild headwind. Hilton is beef-heavy; mixed-protein mince is the hedge; the winners are integrated pork and poultry processors (Cranswick, LDC).
  • Cocoa relief: not relevant.
  • Protein for weight-loss-drug users (Sector story): mild tailwind at industry level.
  • Leaving low-margin businesses (Industry story): tailwind, already executed (Dalco, Fairfax Meadow, Foods Connected).
  • Beef supply squeeze to 2028: strong headwind at value-chain level, sharpest in Australia from 2027.
  • Retailer power and integration: moderate headwind, demonstrated in H1 FY26.
  • Regulation (United States ban, UK labour, European rules): moderate headwind; Saudi policy a tailwind.
  • Currency: tailwind now, reversible.
  • Plant-based retreat: mild tailwind.

Overall: headwinds strong-to-moderate on the value-chain level (supply, customers, regulator); tailwinds strong at the sector level (outsourcing) and mild at the industry level. The external environment caps what the internal change can produce at mid-single-digit profit growth.

Internal change forces/risks

B678th of 36

Memo topic 2, Internal change forces: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Hilton is a genuine "Major Corporate Change" case on its inputs and a modest one on its outputs. In ten months a new chief executive, chair and two regional operating chiefs have sold three non-core units at fair prices, ring-fenced seafood, rewritten the capital framework, refinanced, and committed capital to three partner-contracted geographies. The change is credible because it is executed, not announced, and because the chief executive bought shares at the low. It is limited because the business it repairs earns a contractually capped fee per kilogram: removing about £8m of losses and adding new countries one at a time produce 8 to 10 percent EPS growth from a trough, not a re-based earnings trajectory. Two internal signals tell the Consumer to model modestly: management's own incentive hurdles were cut to 3 to 8 percent EPS growth, and FY2026 guidance, even after its raise, is a like-for-like decline. The internal risks are capital creep (Canada, Poland, bacon) against a free cash flow that does not yet cover the dividend, and a seafood exit that could cost more than it saves in the year it happens. For the model: treat FY2026 as the trough, carry Dalco's removal and a partial seafood improvement into FY2027, start Canada small (about £1.5m) and let it ramp only from FY2028, and do not credit incentive-driven ambition above 8 percent EPS growth.

Group judgement

Risks

D4019th of 35

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The risks in this case sit in the structure of the business more than in the change, and they are asymmetric: the upside is bounded by contract and the downside is sudden. The growth engine is not decelerating now (volumes recovering, new geographies starting), so the near-term risk is not a fade but a shock: a core customer re-allocating or re-tendering volume (already demonstrated in Scotland), a food-safety event (already demonstrated at Foppen), or a Canada start-up that slips. Each is low-to-moderate in probability and, through a 2 percent margin and a geared finance line, large in effect. Perception risk is elevated after a 30 percent three-month rise into unanimous Buy ratings in a thin stock, against a regime that has just turned against momentum. On unchanged earnings the fair trough is about 16 to 20 percent below the price; on trough earnings about 35 percent. For the Consumer: carry a probability-weighted haircut for customer re-allocation in the West, a Canada delay scenario, and a seafood scenario with a write-down; and note that the largest structural unknown, the renewal dates of the Tesco UK and Woolworths contracts, is not in the inputs.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

C+629th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: standards are improving visibly in governance, accountability and cost discipline, from a low base, and the auditor's findings show the base was weak. Execution standards in the core were never the problem; the problems were the non-core units and controls.

  • Governance: combined Chair and Chief Executive ended 1 July 2026; new Audit and Remuneration chairs; IT risk and compliance function introduced in 2025; Provision 29 testing plan for late 2026.
  • Controls (the weak base): Deloitte identified deficiencies in monthly reporting and IT user access and did not rely on controls for the 2025 audit; a £6.8m share-based payment restatement; a £20.1m reduction in prior-year revenue for double counting; revenue recognition a key audit matter (manual adjustments); the Financial Reporting Council reviewed the 2024 audit.
  • Cost discipline: Seachill cost-out (management layers removed), "One Hilton" overhead removal (£4.6m transformation cost in H1 FY26, £2.9m reorganisation in 2025), automation, procurement consolidation, transition of Foppen's United States freight from air to sea.
  • People: staff turnover 19 percent in 2025 from 26 percent (2024) and 30 percent (2023) (one source gives 17 percent); lost-time injury frequency down 16 percent; engagement figures differ by source and should not be relied upon.
  • Operational measures: service level about 98.6 percent; Tesco's UK Quality Supplier of the Year.
C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

C-3813th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the negatives are present, not hypothetical, and they cluster in four places: lowered incentive hurdles, governance and control weaknesses, capital creep, and the seafood exit risk. None stops the case; together they explain why the multiple is capped.

  • Lowered incentive hurdles (present, under-noticed): the 2023 long-term incentive plan's EPS target ran from 6.3 percent (threshold) to 11.2 percent (maximum) compound growth and vested at 2.3 percent; the July 2026 awards require only 3 percent (threshold) to 8 percent (stretch). Management is paid in full for 8 percent EPS growth, which is the consensus path. This tells the Consumer that management does not itself expect double-digit EPS growth.
  • Governance and controls (present, receding): auditor's control deficiencies and no reliance on controls for 2025; £6.8m and £20.1m restatements; related-party purchases from Agito (common directors) up to £26.6m from £9.2m; executive directors own under 0.10 percent of the shares (Allen 3.5 percent of salary against a 300 percent guideline); base salary £850,000.
  • Capital creep (plausible): Canada from £60m to £80m with bacon still to come; Poland "bigger than envisaged"; free cash flow already below the dividend in FY2026.
  • Seafood exit risk (plausible): "assessing all options for Foppen"; a distressed sale would crystallise a further write-down.
  • Talent and relationships (plausible): expert testimony of depleted mid-level talent, weak commercial directors, and a weaker top-level Tesco relationship since the founders left.
  • Recurring "exceptional" costs (present): £27.6m Foppen and £5.0m restructuring in FY2025; £21.0m of continuing adjusting items in H1 FY26 (Foppen £7.0m, transformation £4.6m, restructuring £3.2m). Transformation and restructuring recur and are arguably operating costs.
  • Rising finance cost on customer-provided finance (present): £9.6m in FY2025 from £4.5m.
C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

D4824th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement: the culture is congruent in the core (a partner culture built around serving one lead grocer per country with transparency and service) and was incongruent at the edges (acquired seafood and vegan businesses run hands-off with a copied meat template). "One Hilton" is the attempt to make the whole system congruent; it is in its first year, so alignment is a direction, not a fact.

  • Congruent core: open-book transparency, service levels about 98.6 percent, co-development with customers (Tesco packaging, Woolworths promotions), partner rhetoric matching contract structure.
  • Incongruent history: Seachill and Foppen suffered "hands-off management and cultural mismatches"; the seafood "copy-paste" of meat economics failed on supply-chain complexity; founders' relationship culture not replicated after their departure (expert testimony).
  • Improving signals: staff turnover 19 percent in 2025 from 30 percent in 2023; safety programme ("Destination Zero") cut lost-time injuries 16 percent; women 34 percent of leadership roles.
  • Alignment gaps: executive ownership under 0.10 percent; incentive hurdles lowered; a federated, country-by-country organisation being centralised, which risks friction (the research flags union resistance to a standardised global grading structure).
  • Not a Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair: there is no single, self-reinforcing cultural engine; the system is held together by contracts more than by culture.
C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

F3227th of 35

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: two sudden, high-magnitude risks are structural to this business (a core customer contract lost or re-tendered, and a food-safety or animal-disease event), and a 2 percent margin turns either into a large earnings move. Three further risks are medium-sized (a Canada failure, an IT outage, a currency shock). We rank them below with our plausibility over three years and size.

  1. A core contract lost, re-tendered or materially re-allocated (Tesco UK, Woolworths, Ahold). Plausibility: low (about 5 to 10 percent over three years for a material loss; higher for a partial re-allocation like Scottish beef, which has already happened). Size: very large. FY2025 regional profit was UK and Ireland £37.5m and Continental Europe £43.0m with Asia-Pacific the remainder before central costs; Tesco is 85 to 90 percent of UK sales and Woolworths the sole APAC partner, so a lost anchor removes roughly a quarter to a third of group operating profit, plus stranded-asset write-downs (customer credit exposure £387.1m). Warning signs: re-tenders, volume shifts, promotional withdrawal. The inputs do not give renewal dates for Tesco UK or Woolworths.
  2. A food-safety event in the core or a further one in seafood (Listeria at a Dutch Foppen site is named a thesis-terminator). Plausibility: low-to-moderate (about 5 to 10 percent). Size: large; Foppen alone cost £34.6m of exceptional charges in eighteen months; a core-meat recall at a co-located plant would hit volume and the customer relationship.
  3. Animal disease in a sourcing country (foot-and-mouth, African swine fever, avian influenza). Plausibility: low (about 5 percent for a severe UK, Irish or Australian event). Size: medium to large through supply and prices; Hilton owns no animals, so no biological write-down, but supply would tighten.
  4. Canada failure (a launch slip past mid-2027, a cost blowout to £100m, or a ramp that stalls below hurdle). Plausibility: moderate (about 20 to 30 percent for a material slip or overrun). Size: medium; delays FY2027 to FY2028 EPS by 2p to 5p and dents return on capital and the narrative.
  5. Cyber or IT failure (Evolve4 integrated with customers). Plausibility: low; size: medium (weeks of disruption).
  6. Currency shock (sterling +20 percent). Plausibility: low; size: small (about £4m to £5m of profit).
  7. Withdrawal of customer-provided finance (about £150m). Plausibility: very low; size: liquidity only (covered by the £450m facility), plus higher interest.
D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

B588th of 35

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: concentrated, in a way that makes the forecast checkable rather than fragile. Against the last four to six years (revenue up 2.3 times on acquisitions and inflation, the margin halved, EPS flat, free cash flow about nil on average), the forecast differs through six identifiable items, and three of them (one plant, one regulator, one tax rate) account for most of the difference in EPS. Over the window to FY2030, of the £28m rise in operating profit, two plants and a joint venture supply about £12m (43 percent), the removal of seafood losses £4.5m (16 percent), retained overheads £3.5m (12 percent) and the existing estate's volume plus nominal fee creep only £8m (29 percent); below the line, a tax rate of 28 to 28.5 percent against FY2025's 29.8 percent adds about 1.5p, and the finance charge first takes about 2p (FY2027) and then returns it. Over the next 18 months the drivers are more diffuse in the central case (the existing estate's £3m is the largest item in FY2027) but the risk is concentrated in four binary or lumpy items: Canada's commissioning and ramp (about 8p to 10p of FY2027 EPS between the bear and bull cases), Foppen (±3p to 4p of adjusted EPS and £10m to £15m of cash), Australian cattle availability (2p to 4p, mostly FY2028) and a West re-allocation of the Scottish-beef kind (2p to 4p). The AlphaSense driver report reaches the same conclusion ("highly concentrated geographical and operational drivers") and adds one that neither earlier set weighed: the capital intensity of the next two years (about £100m then £80m of capital expenditure, Canada's lease, Poland's build) means any shortfall in these drivers arrives with a higher finance charge attached.

The drivers, ranked by their contribution to the difference between forecast and record, with the EPS swing each carries over 18 months:

Driver Share of the FY2026 to FY2030 operating profit increase What it does in FY2027 Swing in FY2027 EPS, bear to bull How checkable, and when
Canada (Brantford) about 27 percent (£7.5m by FY2030) £0.5m of profit after an H1 loss; depreciation about £4m plus lease costs; bacon late 2027 about 8p to 10p (from -£3m to +£2m of operating profit) Commissioning announced January 2027; first volumes in the May 2027 update; H1 FY27 results September 2027
Seafood (Foppen and Seachill) about 16 percent +£2.0m of adjusted profit; exceptional cash still £6m to £10m about 6p (unresolved with a further loss, to re-approved) Undated; the FDA decision is binary; Seachill's restructuring visible at the FY2026 results
Existing estate (volume about 2 percent, fee creep about 1 percent) about 29 percent (of which overheads £3.5m) +£3m about 10p (volume -1 to +4 percent; profit per kilogram 17.3p to 19.0p) Volumes by region at results only (April and September 2027)

Currency (A$ and NZ$, 37 percent of revenue) below the line a 10 percent sterling move is about £2.2m of profit about 2p Daily
  • Why concentration is more helpful than harmful here. Each concentrated driver has a date and a disclosure: Canada's start is announced; Foppen is binary and management has said it is assessing all options; Australian cattle has public monthly data; the tax rate is guided. The diffuse part (the existing estate) is the part whose record is best. The combination gives the Consumer a short list to re-test at each event rather than a broad margin assumption to defend.
  • The concentration the sets did not model: customers' sourcing decisions. Tesco's Scottish-beef move, Tesco's long-term contracts with Foyle and Kepak for its Sustainable Beef Group (July 2026; over £92m a year), and the CMA's review of ABP's stake in Dovecote Park (initial enforcement order 21 September 2026; decision due in December) are three dated signs of the integration trend the first set called the live structural threat. No broker models it; the second set's 1 percent West allowance is the only quantification in the file. It is the one diffuse risk that could become a concentrated event.
  • Against the record, the difference is not margin. The existing estate's profit per kilogram stays at 18.5p to 19.2p, inside its FY2024 to FY2025 range. Nothing in the forecast asks the core business to earn more per kilogram than it has already earned; it asks the core to earn the same on more kilograms in more plants with fewer losses beside it.

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

B+684th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the change is complete at every level within ten months, and its signal is a deliberate shift from a volume-led, diversifying growth model to an operator-led model of focus, repair and disciplined capital, with stronger regional accountability. The change is credible; its quality is proven only by one half-year.

  • Chief executive: Steve Murrells resigned with immediate effect on 24 to 25 November 2025 after about two years; Mark Allen (12 years chief executive of Dairy Crest to its £1.21bn sale to Saputo) became Executive Chair and, on 1 July 2026, Group Chief Executive. He bought 42,500 shares at 468p on 30 January 2026, at the low.
  • Board: Mark Clare (formerly chief executive of Barratt, finance director of Centrica) Independent Non-Executive Chair from 1 July 2026, ending an eight-month breach of Code Provision 9; Bindi Foyle Audit chair (January 2026); Gwyn Burr (Sainsbury's, ASDA) Remuneration and Sustainability chair (3 September 2026).
  • Operations: two regional Chief Operating Officers from January 2026: Samy Zekhout (West; formerly finance director and deputy chief executive of Nomad Foods) and Melanie Chambers (East; formerly APAC chief executive). Seachill and Foppen under separate dedicated leadership. New General Counsel and a full-time Investor Relations Director.
  • Continuity: Chief Financial Officer Matt Osborne since May 2022 (at Hilton since 2018).
  • What it signals: Allen's own words, "we will invest, but sort the businesses out in the first place", and his Dairy Crest record (value realised through an exit while organic margin fell about 15 basis points a year) point to a portfolio-and-discipline operator rather than a growth builder. Long-term incentive awards in July 2026 (261,883 options to Allen, 171,654 to Zekhout) lock the team in, but on lower hurdles (see C-86).
C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

C6411th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the strategy has changed decisively in perimeter and capital discipline and only modestly in ambition. It is well articulated and adequately resourced. Its design choice, excluding seafood from the targets, makes the earnings stream more predictable and leaves the seafood outcome as an unmodelled option in both directions.

  • The March 2026 framework: three levers (maximise the core, enhance the mix, expand geographically); capital pointed at core red meat, Central European fresh prepared food, Canada and Saudi Arabia; Seachill, Foppen and (then) Dalco outside the targets.
  • Targets: mid-single-digit adjusted operating profit growth on average, explicitly non-linear; net bank debt 1.0x to 2.0x EBITDA; return on capital employed above 20 percent through the cycle (below 20 percent in FY2026); cash conversion about 100 percent of net income excluding growth capital; core capital expenditure £45m to £55m a year; progressive dividend with cover rebuilt toward 2.0x; acquisitions only above the cost of capital on a risk-adjusted, post-tax, unlevered basis.
  • Operating model: "One Hilton" (common procurement, manufacturing excellence across about twenty markets, digital backbone, aligned incentives), East and West reporting, regional Chief Operating Officers.
  • Resourcing: about £100m of capital in FY2026; up to £30m in Poland over two years (possibly larger); a £450m revolving credit facility to 2031; £76m to £80m of disposal proceeds.
  • The modest part: the growth algorithm is unchanged from 2023 (mid-single-digit), now on a narrower base.
C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

F4029th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: guidance has been raised once, technically, after being cut twice; mid-term targets were reset rather than raised. Net, the guidance trajectory over 12 months is down then partly back, and the mid-term ambition is unchanged in rate and narrower in scope.

  • FY2026 profit before tax guidance: set at £60m to £65m in January 2026 (after the November 2025 warning and cuts of about 17 percent and then 7 percent by Shore Capital), raised on 3 September 2026 to £66m to £71m (+9.5 percent at the midpoint). The raise rests on the exclusion of Dalco (about £3.7m) and Australian-dollar translation; Shore Capital called it "a technical adjustment rather than operational surge".
  • Like-for-like reading: on today's perimeter the FY2025 base is about £73m (orientation note 4), so the raised range still implies a 3 to 10 percent decline in FY2026.
  • Mid-term: mid-single-digit operating profit growth (as in November 2023) but now excluding seafood; a leverage floor added (1.0x to 2.0x rather than below 2.0x); cash conversion redefined (about 100 percent of net income, replacing above 1.5x on a pre-capex basis); core capital expenditure widened to £45m to £55m from below £50m.
  • What is absent: no capital markets day, no margin target, no volume target, no Canada profit target beyond "full profit benefits in 2029".

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

A753rd of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: strongly, and at fair prices. Three exits in twelve months removed the units that did not share the core's economics; the remaining non-core unit (seafood) is ring-fenced and under review. This is the cleanest of the internal forces and the one that has already reached the profit line, but it carries a near-term earnings dilution that the Consumer must model.

  • Fairfax Meadow (food service): sold to Sysco GB in September 2025 for £54.4m gross cash, about 7x EBITDA, a £31.0m gain (bought for £23.8m in 2021). Earnings-dilutive: Deutsche Bank cut FY2026 profit before tax by £4.6m; Shore Capital about £3.0m of EBIT; about 5 percent of FY2026 EPS.
  • Foods Connected (software): 65 percent sold to Apax Global Impact in July 2025 for £21.8m cash plus £24.3m of equity; £35.5m gain; 26.3 percent retained with commercial contracts.
  • Dalco (vegan): agreed July 2026 with Livekindly for £5.4m after a £16.7m impairment; completed 9 October 2026; removes a loss of about £3.7m to £4m a year (£2.0m in H1 FY26); Deutsche Bank: outer-year EPS +5 to 6 percent.
  • Seafood (Seachill, Foppen): outside the regional divisions and the targets; "assessing all options for Foppen".
  • Net effect for the model: a smaller, purer base (core meat and convenience at about 90 percent of revenue); a one-off FY2026 earnings dip from Fairfax Meadow; Dalco's removal lifts FY2027; a Foppen resolution or sale is a further £4m to £5m swing (Foppen lost £2.2m in a half plus recurring "exceptional" costs).
C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

B+636th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Hilton is entering three new countries inside 15 months, and the choices are sensible because each is a close adjacency: the same co-located, contracted, single-anchor-retailer model that worked in Australia and Europe, with a named partner carrying the demand risk. The weakness is the capital terms of Canada, where Hilton bears the build-cost inflation and starts at a cost disadvantage, so the return arrives late.

  • Canada (Walmart, Brantford, Ontario): ten-year partnership, multi-protein plant supplying Walmart's roughly 400 Canadian stores (initially Eastern Canada), launch January 2027; budget raised from £60m to £80m (£80.7m spent cumulatively), launch already slipped once; bacon added to scope, with its capital delayed to later in 2027; 100 percent of capacity committed to Walmart. Sourcing is 10 to 15 percent dearer than the four big North American packers at first; Hilton bears build inflation while Walmart's pricing is protected (expert testimony). Shore Capital models a FY2027 contribution of about £1.5m on £65m of sales; management expects "a small low-single-digit profit contribution" in 2027 and full profit benefit in 2029. To earn 20 percent pre-tax on £80m the plant needs about £16m of operating profit, ten times the FY2027 figure (our arithmetic). Walmart's stated wish to replicate the model elsewhere in North America is the largest optional upside in the case.
  • Saudi Arabia (NADEC): 49 percent joint venture, about £6.5m initial investment (49 percent of SAR 60m), ten-year agreement, launch Q4 2026, break-even in year one, about a 20 percent return on capital; Deutsche Bank estimates about £1.3m of net income by year three. Equity-accounted, so revenue is not consolidated. The partner owns cattle, which partly fixes Hilton's usual raw-material weakness. Capital-light and well structured; small.
  • Romania (with Żabka's Froo fascia) and Poland: follows an existing partner; Polish fresh prepared food capacity up to £30m, "bigger than originally envisaged", scoping complete late 2026, capacity on stream about 2028; interim capital-light capacity added.
  • Products: bacon (Canada), frozen burgers in Sweden (new customer Burger King), mixed-protein mince, hake fishcakes. Incremental.
  • Wisdom Library fit: these are core-adjacent moves sharing the core's customers and skills (the "Core versus Adjacency Growth" model's success case), unlike seafood and vegan, which were distant moves that failed.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

D4623rd of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms, and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent?

Judgement: there are no real price rises and none latent. Hilton's revenue growth is nominal pass-through of raw-material cost; its processing fee is set by open-book audit and benchmarking, and management does not present pricing as a lever. Price is not a profit driver in this model; the fee per kilogram is, and it is reset toward a contractual norm.

  • Mechanism: cost-plus or fee-per-kilogram contracts; over 90 percent of raw-material cost passed through; retailers audit costs, track raw-material indices daily and hold benchmarking clauses allowing them to re-tender; UK processing costs fixed annually (so Hilton absorbs in-year inflation in energy and labour).
  • Evidence of the absence: H1 FY26 revenue +15.3 percent while adjusted operating profit fell 3.4 percent; the H1 margin fell 40 basis points; retailers "actively resist real-time price pass-throughs" in rapid inflation.
  • What the Consumer should model instead: fee resets that recover inflation with a lag of up to a year (positive in the year after an inflation spike, as in FY2023 and FY2024), and annualised savings that are partly shared back at renewal.
  • Peer contrast: Cranswick's cost-of-production formulas pass feed and utility costs through "with no lag" and reset quarterly; Greencore recovers about 75 percent of inflation automatically; LDC uses the French EGalim indexation. Hilton's annual reset for non-raw costs is among the slower mechanisms.
C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

C-5515th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: there is no step-up, by design; the period is one of disposals and organic partnership builds. Given the record (seafood acquisitions that failed to integrate and a vegan acquisition sold at a loss), the absence of acquisitions is a positive, and the raised hurdle makes a value-destroying deal less likely.

  • Hurdle: any acquisition must earn risk-adjusted, post-tax, unlevered returns above the cost of capital (about 11 percent weighted average cost of capital in the research); new geographies must clear a 20 percent return on capital employed.
  • Record: Seachill (2017) suffered "hands-off management and cultural mismatches" and delayed synergies for years; the Saucy Fish Co brand was allowed to fade; Foppen became a regulatory and inflation drag; Dalco took a £9.8m impairment in 2024 and £16.7m in 2026 before sale; the 2022 SV Cuisine acquisition was a related-party deal (the former chief executive held 30 percent). Fairfax Meadow was the one acquisition sold at a profit.
  • Capacity to act: leverage at 1.4x and a £450m facility allow a bolt-on, but management's stated priority is to fix and invest organically.
  • Risk to watch: a Foppen sale could be presented as M&A a distressed sale would crystallise a loss.
C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

C5414th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the balance sheet is being used more, for organic capital expenditure (Canada and Poland), within a stated and comfortable range; shareholder returns are flat (the dividend is held while cover rebuilds) and buybacks are not on the agenda, though management has, for the first time, mentioned returning surplus cash if high-return projects do not appear.

  • Leverage: net bank debt £126.7m (0.9x) at FY2025 to £194.4m (1.4x) at H1 FY26; target 1.0x to 2.0x; £450m five-year multi-currency revolving credit facility signed February 2026, maturities to 2031. Including £198.1m of leases, total net debt was £324.8m at FY2025.
  • Capital expenditure: about £100m in FY2026 (£45.3m in H1); consensus £70m (FY2027), £58m (FY2028), £60m (FY2029). Management's core range is £45m to £55m; Shore Capital expects total investment to "rise in 2027 and 2028" for Canada's bacon line and Poland. Our judgement: consensus capital expenditure for FY2027 to FY2028 is more likely too low than too high (core £45m to £55m plus Poland up to £30m plus Canadian bacon), which would lower consensus free cash flow (£45.0m in FY2027, £66.6m in FY2028).
  • Dividend: 35.0p for FY2025 (+1.4 percent; about £31.5m a year in cash), interim held at 10.1p; consensus 35.0p, 35.5p, 35.8p for FY2026 to FY2028; payout about 63 percent of adjusted EPS; cover target 2.0x (about 1.5x on FY2026 consensus). Yield about 4.9 percent at 715.5p.
  • Mooted: Osborne said surplus cash would be returned if attractive reinvestment opportunities did not arise. Not a near-term event: FY2026 consensus free cash flow (£10.6m) does not cover the dividend.
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–56

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: four further positives are present or plausible, none of them large on its own: retained technology value, secondary customers filling capacity, financing structure, and an investor register beginning to include a concentrated holder.

  • Retained 26.3 percent of Foods Connected (with Apax as partner), carried as £24.3m of associate equity; a future exit is optional value; Hilton keeps the platform under long-term contracts.
  • Secondary customers filling capacity: Salling (Denmark, 2024), Netto, Dunnes Stores (Ireland), Burger King (Sweden); each lowers unit cost in an existing plant. This is the multi-customer facility model replacing pure sole supply, a quiet improvement in asset utilisation and in concentration.
  • Financing: a refinanced £450m facility to 2031; customer-provided supply-chain finance at 0.5 to 1.5 percentage points below bank rates (cheap, but see C-86).
  • Ownership: Western Standard (Eric Anderson) holds a notifiable 5.01 percent (July 2026); the chief executive and the Audit chair bought shares in 2026.
  • Plausible: a United States re-approval for Foppen (an earnings upgrade of size in the research's view), a Foppen sale, Walmart replicating the model, a Woolworths range extension (13 South Island stores on trial), IFRS 18 forcing clearer adjusted measures from FY2027.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

D5011th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: there is no controlling actor and no activist; the register is passive and the board, not shareholders, has driven the change. The one new concentrated holder is a watch item, not a force.

  • Register: no ultimate controlling party; single share class; co-founders Philip Heffer (4.74 percent) and Robert Heffer (3.19 percent), Robert Watson (about 2.04m shares); Western Standard 5.01 percent (TR-1, 22 July 2026); BlackRock Throgmorton Trust held a short in FY2025.
  • No activist in the research library; no proxy campaign in the last 12 months.
  • The board's own change (new chair, new committee chairs) is the vehicle of change; the 2025 AGM gave Allen only 79.5 percent support at AG Barr (another company), a sign that some investors watch his record.
  • Takeover angle: no poison pills, but major retail supply contracts allow customer termination on a change to an unacceptable owner, which deters a bid and removes a valuation floor that cheap UK companies otherwise enjoy (the Geography Digest notes private equity closing the UK discount elsewhere).
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

C+689th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the internal change is strong, broad and adequately resourced, among the most complete in the universe by count (eight of twelve forces at B or better in the prior memo); what limits it is its output, not its strength. It repairs a business damaged by its own diversification and points it back at the model that once compounded; it does not change the economics of that model.

  • Breadth: chief executive, chair, both operating regions, every board committee, strategy, perimeter (three exits), new geographies (three), operating model, financing, reporting.
  • Resourcing: about £170m to £190m of capital across FY2026 to FY2027, a refinanced facility, £76m to £80m of disposal proceeds, a new team with relevant experience.
  • What the change produces: removal of about £8m of losses (Dalco about £3.7m, Foppen about £4.4m annualised from its H1 loss, Seachill marginal), new-geography contributions of £1m to £3m in FY2027 rising later, and mid-single-digit core growth: about 8 to 10 percent EPS growth from the FY2026 trough.
  • What it cannot change: customer power, no pricing power, the raw-material cycle.

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

D4722nd of 35

Judgement: there is a real, dated, company-delivered staircase, densest in the next six months; its weakness is novelty, since almost every step is already in the brokers' notes. The steps most able to surprise are a Foppen resolution (either direction), Canada running faster than modelled, and, in months 18 to 36, Walmart extending the model or a first capital return.

Next 6 months (November 2026 to April 2027):

  • NADEC launch in Saudi Arabia, Q4 2026 (well-founded; known; small).

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

F3832nd of 35

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

F3832nd of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Hilton occupies the structurally squeezed middle of a value chain whose two ends are both gaining power: concentrated grocers above and consolidating, scarcity-constrained primary supply below. Its niche (sole automated packer to the leading grocer in each country) is defensible for volume because of co-location, embedded systems and long contracts, and it is gaining new business from the strongest industry trend (grocers outsourcing own-label to scaled partners). But the same niche gives it no pricing power: the fee is set by open-book audit, the customer can re-allocate streams inside a partnership, and the advantaged competitor in mature markets is the one that owns the animal. The industry precedents say Hilton's share price outperforms when new partnerships visibly add to EPS and underperforms on inflation spikes and non-core failures; the next 18 months contain both (Canada from January 2027, Australian beef scarcity from 2027). For the model: the value chain will not let the margin per kilogram rise much above its FY2024 level of about 19p; it will let volume grow 2 to 3 percent a year plus new geographies; the fragility is concentrated at contract renewals and in Australia, and the inputs do not say when the Tesco UK and Woolworths contracts next renew, which is the largest single unknown in the structure.

3a

Value chain dynamics

6 answers

C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?D5224th of 35full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

D5222nd of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: there are no intermediaries; the route to market is direct and physically integrated with the customer, which helps continuity and cost and makes the route itself the dependency. Customer-provided finance is a further, under-appreciated part of the route that helps cash and raises finance cost.

  • Direct and co-located: plants sited at or linked to retailers' distribution hubs; the Evolve4 system integrated into customers' logistics; relocating would cause severe short-term supply disruption for the retailer.
  • Financing through the route: customer-provided supply-chain finance from Tesco, Albert Heijn and ICA funds about £150m of working capital at 0.5 to 1.5 points below bank rates; its interest rose to £9.6m in FY2025 from £4.5m; a separate £24.5m supplier finance programme began in FY2025. This makes reported debt lower and finance cost more inflation-sensitive, and the facilities sit at the customer's discretion.
  • New routes: Walmart Canada (100 percent of capacity committed), NADEC, Burger King (food service, Sweden).
  • Fragility: low in normal times; high only if a customer withdraws finance or volume at once.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

D4024th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: existing competitors hurt Hilton in its mature Western markets and help it in new ones. Integrated processors are moving up the chain and winning retailers' preference for captive supply; weak transactional packers are falling away. Hilton's position is fragile at the margin of each contract, not at its core.

  • Hurting: ABP and Kepak moving into packing; Cranswick out-executing in UK pork and poultry (volume +6.4 to 8.3 percent, margin 7.9 percent, a 10-year sole-supply deal with Sainsbury's); Irish price competition; integrated suppliers offering raw-material guarantees that a packer cannot.
  • Helping: transactional packers under financial stress and unable to automate; few independent packers can offer a multi-country partner model, which is why Walmart and NADEC chose Hilton.
  • Fragility: the Tesco Scotland episode shows a competitor with captive supply (Scottish beef processors) can take a stream inside a Hilton partnership.

C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

D4225th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: disruption is acting most on the supply end (animal supply, disease, climate) and on the customer end (retailer integration and own-label expansion), and least on Hilton's own stage, where automation strengthens it. The net leaves Hilton's position intact but squeezed between two ends that are both changing.

  • On demand (consumers): plant-based retreat and weight-loss-drug protein demand, mildly positive; trade-down, negative for mix.
  • On customers (retailers): own-label growth (positive for volume), backward integration (negative), digital and data demands (Hilton's systems are an asset).
  • On competitors: automation favours scaled packers; integration favours integrated processors; transactional packers weaken.
  • On regulators: stricter microbiological testing and traceability (cost, and the Foppen precedent).
  • On suppliers: herd contraction (UK, Ireland, Australia from 2027), disease (foot-and-mouth in Germany, swine fever in Spain), climate (Queensland heat), fish quota cuts. These raise prices and lower availability.
  • Fragility: moderate. Hilton's stage is strengthening relative to other packers but its two neighbours are gaining power over it.

Industry structure
