C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

C-6614th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Hilton is number one in its specific activity (independent, automated retail packing of red meat under long-term single-retailer partnerships across Europe and Australasia) by construction, because in each core country it is the sole or lead packer to the leading grocer. It is not a market-share leader in meat processing, where it is small against integrated primary processors.

  • Scale: 523,379 tonnes in FY2025 across 21 facilities in 19 countries; revenue £4.2bn, larger in revenue than Cranswick (£3.0bn) and similar to Greencore with Bakkavor (about £4bn pro forma), but with about £0.77bn of value added.
  • Partner positions: Tesco (UK and Ireland; Tesco about 28 to 29 percent of UK grocery), Woolworths (sole partner in Australia and New Zealand), Ahold Delhaize (Albert Heijn, Netherlands and Belgium), ICA (Sweden), Coop Danmark and Salling (Denmark), Żabka (Poland), now Walmart Canada and NADEC.
  • Against meat processors: small next to JBS, Tyson and Cargill globally and below ABP, Kepak, Vion, Danish Crown and Tönnies in primary processing in Europe; Cranswick holds about 50 percent of UK fresh pork. No market-share data for retail packing as an activity exists in the inputs.
  • For the model: rank does not confer pricing power, because the relevant rank is against the buyer, and each buyer is far larger than Hilton.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

F3427th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the markets on both sides of Hilton are highly concentrated, and that is the problem: the customer market (grocery) is an oligopoly in every country Hilton serves, and the supply market (primary processing) is consolidating. Hilton's own stage (outsourced retail packing) is concentrated by the nature of single-partner contracts, but the inputs hold no share data for it.

  • Customers: UK grocery dominated by Tesco, Sainsbury's and Marks and Spencer (with Aldi and Lidl about 18 percent combined); Tesco captures about 46 percent of the UK grocery profit pool on about 29 percent share; Nordic grocery concentration 80 percent or more; three Dutch chains about 70 percent; Australia a Woolworths and Coles duopoly (general knowledge).
  • Suppliers and competitors: Cranswick 50 percent of UK fresh pork; Moy Park and Boparan about 60 percent of UK fresh poultry; LDC 40 percent of French poultry; global beef led by JBS and Cargill; primary processing consolidating (smaller abattoirs closing).
  • Steepness: on the customer side, very steep (top three hold the majority in each country).
  • Implication: concentrated buyers neutralise any concentration Hilton has; the Wisdom Library's "Five Forces" entry applies precisely: buyer power is absolute, not relative.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

D5520th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Hilton's share is stable by contract (renewals rather than losses over five years) and unstable at the margin (5 to 7 percent of volume moved tactically, and a whole stream moved in 2026). New entrants have taken nothing visible; the share that moves goes to integrated processors and to retailers' own operations, not to newcomers.

  • Stability: renewals in 2025-26 at Tesco (extended in Q2 2026), Albert Heijn, Coop and Salling; contracts of five to fifteen years; no core contract lost in the record.
  • Margin instability: expert testimony that Tesco shifts 5 to 7 percent of packing volume to secondary suppliers to build leverage; Tesco's Scottish beef move in H1 FY26.
  • New entrants: none; entry requires a retailer partner and tens of millions in automated plant.
  • Hilton's own gains: secondary customers (Salling, Netto, Dunnes, Burger King) and new countries.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

D4523rd of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: share in protein processing consolidates in two directions: toward scale (which includes Hilton) and toward vertical integration (which excludes it). Hilton benefits from the first in winning new geographies and from the second's absence in being the partner retailers use when they do not want to own processing. In its home markets the advantaged party is the integrated processor, and Hilton is not among the most advantaged.

  • Toward scale: sub-scale processors are leaving under capital and compliance pressure; Greencore and Bakkavor merged; retailers want fewer, larger, automated suppliers. Hilton gains (Walmart chose it; NADEC chose it).
  • Toward integration: Cranswick (55 percent pig self-sufficiency, 100 percent poultry, its own genetics) holds 18.5 percent return on capital and a 7.9 percent margin; retailers favoured captive-supply suppliers after the pandemic; ABP and Kepak move into packing. Hilton owns no animals.
  • Net: Hilton is the advantaged independent partner for grocers who prefer to outsource packing without being tied to a processor's animals; it is not advantaged where grocers prefer raw-material security.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

F2435th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the competitor whose attitude sets Hilton's margin is its customer, and the customer puts price and volume first and will suffer losses to win share (Lidl in Ireland). Among packers and processors the attitude is rational and utilisation-led. When volume falls, Hilton's effective price falls with it, because the fee is per kilogram on a fixed cost base.

  • Retailers: open-book audits, daily raw-material index tracking, benchmarking clauses and volume shifts; Lidl Ireland accepts a financial hit to hold a 15 to 20 percent price gap; Aldi price-matching across 600 items in the UK; Tesco tripled Everyday Low Price items.
  • Processors: index-linked, professional pricing; utilisation prioritised over price wars (Cranswick research); Bernstein warns irrational undercutting by packers could compress processing fees.
  • Prices when volume falls: the processing fee per kilogram does not fall in the contract, but cost per kilogram rises through under-recovery, so the margin falls; that is what happened in UK seafood (volumes -6.8 percent in FY2025, Seachill loss-making) and in the West in H1 FY26.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

F3030th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: is there 'Michael O'Leary' instability, and would competitors launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement: the direction is stable (a steady squeeze) and the timing is unpredictable. Over three years the attitude has tightened: pass-through delayed in the 2025 inflation, backward integration advancing, an Irish price war, Dutch promotional withdrawal, and a Scottish sourcing switch inside a renewed partnership. A "shark attack" is possible as a tender or benchmarking exercise rather than a price war.

  • Tightening record: 2022 seafood warning; 2025 Q3 warning (23 percent one-day share fall) as beef price elasticity "snapped" and promotions were withdrawn; 2026 Scottish beef loss; Irish pressure.
  • Instability: customers' sourcing decisions arrive without warning and inside partnerships; Ahold's simultaneous chief executive changes add uncertainty.
  • Shark-attack mechanism: the contractual right to re-tender if benchmarks fail; deliberate 5 to 7 percent volume shifts. The trigger would be Hilton losing cost leadership (a risk the risks research ties to the Foods Connected sell-down; we think the retained contracts answer most of it).
  • No easing evidence in the latest period.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

F4030th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the structure supports Hilton's continuity and volume and does not support its margin. The support is real for the life of each contract and fragile at three points: renewal dates, in-year sourcing decisions, and moments of supply scarcity when retailers prefer integrated suppliers.

  • Supportive: grocers in every core country are concentrated winners outsourcing packing; switching costs (co-location, embedded systems, co-invested automation) are high; long contracts; few capable rivals for a multi-country partner.
  • Unsupportive: absolute buyer power; open-book caps; raw material owned by others; integration pull; regulators acting on food safety.
  • Fragility: Tesco at 85 to 90 percent of UK sales, Woolworths the sole APAC partner, five customers nearly all of revenue, maximum customer credit exposure £387.1m; the inputs do not give contract expiry dates for Tesco UK or Woolworths (a research gap the Consumer should close, see the requests).
  • Peer contrast: Greencore caps any customer at about 23 percent of sales; Cranswick's top three are 24, 15 and 11 percent; LDC's brands give it retail pull Hilton lacks. Hilton is the extreme of the cohort on concentration.

C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

C5812th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: substitutes are a low and falling threat to Hilton's total volume and a moderate threat to its mix. Within protein, the substitution runs from beef to pork and chicken and from steak to mince; Hilton handles the substitute products too, but at lower value per kilogram.

  • Within protein: beef price inflation of 23 to 30 percent drives volume to pork and chicken (pork mince volumes +30 percent at Cranswick's markets); Hilton reformulates mixed-protein mince and burgers, keeping volume but lowering value.
  • Plant-based: retreating; Dalco sold.
  • In-store butchery: declining (the Saudi venture is built on that substitution reversing).
  • Weight-loss drugs: shrink snack and some prepared-food volume but favour protein.
  • Fish: white fish substituted by cheaper species (hake) or by salmon and prawns (seafood volumes +6 percent in H1 FY26 on salmon and prawns).

Strong business SRCA and BTE; management; financial profile

F4227th of 36

Memo topic 3, Value chain, structure, model, financials, management: the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

D4526th of 358 answers
Judgement45C-4158C-4252C-4345C-4455C-4512C-4664C-4733
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

D4526th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Hilton's competitive advantage is real, durable and of the wrong kind for earnings growth: it secures volume and relationships and cannot secure price. The customer moat (co-location, embedded systems, co-invested automation, long contracts) is strong and stable and is why no core contract has been lost; the execution moat (automation, multi-country partner record) is modest and is what wins new countries; the culture moat narrowed and is being rebuilt. Because the model is built to share scale economies with the customer, gains in efficiency return to the customer at each reset, so the margin per kilogram reverts toward a contractual norm (about 18p to 19p) rather than compounding; the evidence is a decade-long drift down in the margin and a return on capital edging below 20 percent. The one growing advantage is reputational and optional: Walmart and NADEC chose Hilton because it has done this before, and a Walmart roll-out beyond Ontario would be the only route back to compounder-like growth. For the model: hold profit per kilogram at 18p to 19p in the existing estate, grow it only through mix (prepared food, bacon) and loss removal, and treat new-geography wins as the growth engine, with Canada's returns ramping slowly because Hilton started at a 10 to 15 percent cost disadvantage and bore the build-cost overrun.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

C-5816th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Hilton holds one strong moat (customer embedding through co-location, systems and long contracts), one moderate one (an automation and multi-country execution lead), and one growing intangible (a proven record as the independent partner a grocer can outsource meat packing to in a new country). All protect volume and relationships; none protects price. The customer moat is stable, the execution moat is widening slowly, the culture moat has narrowed.

  • Customer-related, strong, stable: co-location with retailer hubs; co-invested automated sites; the Evolve4 system integrated with customer logistics; five-to-fifteen-year contracts; joint-venture equity in some markets; renewals secured. Bounded by benchmarking clauses and the right to re-allocate.
  • Production-related, moderate, slowly widening: end-of-line robotics with Agito (£21m invested in UK plants), machine-learning line control, water-jet cutting, exclusive OEM rights to next-generation machinery through the Greenchain technology arm; about 10 percent labour reduction per site. The risks research argues the Foods Connected sell-down narrows it; the retained contracts and 26.3 percent stake mostly answer that.
  • Scale-based, moderate: 523,000 tonnes and 21 plants give unit-cost leadership over transactional packers and the credibility and balance sheet to build an £80m plant for Walmart; no scale advantage against the customer.
  • Regulatory, weak: compliance capability (traceability, certification) valued by retailers, not a barrier.
  • Culture and knowledge, narrowed: expert testimony that founders' retail relationship skills were not replicated and mid-level talent was depleted; now being rebuilt by the new team.
  • The growing intangible: Walmart and NADEC chose Hilton because it has done this in multiple countries; Walmart wants to replicate the model. This is the asset that could re-open the compounder phase.
C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

D5216th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Hilton is large in tonnage and revenue and small in value added and against its customers. Its scale confers cost leadership over other packers and credibility with multinational grocers; it confers no bargaining power over customers or primary suppliers.

  • Relative to peers: revenue £4.2bn against Cranswick £3.0bn (7.9 percent margin), Greencore and Bakkavor about £4bn pro forma (5.6 percent), LDC €7.3bn (5.9 percent); but Hilton's value added is about £0.77bn and its operating profit about £95m against Cranswick's £237m.
  • Relative to customers: Tesco, Woolworths, Ahold and Walmart are each many times larger; the Wisdom Library's "buyer power is absolute" applies.
  • Relative to suppliers: small against JBS, Cargill, ABP, Vion.
  • What it confers: throughput that minimises unit packing cost; a group procurement centre; the ability to roll technology across 21 sites; the financial capacity to fund dedicated plants; and the multi-country reference record that wins new partners.
C-43How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?F4532nd of 35full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

D5520th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: for the retailer, Hilton's utility is high and demonstrably not inferior on the packing service (reliability, automation, transparency, cost); it is inferior on raw-material security against integrated suppliers, which is what retailers have valued more since the pandemic. Inferiority is disproved on service and not disproved on supply.

  • Evidence of high utility: service levels about 98.6 percent; Tesco's UK Quality Supplier of the Year; Tesco's supplier satisfaction 89 percent; retailers praise automation and packaging innovation (Tesco co-developed mince packaging); the open-book model lets the retailer see and capture efficiency; renewals across four customers.
  • Evidence of inferiority: Tesco Scotland moved to Scottish-sourced beef; integrated competitors offer captive supply; expert testimony that the declining UK herd makes retailers nervous about packers without raw-material control.
  • For consumers: invisible (own-label), so no brand utility.
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

F1234th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility?

Judgement: none. The model is cost-plus and open-book by design; pricing is set by audit and benchmarking; the evidence of application is nil. Hilton's equivalent of pricing power is the lagged recovery of cost inflation at annual resets, which restores the margin per kilogram rather than raising it.

  • Mechanism: over 90 percent of raw-material cost passed through; processing costs fixed annually; retailers track indices daily and resist real-time pass-through; benchmarking clauses allow re-tender.
  • Record: margins compressed in each inflation spike (FY2022, FY2025) and recovered afterwards (FY2023, FY2024); never expanded above about 2.6 percent in recent years (3.0 percent pre-pandemic average quoted in the research).
  • Peers: LDC's brands (Le Gaulois, Maître CoQ, Loué) and Cloetta's Superbrands carry real pricing; Cranswick prices through genetics-led premium products; Hilton has no brand and no proprietary product.
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

B647th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: entirely and structurally. The open-book cost-plus contract is a mechanism for sharing scale economies with the customer; adding secondary customers to fill plants lowers unit cost for the anchor too. This is the source of Hilton's durability (customers have no reason to leave) and the reason its margin cannot expand (the savings go to the customer at each reset).

  • Stated: "mutual supply efficiencies through open-book cost-plus structures"; partnership language throughout; Woolworths co-created value promotions; Tesco co-developed packaging.
  • De facto: efficiency gains from automation are visible to the retailer in the audit and are partly passed on at renewal; in APAC the contract passes through interest on capital employed (the retailer funds Hilton's capital cost).
  • Implication for the model (Wisdom Library, "Scale Economies Shared"): high retention and long contract life; a balancing loop on margin (see the requests for set 2); returns on capital protected rather than margins.
C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

F3328th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: weakly. Each new co-located plant locks in another retailer and each automation roll-out compounds know-how, but the financial outputs have not compounded: the margin and profit per kilogram are lower than five years ago and the return on capital is drifting down. Hilton's improvement depends on management actions (exits, new contracts), not on mechanics.

  • Mechanical positives: technology stack rolled site by site; secondary customers filling capacity; each new geography adds a reference for the next.
  • Evidence against: FactSet adjusted EBIT margin 2.7 percent (FY2019) to 1.4 percent (FY2025); company adjusted operating margin 2.6 percent (FY2024) to 2.0 percent (H1 FY26); profit per kilogram about 19.0p (FY2024) to 17.7p (H1 FY26); return on capital employed 21.7 percent to 20.1 percent and below 20 percent in FY2026; revenue CAGR 3.07 percent over three years versus Cranswick's decade of about 10.5 percent.
  • Why: the customer captures efficiency at resets; the customer's power does not fall as Hilton grows.

Group judgement

Company - Management

D5025th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The management is a competent, decisive repair team with modest growth ambition, running a business whose organisation and controls beneath it are weaker than its leadership. In ten months the chief executive, a portfolio-and-discipline operator by record, has done what a repair requires: exits at fair prices, a ring-fence, a capital framework with hurdles, regional accountability, a refinancing, honest communication (labelling his own upgrade technical) and a personal share purchase at the low. The case against greatness is that ambition is deliberately modest (and incentive hurdles were cut to 3 to 8 percent EPS growth), the mid-term delivery record is of misses in inflation spikes, the auditor found control deficiencies, executives own almost nothing, and the mid-level bench and commercial depth are reported thin. For the Consumer this means: (1) trust management's stated direction and its labelling of results; (2) do not credit growth above the incentive plan's 8 percent stretch; (3) lean FY2027 forecasts down if Australian beef or Canada's start-up stress the business, because this organisation has not outperformed in tougher years; (4) expect conservative-to-honest guidance from this team, a change from the prior one.

C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

D5820th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities: widening the moat, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a range of environments?

Judgement: the strategy is smart and right, and not bold. It points capital at the one thing Hilton demonstrably does well (contracted, automated packing for a lead grocer), exits what it cannot do, and grows through partner-carried geographies. It is robust across environments (the cost-plus model survives inflation; the weakness, volume, is where the strategy adds) but it does not widen the moat on price, which no strategy available to a packer can.

  • Right choices: exit units without synergies; ring-fence seafood; capital-light where possible (Saudi Arabia); acquisitions only above the cost of capital; automation for cost; mix toward fresh prepared food; regional accountability.
  • Robust across environments: inflation (pass-through), deflation (cash release and percentage-margin optics), weak consumers (value tiers and mixed proteins), strong partners (new countries).
  • Not bold: no integration move, no capital return programme, no large M&A the seafood decision deferred to an "options" review.
  • Open question: whether Canada's terms (Hilton bearing build inflation, a cost disadvantage at start) show a strategy too eager to win the partner.

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

D4521st of 35

Pay has fallen with performance and the chief executive bought shares, but executives own almost nothing, base pay is high, incentive hurdles were lowered for the new awards, and governance scaffolding is being added after failures rather than ahead of them.

  • Prudence of governance: reactive rather than prudent. The remedies (independent Chair, new Audit chair, IT risk function, Provision 29 testing plan for late 2026) are in train.

Financial profile

Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

F3231st of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Hilton's economic profile is weak on margin, adequate on returns, volatile on cash and geared on EPS, and the next 18 months sit at its weakest point. A 2 percent operating margin on pass-through revenue is the wrong lens; the right one is a £0.77bn value-added business earning about 12 percent on value added and about 18p per kilogram, contractually anchored by open-book resets, which keeps returns on capital near 20 percent (helped by customer-funded working capital) but stops margins compounding. Below operating profit, a finance charge of about £26m (bank, lease and customer-finance interest) and a tax rate near 30 percent take operating profit growth of about 6 percent to EPS growth of about 8 percent, a lower-quality component. Cash is the weak line: about £100m of capital expenditure in FY2026 leaves free cash flow near zero against £31.5m of dividends, and consensus FY2027 to FY2028 capital expenditure looks low given Poland's upsizing and Canada's bacon line. The earnings base carries recurring "exceptional" costs and accounting flags. The positive is concrete, bounded scope to improve: about £8m of losses to remove and a Canadian plant that, if it reaches its hurdle, adds more than consensus carries by FY2029. For the model: build operating profit from tonnes and profit per kilogram, carry finance cost at £26m to £29m, tax at 29 to 30 percent, recurring restructuring cash of £5m to £10m, and capital expenditure about £10m to £20m a year above consensus in FY2027 to FY2028.

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

D2025th of 35

IFRS 18 from FY2027 will require formal reconciliation of adjusted measures.
  • Tax leakage: adjusted effective rate 24.9 percent (FY2024), 29.8 percent (FY2025, capital-allowance true-ups), 27.7 percent (H1 FY26); a return to 27 percent would add about 4 percent to EPS once; consensus implies about 29 to 30 percent.
  • Finance leakage: customer-provided finance interest £9.6m (FY2025) from £4.5m, rising with meat prices and rates.

C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

F3829th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: balance-sheet leverage is comfortable on the company's covenant measure and higher than it looks on a full measure; the gearing of EPS to the finance charge is material. Net finance cost absorbs about 27 percent of operating profit (interest cover about 3.7x), so every 1 percent change in operating profit moves profit before tax by about 1.4 percent, and every £2m of finance cost moves EPS by about 3 percent.

  • Measures: net bank debt £126.7m (0.9x, FY2025) and £194.4m (1.4x rolling, H1 FY26), target 1.0x to 2.0x; including £198.1m of leases, total net debt £324.8m (FY2025); FactSet net debt to its reported EBITDA 3.32x at H1 FY26; plus about £150m of customer-provided finance outside debt. £450m revolving credit facility to 2031 with ample covenant headroom.
  • Finance charge: about £26m in FY2025 (adjusted operating profit £95.1m less continuing adjusted profit before tax £69.0m) and £13.0m in H1 FY26; consensus £25m to £27m (FY2026 to FY2028). Approximate composition (our estimate): bank interest £9m to £12m, lease interest £8m to £10m, customer-provided finance about £9.6m.
  • Rate sensitivity: a 50 basis-point rise is about £2.1m (company); with Bank Rate rising to about 4.25 percent by mid-2027, the bank and customer-finance components rise, not fall.
  • EPS amplification: the gearing is part of consensus EPS growth: with finance cost flat at about £26m, operating profit growth of 6 percent becomes profit-before-tax growth of about 8 percent; the prior memo attributed about 2 points of its 8 percent EPS growth to this gearing, a lower-quality component.
  • Comfort: high for solvency; moderate for EPS, because the charge is inflation- and rate-linked.

Pricing power and operating leverage (and others)

Memo topic 5, Pricing power, leverage, cost control, feedback loops: the Rater's score

5a

Pricing power

C-45To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?F1234th of 35full answer under 4a Business quality
P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

F2434th of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: price will contribute a great deal to revenue in FY2026 and very little thereafter, and nothing to margin in any year except through the lagged recovery of conversion-cost inflation, which is worth about £1m to £2m of operating profit in FY2027 and which the open-book reset then caps. Price is a revenue line, not a profit driver: raw-material pass-through added about 7 percentage points to FY2026 revenue growth (with currency about 3 points and volume about 2 points), and we carry price and mix at only +0.5 percent in FY2027 and about +1 percent a year from FY2028, as beef prices plateau at a high level. In percentage terms, price is margin-dilutive whenever it is positive. The one place where price acts on Hilton's profit is the processing fee itself, and over the next four years the forces on that fee (retailer price investment at Tesco, Woolworths and Walmart; benchmarking at renewal; the Australian cattle squeeze) point down more than up. We therefore carry no real fee increase and treat any renewal of Tesco UK or Woolworths on worse terms as the main downside to profit per kilogram.

How price enters each line (the mechanics, refined by the AlphaSense pricing report):

  • Raw material (over 90 percent passed through): quickly, through indices; a former Hilton director (March 2023 expert call) puts the lag before retailers accept raw-material increases at ten to twelve weeks. This moves revenue, not profit, except that the lag compresses profit in a sharp spike (FY2022, FY2025).
  • Conversion costs (labour, energy, packaging, overheads): reset annually in the UK open-book model ("processing costs fixed annually", former employees), so in-year inflation is absorbed for up to a year. This is the one price mechanism that moves profit, and it moves it with a lag: margins compressed in the spike year and recovered the year after (company adjusted operating margin 2.4 percent in FY2023 to 2.6 percent in FY2024).
  • Australia and New Zealand (fee per kilogram): cash profit per kilogram is fixed by the fee; a rise in beef prices lowers the percentage margin "mathematically" (Deutsche Bank, 3 September 2026) but not the cash. East margins held at about 2.45 percent through the 2023 to 2024 deflation.
  • Labour, the cost that matters next: the evidence is better than the first set suggested. Deutsche Bank (30 October 2024) found Hilton's residual exposure to the 2025 National Insurance rise to be only £1.8m, the lowest among UK mid-cap food makers, because "pass-through agreements cover UK tax inflation". The first set (C-04, C-17) treated labour as not clearly passed through within the year. We read it as passed through with a lag of a few months to a year, which makes the April 2027 National Living Wage rise a timing drag in H1 FY2027 (we carry about £1m) rather than a permanent leak.

What price contributes to revenue, year by year (our build):

  • FY2026: +9.6 percent reported on FactSet's FY2025 base of £4,214.6m (about +12.5 percent on today's perimeter). H1 +15.3 percent (11.5 percent at constant currency) and H2 about +10 percent (beef still inflating, the Australian dollar still a tailwind, Dalco out). Of the like-for-like growth, about 2 points are volume, about 3 points currency and about 7 points price and mix.
  • FY2027: +3.7 percent. Volume about +1.8 percent in the existing estate, Canada about +1.4 percent (Shore Capital's £65m), price and mix about +0.5 percent (European beef plateauing after a 50 percent rise; Australian cattle prices rising as availability falls; mix still trading down), currency held flat.
  • FY2028 to FY2030: +4.2, +4.0 and +3.5 percent, of which price and mix about +1 percent a year.
  • The cone on price: beef could fall (European pork oversupply, demand elasticity) or rise further (global beef production -2 percent in 2027 and -3 percent in 2028, Morgan Stanley). A 10 percent move either way moves revenue by about £350m to £400m and the EBITDA margin by about 0.3 percentage points the other way, with little effect on cash profit. Revenue is the least predictable line in the model and the least important.

What price contributes to margin:

  • In percentage terms: negative while prices rise, positive if they fall. Our EBITDA margin rises from 3.63 percent to 3.91 percent over FY2026 to FY2030 because cash profit grows faster than revenue, not because of price.
  • In cash terms: about +£1m to +£2m in FY2027 from the lagged recovery of FY2025 to FY2026 conversion-cost inflation, which sits inside our existing-estate growth of 3.0 percent. We do not carry it again in later years, because the reset that recovers it also re-benchmarks the fee.
  • No real fee increases, and the evidence of give-back. Retailers "actively resist real-time price pass-throughs" (former Hilton seafood procurement head, November 2025); a former ICA marketing head said Hilton "was not competitive on price" against Danish Crown (March 2023); Tesco shifts 5 to 7 percent of packing volume tactically to keep leverage (December 2022 expert call). Hilton has no brand and no proprietary product.

The forces on the processing fee over the next four years (all point down or sideways):

  • Retailer price investment: Tesco tripled Everyday Low Price items and is price-matching; Walmart is running "massive price rollbacks" (Morgan Stanley, 22 May 2026); Woolworths cut prices on hundreds of items under political pressure; Lidl Ireland accepted "a significant financial hit" to hold a 15 to 20 percent gap; SuperValu cut prices on 500 lines (January 2026). Each gives the retailer a reason to press the processing fee at the next reset.
  • Integrated competitors: Tesco moved Scottish beef to Foyle and Kepak and pays over £92m a year into its Sustainable Beef Group through them (Tesco, 31 July 2026); the Competition and Markets Authority opened a review of ABP's stake in Dovecote Park (September to October 2026). Integrated processors "often accept lower margins" to win retail accounts (former Kepak executive, October 2026).
  • Renewal timing, the largest unknown: the company does not publish expiry dates for Tesco UK or Woolworths. The Tesco partnership was extended in 2026 (Deutsche Bank, 19 May 2026; dates not given). We carry no renewal loss in the base and treat a renewal on materially worse terms (for example a 0.5p to 1p per kilogram cut on 40 percent of volume, about £1m to £2m) as a downside the Consumer should test in the next set.
  • Australia from 2027: falling cattle availability raises raw-material prices (revenue up, percentage margin down) and may lower Woolworths' volumes; the fee per kilogram protects the cash per kilogram but not the kilograms. We carry a probability-weighted £1.5m drag on East operating profit in FY2028 through volume, not price.
  • Walmart Canada: the fee is set for a ten-year term; Hilton bears the start-up cost, Walmart the retail price. The expert evidence that North American sourcing is "10 to 15 percent higher per pound initially" (Walmart executive, December 2025) is a pressure on Walmart's margin that could, at the first review, become a pressure on Hilton's fee.

Peer contrast (why Hilton is at the slow end): Cranswick's cost-of-production formulas reset quarterly with no lag on feed and utilities and keep a cash margin; Bell passes raw material one-to-one "with a slight delay" and can move pork and poultry prices within one or two days; Greencore recovers about 75 percent of inflation automatically and negotiates the rest; LDC has statutory EGalim indexation; Almarai pushed a 17 percent Saudi fresh dairy price rise through with limited elasticity in 2026 (Morgan Stanley, 13 October 2026), which is relevant only as context for the NADEC venture's environment. Hilton's annual conversion-cost reset is the slowest mechanism in the cohort and carries no brand-led pricing.

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7535P-0138
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

F3533rd of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: operating leverage is material against volume and almost nil against revenue. A 1 percent change in volume moves operating profit by roughly £3m to £5m (3 to 5 percent) and EPS by about 4 to 7 percent; a 1 percent change in revenue from raw-material prices moves profit hardly at all. The leverage is asymmetric: downside volume falls fully onto a fixed cost base, while upside efficiency is partly handed back to the customer at the next open-book reset.

  • The cost structure (FY2025, our derivation): revenue £4,214.6m; raw proteins and consumables £3,443.5m (passed through); value added about £771m; conversion and overhead costs about £676m (labour £295.9m, depreciation about £77m including leases, energy, logistics, site costs); adjusted operating profit £95.1m (about 12 percent of value added).
  • Volume sensitivity: about £1.47 of value added per kilogram; if 40 to 60 percent of conversion cost is fixed in the short run, 1 percent of volume (about 5,200 tonnes) moves operating profit by about £3m to £5m; that is about 2.5p to 4p of EPS at a 29.5 percent tax rate, 4 to 7 percent.
  • Profit-per-kilogram sensitivity: 1p a kilogram on about 530,000 tonnes is £5.3m of operating profit, about 4p or 8 percent of EPS.
  • The asymmetry, evidenced: Seachill (-6.8 percent volume in FY2025, turned loss-making); West in H1 FY26 (Scottish beef lost, core meat profit -20.5 percent on +0.6 percent volume and Irish pressure); upside recoveries have been gradual.
  • Range of outcomes for FY2027 (our judgement): volume from -1 to +4 percent and profit per kilogram from 17p to 19.5p give operating profit of about £88m to £108m around a consensus £99.6m, and EPS of about 48p to 63p around 57.6p: a cone of about plus or minus 13 to 17 percent.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

F3832nd of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: operating leverage will shape the next four years in three distinct ways, and only one of them is favourable and retained. (1) In the existing estate, leverage operates on volume, not on revenue, and is asymmetric in time: volume losses hit profit in the year they occur, while volume gains and efficiency are partly handed back at the next open-book reset; over a full cycle this holds profit per kilogram near a contractual norm of 18.5p to 19.2p rather than compounding it. (2) In the new plants (Canada from January 2027, Polish fresh prepared food from about 2028), leverage is strongly negative first and strongly positive later, because a fixed cost base arrives before the volume; this is the one place in the model where operating leverage is both large and retained during the contract term, and it drives most of the improvement in operating profit from FY2028. (3) In the percentage margin, leverage is an optical illusion: raw-material prices move the revenue denominator, so the EBITDA margin input must be derived from cash profit, never set as a driver. Against FY2019 to FY2025, when operating leverage worked against the company (FactSet adjusted EBIT margin 2.7 percent to 1.4 percent; company adjusted margin 2.6 percent in FY2024 to 2.0 percent in H1 FY26), the forecast has leverage turning mildly positive from H2 FY2027, worth about 0.3 percentage points of EBITDA margin by FY2030, of which about two-thirds is new-plant absorption and loss removal and one-third is the existing estate.

The cost structure that creates the leverage (FY2025, our derivation from the first set):

  • Revenue £4,214.6m; raw proteins and consumables £3,443.5m (about 82 percent, passed through); value added about £771m (about £1.47 per kilogram on 523,379 tonnes); conversion and overhead costs about £676m (labour £295.9m, depreciation about £72m on the continuing IFRS 16 basis, energy, packaging, logistics, site and central costs); adjusted operating profit £95.1m (about 18.2p per kilogram, 12 percent of value added).
  • At plant level the fixed portion in the short run is depreciation, energy base load, the minimum crewing of automated lines (the AlphaSense operating-leverage report cites Hilton's March 2026 strategic update that lines need "a minimum staffing complement to operate safely") and site overheads; we take 40 to 60 percent of conversion cost as fixed within a year.
  • Sensitivity: 1 percent of group volume (about 5,300 tonnes) moves operating profit by about £3.5m to £5m (3.7 to 5.2 percent of FY2026 operating profit) and EPS by about 2.8p to 4.0p (5 to 7 percent) in the year it occurs. 1p per kilogram of profit is about £5.3m, about 4.2p or 8 percent of EPS.

How it works in the existing estate, and why it does not compound:

  • Downside, evidenced and immediate: UK and Ireland adjusted operating margin fell from 3.5 to 2.5 percent in FY2025 when Seachill volumes fell 6.8 percent; UK and Ireland revenue rose 16 percent to £1.5bn while segment operating profit fell 17 percent to £37.5m (Shore Capital, 31 March 2026). West core meat adjusted operating profit fell 20.5 percent in H1 FY26 on volume growth of only 0.6 percent, after Tesco moved its Scottish beef stream to Foyle and Kepak and Irish price competition intensified. Profit per kilogram fell from 18.6p to 17.7p in H1 FY26.
  • Upside, evidenced but shared: East adjusted operating profit rose 17.7 percent at constant currency on 3.2 percent volume growth in H1 FY26 (margin 2.4 to 2.45 percent). The incremental margin on that volume was high, which is the leverage working. But the open-book audit and benchmarking clauses mean the retailer sees the lower unit cost; Deutsche Bank (3 September 2026) states that retailers "claw back yield and labour savings" at resets, and Deutsche Bank's long-run model holds the group margin flat at about 2.1 percent for a decade (29 January 2026).
  • The consequence for the model: in the existing estate, operating profit should grow at about volume growth (2 percent) plus a small nominal creep in profit per kilogram (about 0.5 to 1 percent a year, because the "plus" in cost-plus is earned on a nominal cost base that inflates), less the share of efficiency returned at resets. We carry existing-estate operating profit growth of 3.0 percent (FY2027), 2.5 percent (FY2028, the Australian cattle squeeze), 3.0 percent (FY2029) and 3.0 percent (FY2030). That takes existing-estate profit per kilogram from 18.5p (FY2026) to 19.2p (FY2030), roughly back to FY2024's 19.0p in nominal terms and a continued decline in real terms.
  • We differ slightly from the first set here. The first set treated about 19p as a ceiling (Industry and Value Chain group judgement). We think a nominal ceiling is too strict: under cost-plus the processing margin is earned on conversion costs that inflate at 3 to 5 percent a year (UK National Living Wage, energy), so nominal profit per kilogram should drift up even as real profit per kilogram drifts down. We allow about 1 percent a year, below conversion-cost inflation, which is consistent with the decade-long real decline.

How it works in the new plants (the favourable and retained leverage):

  • Canada (Brantford, Ontario, for Walmart Canada): a dedicated plant whose fixed costs (depreciation of about £4m a year on £80.7m spent, plus fixed labour and site overheads that we estimate at £8m to £10m) start in January 2027 while volume ramps "over the first half of 2027" (interim report, 3 September 2026), with bacon lines added later in 2027. The precedents show the cost of the ramp: Maple Leaf Foods' London, Ontario poultry plant carried start-up costs that rose to C$20.6m in 2024 before trailing off, with "duplicate overheads and yield inefficiencies" (Maple Leaf, 11 March 2025); Scandi Standard warned in 2025 that start-up costs and under-utilisation at Oosterwolde would dilute margins through a multi-year ramp. Shore Capital models £65m of sales and £1.5m of operating profit in FY2027, rising to over £5m by year three; management guides "a small low-single-digit profit contribution" in 2027 and "full profit benefits in 2029".
  • Our path, probability-weighted (with a 20 to 30 percent chance of a material slip or overrun, first set C-81): Canada operating profit £0.5m (FY2027, with a loss in H1 and a profit in H2), £2.5m (FY2028), £5.0m (FY2029) and £7.5m (FY2030) on sales of about £65m, £110m, £135m and £150m. The incremental margin on Canada's sales growth is about 8 percent, four times the group's, which is the operating leverage of a dedicated plant filling up under a contract whose price terms are fixed for the term.
  • The unresolved number, and the largest single swing in the model: to earn the 20 percent pre-tax hurdle management still claims for Canada on about £95m of capital (£80.7m plus bacon), the plant needs £16m to £19m of operating profit, more than twice our FY2030 figure and three times Shore Capital's year-three figure. Either Shore Capital and we are too low, or the hurdle is measured on Hilton's capital net of customer funding (Shore Capital, 4 September 2024, states that "a significant portion of lease liabilities is directly covered by the retail customer" in these agreements, which contradicts the expert testimony the first set relied on that Hilton bore the build inflation). We carry the lower path because the evidence for it is a published broker model and a greenfield precedent, while the evidence for the higher path is management's assertion; we flag it as upside of £5m to £10m of operating profit by FY2030 if the contract passes capital cost through.
  • Poland: fresh prepared food volumes grew 26 percent in H1 FY26; up to £30m of capacity (possibly larger) is to be decided around the end of 2026 and come on stream in about 2028. We carry £0.8m (FY2028), £2.0m (FY2029) and £3.0m (FY2030) of incremental operating profit, with an initial start-up drag inside the FY2028 figure.

The percentage margin, and why it must be derived, not driven:

  • A 10 percent rise in raw-material prices raises revenue by about 8 percent and lowers the EBITDA margin by about 0.27 percentage points with no change in cash profit; a fall does the opposite. This is why H1 FY26 revenue grew 15.3 percent while the adjusted operating margin fell 40 basis points to 2.0 percent, and why Cranswick's margin rose when pig prices fell 15 percent.
  • For the model: our EBITDA margin input (3.63 percent FY2026 rising to 3.91 percent FY2030) is the output of the cash profit build divided by a revenue line that assumes beef prices plateau (price and mix +0.5 percent in FY2027 and about +1 percent a year thereafter). If beef prices fall 10 percent in FY2028, revenue falls about £400m, the EBITDA margin rises to about 4.1 percent and profit does not change; the Consumer should not read that as a margin beat.

Against the last four to six years:

  • FY2019 to FY2025 revenue grew from £1,815m to £4,215m (Australia's step-up, acquisitions and inflation), while FactSet's adjusted EBIT margin fell from 2.7 to 1.4 percent and FactSet operating EBITDA margin from 5.2 to 3.3 percent. The company's own adjusted operating profit was £71.1m (FY2022), £95.0m (FY2023), £104.7m (FY2024) and £99.3m (FY2025 including discontinued units). Operating leverage worked against the company in each inflation spike (FY2022, FY2025) and for it in stable years (FY2023, FY2024: margin 2.4 to 2.6 percent).
  • The forecast differs from that history in one structural way: the growth now comes from dedicated new plants with contracted volume rather than from acquisitions of businesses (seafood, vegan, food service) that did not share the core's economics. That makes the leverage in FY2028 to FY2030 more favourable than in any year since FY2019, but it is concentrated in one plant (Canada) and one market (Poland).

The cone that operating leverage creates (next 18 months):

  • FY2027: volume growth of -1 to +4 percent and profit per kilogram of 17.3p to 19.0p give operating profit of about £94m to £108m around our £101.0m and EPS of about 50p to 62p around our 56.6p. The downside is reached by volume (an Australian supply shortfall or a further West re-allocation) and arrives within the year; the upside is reached by Foppen's re-approval and Canada ramping faster, and arrives with a lag.
  • The skew is downward in the next 18 months (a volume loss falls fully on fixed cost; an efficiency gain is shared) and becomes balanced to slightly upward in FY2029 to FY2030 (Canada's absorption).
5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7638P-0255
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?F3829th of 35full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

C5513th of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: financial leverage will add nothing to EPS growth in FY2027 and about 2 to 3 points a year from FY2028, and the balance sheet will pass its leverage peak in FY2027 and fall below management's own 1.0x floor by FY2030, which creates a capital-return or second-project decision that is not in consensus. The first set described the finance charge as gearing that turns 6 percent operating profit growth into 8 percent profit before tax growth (C-76). That only holds if the charge is flat. It is not flat in FY2027: net bank debt does not fall (free cash flow after leases and growth capital is about £20m against a £32m dividend), Bank Rate rises to about 4.25 percent by mid-2027, and the charge rises about £1.5m, so in FY2027 the gearing is neutralised (profit before tax +5.8 percent, the same as operating profit; EPS +4.8 percent after a higher tax rate and dilution). The gearing turns favourable from FY2028, when the charge stops rising, and strongest in FY2029, when free cash flow covers the dividend and net debt falls about £25m to £30m a year.

The composition of the charge (H1 FY26 interim results, via the AlphaSense EPS-bridge report):

  • Gross finance cost of £16.8m for the half: bank interest £8.2m, lease interest £3.8m, customer-provided supply-chain finance £4.2m, other about £0.6m. Net of finance income and other items the adjusted charge was £13.0m (adjusted operating profit £45.8m less adjusted profit before tax £32.8m). Annualised: bank about £16m gross on £326m of gross borrowings, lease about £7.6m on about £198m of lease liabilities (an implied rate of about 3.8 percent), supply-chain finance about £8.4m, less interest income on about £130m of cash.
  • Management guides an average borrowing rate of about 5.5 percent for 2026; a 50 basis-point rise in rates adds about £2.1m (annual report), about 1.65p or 2.9 percent of FY2027 EPS.
  • We differ from the first set on the supply-chain finance channel. The first set (C-05, C-86) treated interest on customer-provided finance as a cost that rises with meat prices (£9.6m in FY2025 from £4.5m). The H1 FY26 figure was £4.2m against £4.4m in H1 FY25: the channel has plateaued at a high level despite further beef inflation. We carry it at about £8.5m a year, flat, rising only if beef prices make a further leg up or the customers' reference rates rise.

Our charge, and why:

  • £26.5m (FY2026: H1 £13.0m, H2 £13.5m on higher average debt after the H2 frozen-meat purchase and £55m of H2 capital expenditure), £28.0m (FY2027: average net bank debt about £26m higher than FY2026, Bank Rate 4.00 percent from 5 November 2026 and about 4.25 percent by mid-2027), £28.0m (FY2028: debt starts to fall, rates stable), £27.5m (FY2029) and £27.0m (FY2030) as net bank debt falls toward £130m.
  • Consensus is £25.2m, £25.6m and £27.4m for FY2026 to FY2028 (implied). Our FY2027 charge is £2.4m above consensus, about 1.9p of EPS; this is one of the two reasons our FY2027 EPS sits below consensus (the other is Canada).

What it does to EPS:

  • The charge absorbs 28 percent of operating profit in FY2026 and FY2027, 26 percent in FY2028 and 22 percent by FY2030. Interest cover (operating profit over net finance cost) rises from 3.6x to 4.6x.
  • EPS effect of the rise in the charge, relative to holding it at FY2026's £26.5m: about -1.2p in FY2027 and FY2028, -0.8p in FY2029 and -0.4p in FY2030.
  • The gearing, year by year: in FY2027 it adds nothing (operating profit and profit before tax both +5.8 percent, because the charge rises £1.5m); in FY2028 to FY2030 it adds about 2 to 3 points a year (operating profit +6.9, +7.9 and +6.4 percent; profit before tax +9.6, +11.3 and +8.9 percent), because the charge stops rising and then falls. Over FY2026 to FY2030 as a whole, operating profit grows 6.7 percent a year and profit before tax 8.9 percent a year. The first set's estimate that about 2 points of 8 percent EPS growth come from gearing (C-76, from the September memo) is therefore right for the window as a whole but wrong for FY2027, the year the next set must test most closely. It is a lower-quality component, and it reverses if rates or debt rise instead of fall.
  • The rate sensitivity is the largest single below-the-line swing: Bank Rate 50 basis points above our path from mid-2027 costs about £2.1m (2.9 percent of EPS); 50 basis points below adds the same. A full withdrawal of the £150m of customer-provided finance (very low probability) would add about £1.5m to £2.5m of interest (the 0.5 to 1.5 point spread) and lift net bank debt to about 2.5x, above the target range.

What it does to the balance sheet:

  • Net bank debt (excluding leases): £126.7m (FY2025), about £192m (FY2026), about £204m (FY2027, the peak), about £188m (FY2028), about £163m (FY2029) and about £133m (FY2030). On a covenant-style EBITDA (our IFRS 16 EBITDA less total lease payments), leverage is 1.4x in FY2026 and FY2027, 1.2x in FY2028, 1.0x in FY2029 and 0.8x in FY2030. The first set's year-end FY2026 estimate (£170m to £200m) is consistent with ours.
  • Including leases (about £200m, rising slowly), total net debt is about £390m to £400m through FY2028 (FactSet showed £389m at June 2026, 3.3x its own reported EBITDA). The leases are the larger part of the "debt" in the share price and do not fall.
  • Off balance sheet: about £150m of customer-provided supply-chain finance and the £24.5m supplier finance programme. These make reported net bank debt about £150m lower than an economic measure would; the Consumer should count them when comparing leverage with Cranswick (net debt excluding leases £65m, 0.2x) or Greencore (2.3x after Bakkavor).
  • Covenants: net bank debt to EBITDA of 3.0x and interest cover of 3.5x on the £450m revolving credit facility to 2031 (£141.7m undrawn at June 2026). Headroom is ample on our path; even a £20m operating profit shortfall in FY2027 leaves leverage below 1.7x.
  • Equity: retained earnings grow by net income less dividends, about £17m (FY2026, adjusted; statutory lower because of adjusting items and the Dalco impairment), £19m (FY2027), £24m (FY2028), rising thereafter. Return on equity stays about 13 to 14 percent.

The decision the balance sheet will force (not in consensus):

  • Management's target range is 1.0x to 2.0x, with a stated intention to return surplus cash if high-return projects do not appear (Matt Osborne, first set C-19). On our path leverage reaches 1.0x in FY2029 and 0.8x in FY2030. Staying at 1.0x in FY2030 implies about £30m to £35m of capacity for a buyback or special dividend in FY2030, rising if growth continues, or a new project of that size (a second Walmart site, a larger Poland, a Saudi expansion).
  • We do not put a buyback in the base case, because nothing has been announced and the history (no buyback since flotation in 2007) and the incentive to fund a second North American plant both argue against assuming one. But a buyback of £30m at 12x earnings in FY2030 would add about 4 percent to FY2031 EPS; the Consumer should treat it as an option that the balance sheet creates and the market does not price.

What would change the picture: a Poland project above £30m (management says "bigger than originally envisaged"), a second Canada phase, a Foppen sale at a loss (cash neutral to positive, statutory negative), or a further inventory build like FY2025's £64m (which would push the FY2026 to FY2027 peak to about £230m and 1.6x).

5d

Cost-out scope NTM

2 answers
C-7735P-0355
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

F3526th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: in an open-book model, very little of the cost base is cuttable for Hilton's own benefit for long: raw materials are the customer's cost, and savings in labour and overheads are visible in the audit and partly shared at each reset. The cuttable costs that accrue to Hilton durably are the losses in units outside the contracts (seafood) and central overheads outside pass-through; realistic gross savings over three years are about £10m to £20m, of which Hilton might keep £5m to £10m.

  • Raw proteins and consumables (£3.44bn, about 82 percent of revenue): not Hilton's lever in price; yield (pack-weight accuracy, waste) is. Machine-learning line control at Huntingdon is rolling out; 0.1 percent of yield on this base is about £3.4m, largely shared with the customer under open book.
  • Labour (£295.9m): automation cuts labour participation about 10 percent per deployed site; offset by National Living Wage and National Insurance rises; within-year savings retained, later partly shared.
  • Overheads and central: "One Hilton" removes management layers and duplicate regional functions; procurement synergies; the transformation cost (£4.6m in H1 FY26) suggests savings of a similar annual order; overheads sit largely outside pass-through, so savings here are kept.
  • Seafood (outside contracts): Foppen's airfreight already removed (£2.1m in Q1 FY26); production inefficiency (£3.0m in H1) removable on re-approval or sale; Foppen's adjusted loss (£2.2m in H1); Seachill's cost-out. About £5m to £8m a year, the largest durable item.
  • Finance cost: reducible only by lower debt or rates; customer-finance interest falls if meat prices fall.
P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

C+556th of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: about £8m to £9m of cost elimination is likely to reach adjusted operating profit by FY2030 (about 7 percent of FY2030 operating profit, or about 6.5p of EPS), arriving slowly from H2 FY2027, and about £15m a year more is likely to reach statutory profit and free cash flow through falling exceptional costs by FY2029. The two streams differ because most of the identifiable cost (Foppen's disruption, transformation, restructuring) is booked below adjusted operating profit. The efficiency that Hilton generates inside its pass-through plants (automation, machine-learning line control, yield) is a rolling float, not a cumulative stock: it is retained between resets and returned at them, so it supports the margin rather than lifting it. Nothing in the evidence supports a large step-change cut of the Greencore-Bakkavor kind, because Hilton has no comparable overlap to remove.

What reaches adjusted operating profit, by source and year (increments against FY2026, cumulative):

Source FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030 Why this size
Seafood losses removed (Seachill to a small profit, Foppen's adjusted loss shrinking) +2.0 +3.0 +4.0 +4.5 Group seafood adjusted operating profit about -£3.5m in FY2026 (Foppen -£2.2m in H1); Seachill's layer removal and yield work are under way; Foppen at even odds between continued restriction and resolution or sale
"One Hilton" overheads, procurement and regional duplication (retained, outside pass-through) +1.5 +2.5 +3.0 +3.5 Transformation cost £4.6m in H1 FY26 and about £10m a year projected; savings target undisclosed; benefits of the earlier "Genesis" transformation were "re-phased" in the remuneration report (AlphaSense operating-leverage report), so we haircut timing
Automation and yield in pass-through plants (net of resets) about 0 to +0.5 about +0.5 about +0.5 about +0.5 Retained within the year, returned at the reset: a float of £1m to £2m that does not grow
Total +3.5 to +4.0 +6.0 +7.5 +8.5 These sit inside the existing-estate and seafood lines of our build

What reaches statutory profit and free cash flow, not adjusted operating profit:

  • Adjusting items were £27.6m for Foppen and £5.0m of restructuring in FY2025, and £21.0m of continuing items in H1 FY26 (Foppen £7.0m including £2.1m of airfreight and £3.0m of production inefficiencies, transformation £4.6m, restructuring £3.2m). Management has projected about £10m a year of exceptional cash transformation cost (AlphaSense, citing the 3 September 2026 call).
  • We carry exceptional cash costs of about £20m (FY2026), £12m (FY2027), £8m (FY2028), £6m (FY2029) and £5m (FY2030). The fall from £20m to £6m (+£14m a year) is the largest cost-elimination benefit in the case, and it reaches free cash flow and statutory EPS (about 11p of statutory EPS), not adjusted EPS.
  • Foppen is the swing item. Re-approval by the United States Food and Drug Administration (four outstanding issues fed back, per the September 2026 call) would end the production inefficiency (about £6m a year at H1's rate), remove most of the adjusted loss (about £4m a year) and restore the higher-margin United States trade; a sale would remove the loss and the inefficiency at the cost of a write-down. In both cases about £4m reaches adjusted operating profit and about £10m more reaches cash. Continued restriction through 2027 keeps both in place.

Why the efficiency inside the pass-through plants does not accumulate:

  • Under open-book audit the retailer sees every saving; Deutsche Bank (3 September 2026) and a former Hilton UK manager (December 2022 expert call) describe a target processing margin with shared savings at resets. The share Hilton keeps and the reset interval are undisclosed (the AlphaSense report found no source).
  • The precedent from Cranswick (a 10 percent headcount reduction at Preston on a 15 percent higher kill, line speeds up 33 percent; Capital Markets Day, March 2025) and Greencore (£15m of automation for about £8m of savings over three years; Deutsche Bank) shows that automation savings are real and large. But Cranswick prices on a cost-of-production basis with a cash margin and Greencore recovers only about 75 percent of inflation automatically, so both keep more of their savings than Hilton does.
  • For the model: automation is defensive. It offsets National Living Wage and National Insurance rises (Deutsche Bank's October 2024 analysis puts Hilton's residual exposure to the 2025 National Insurance rise at only £1.8m, against £13.5m at Bakkavor and £11.7m at Greencore), secures renewals, and wins new partners (Walmart chose Hilton for its automated plant design). It does not lift profit per kilogram above the contractual norm.

What does not reach the line items, and where we differ from the first set:

  • Dalco: no further benefit; its loss is already out of the FY2026 continuing base (orientation point 1). The first set's "about £8m of losses to remove" (C-21, C-78) should be read as about £4m to £5m from FY2026.
  • Fairfax Meadow: its contribution was lost in FY2026 (about £3m to £4.6m) and is already in the base.
  • Large cuts: the first set's gross £10m to £20m of savings over three years, of which Hilton keeps £5m to £10m (C-77), is consistent with our £8.5m by FY2030 retained in adjusted operating profit plus a float of £1m to £2m in the plants; the timing is later than the first set implied, because there is no disclosed target and the earlier programme was re-phased.

When: almost nothing in H2 FY2026 (transformation costs still running at about £10m a year); the first measurable benefit in H2 FY2027 (seafood and overheads); the bulk in FY2028 to FY2029. The cash benefit (lower exceptional costs) starts in FY2027 and is largest by FY2029.

Peer precedents on delivery against promise: Cloetta's "One Cloetta" delivered SEK 60m to 70m of annual savings by Q1 2026 and 30 percent of it in 2025 against a 20 percent target (an early deliverer); Greencore's Bakkavor procurement synergies delivered £15m in FY2026, £5m ahead of Shore Capital's forecast, on a stated £80m target by January 2029. Both had disclosed targets; Hilton does not, and its last programme was re-phased. We therefore lean later and smaller than a disclosed-target precedent would suggest.

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4733P-0530
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?F3328th of 35full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

F3033rd of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the strongest loop over the whole four years is balancing: the open-book reset returns efficiency and cost shocks to a contractual norm, which pins existing-estate profit per kilogram to 18.5p to 19.2p and caps the margin. The strongest loop over the next 18 months is a second balancing loop on volume (raw-material inflation, consumer trade-down, lower value mix, higher cost per kilogram), which is fading in Europe and will restart in Australia in 2027 to 2028. The one reinforcing positive loop (a record of single-retailer partnerships attracting new partners) is real but slow and lumpy, and its next step (a second Walmart site) is undated and is not in our base. Against it runs a slow reinforcing negative loop in the UK (retailers moving streams to integrated processors with captive cattle, which weakens Hilton's utilisation and its cost position at the next tender). Net, the loops make the model mean-reverting in profit per kilogram and step-wise in volume: operating profit grows about 5 to 8 percent a year, revenue 3.5 to 4 percent, and the FY2029 to FY2030 numbers depend on Canada and Poland more than on anything in the existing estate.

The loops, ranked by their effect on the line items:

  1. Balancing, strongest over four years: the open-book reset (margin per kilogram). - How it works: Hilton invests in automation or yield; unit cost falls; the retailer's audit sees it; at the reset the fee is lowered or the saving shared; the margin returns to the target (a former manager put the typical red-meat target at about 3 percent with shared savings, December 2022). In reverse, a cost shock compresses the margin until the next reset restores it. - Evidence it is operating now: Deutsche Bank's flat-margin assumption (about 2.1 percent for a decade, 29 January 2026); profit per kilogram 19.0p (FY2024), 18.2p (FY2025), 17.7p (H1 FY26) with the recovery pattern of FY2023 to FY2024 (margin 2.4 to 2.6 percent). - Effect on the lines: caps the EBITDA margin in the existing estate; turns automation into a defensive float (P-03); stops any margin expansion story. Strengthened by retailer price wars (Tesco, Walmart, Woolworths); weakened only by a contract that lets Hilton keep savings for the term (the Canadian ten-year contract may be such a case, untested).

  2. Balancing, strongest over 18 months: inflation, trade-down and cost per kilogram. - How it works: beef prices rise (European retail beef over 50 percent year on year, UK cattle over 20 percent); retail prices rise; shoppers trade from steak to mince and lighter packs (a Kepak commercial director, December 2025: steak demand "dropped significantly" while mince held); value per pack and line efficiency fall; cost per kilogram rises on a fixed plant base; profit per kilogram falls. - Evidence: West core meat volumes +0.6 percent and profit -20.5 percent in H1 FY26; Seachill's 6.8 percent volume fall in FY2025 on white-fish inflation (haddock up to 90 percent, cod 30 percent). - Effect on the lines and timing: in Europe the loop is near its trough (prices plateauing); in Australia it restarts in 2027 to 2028 as cattle availability falls. We carry it as a £1.5m drag in FY2028 and a slower existing-estate growth rate (2.5 percent) that year. It is self-limiting because the fee per kilogram and the annual reset eventually reprice; it does not compound.

  3. Reinforcing, positive, real but slow: the partnership record attracts partners. - How it works: a successful dedicated plant for a lead grocer becomes the reference that wins the next grocer (Australia and Europe led to Walmart Canada and NADEC; Żabka's growth led to Romania); each new plant adds volume, technology scale and credibility. - Evidence: Walmart Canada (ten years, launch January 2027), NADEC (49 percent, Q4 2026), Żabka volumes tripled in three years, Romania with Froo. Walmart's stated wish to replicate the model elsewhere in North America is the next step, but Shore Capital (May 2026) notes that "current focus is solely on the Ontario facility", and no date, site or budget exists. - Effect on the lines: revenue +1 to 2 percent a year from FY2027; operating profit +£2m to £3m a year from FY2028 as Canada and Poland absorb fixed cost (P-01); capital expenditure stays above the £45m to £55m core range through FY2028. A second Walmart site would add, by analogy with the first, about £80m to £100m of capital from about FY2029 and £5m to £10m of operating profit from about FY2031, outside our window. - What would stall it: a Canada start-up that is late or loss-making (the same expert evidence cites 10 to 15 percent higher initial sourcing costs), or capital overruns (Canada's budget rose by a third). The loop's first test is observable at the January 2027 trading update and the H1 FY27 results in September 2027.

  4. Reinforcing, negative, slow: volume re-allocation to integrated processors. - How it works: retailers since the pandemic prefer suppliers with captive raw material (a former Tyson vice-president, November 2025); a stream moves (Tesco's Scottish beef to Foyle and Kepak); Hilton's plant utilisation falls; its unit cost rises; its position at the next benchmark or tender weakens; more volume can move. - Evidence: the Scottish beef move in H1 FY26; Tesco's Sustainable Beef Group investment through Foyle and Kepak; ABP's Dovecote Park stake under review; Dawn Meats' £270m (New Zealand dollar) stake in Alliance Group to secure year-round supply. - Counter-evidence: West volumes still rose 1.1 percent in H1 FY26 because lamb and Nordic volume (Coop, Salling, Burger King in Sweden) offset beef; integrated processors are themselves closing plants (ABP's Linden plant, 338 jobs, January 2026; Kepak's Merthyr review, June 2026) as herds shrink. - Effect on the lines: we carry an allowance of about 1 percent of West volume a year (inside the existing estate's volume growth of about 1.8 percent), which the first set also proposed. A second large stream move would cost £2m to £4m of operating profit in the year.

  5. Reinforcing, positive, small: secondary customers fill plants. Salling, Netto, Dunnes Stores and Burger King lower unit cost in existing plants; this offsets loop 4 at the margin but lacks the scale of a national contract. Inside our existing-estate volume.

  6. Balancing, financial: the capital-expenditure peak. Growth capital raises net debt; the finance charge rises; EPS growth slows (FY2027); free cash flow then covers the dividend; leverage falls below 1.0x by FY2030; surplus capacity appears. This loop is why the finance line rises before it falls (P-02).

  7. Weaker than the first set thought: inflation and supply-chain finance interest. The first set treated higher meat prices as raising working-capital values and the interest on customer-provided finance. Interest on that finance was £4.2m in H1 FY26 against £4.4m a year earlier despite further inflation; the loop is not currently amplifying.

How the loops shape the main lines over the window (the net):

  • Revenue: grows about 3.5 to 4.2 percent a year after FY2026, of which loop 3 adds 1 to 2 points.
  • Operating profit and EBITDA margin: loop 1 holds the existing estate to 2.5 to 3 percent growth; loops 3 and 5 add new-plant absorption; loop 2 subtracts in FY2028; net operating profit growth of 5.8, 6.9, 7.9 and 6.4 percent (FY2027 to FY2030). The EBITDA margin rises only 0.3 points in four years.
  • Finance cost and free cash flow: loop 6 makes FY2027 the worst year for EPS gearing and FY2029 to FY2030 the best for cash.
  • The asymmetry: the balancing loops act within a year; the reinforcing positive loop acts over three to five years. That is the mechanical reason the next 18 months skew down while FY2029 to FY2030 skew up.
