6c

Case simplicity

7 answers
Judgement45C-7952D-0268D-0358D-0436D-0532D-0650
Group judgement

Risks

D4524th of 35

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The forecasts are predictable in structure and unpredictable in timing: the Consumer can see every link from the company's statements to EPS, can name the six items that separate the forecast from the record, and can date the checkpoints at which each is tested; what the Consumer cannot do is trust the dates, because every Hilton project of the last four years has slipped, and the next 18 months contain a plant start, a regulator's decision, a cattle cycle and a wage reset at once, with a 2 percent margin and a finance charge that absorbs 28 percent of operating profit to amplify any miss. The two earlier sets are congruent, and the new evidence has on balance improved predictability: the Woolworths contract runs to 2033 and Tesco was extended in 2026, which shrinks the largest tail; the FY2026 tax rate is guided at about 28 percent; Canada's lease and overruns are at least partly shared with Walmart. The same evidence worsens the FY2027 central case by about 1.5p (capitalised interest ending) and raises my view of exceptional cash. The cone for FY2027 (47p to 61p around 55p) is wide for a defensive staple and skewed down, because the bear ingredients act within the year and the bull ingredients lag; FY2026 is the reverse, narrow and skewed slightly up against consensus. Disclosure is honest and twice-yearly, so between results the Consumer must monitor from outside (MLA cattle data, Woolworths' and Tesco's sales, the CMA, the FDA, the Bank of England). The shares and the multiple are company-driven (10 percent explained by peers), which makes the outcome depend on the staircase the Consumer can model and removes the sector as a cushion. For the model: FY2026 revenue growth 12.4 percent (a base correction); net financial items £30.0m, £29.5m, £28.5m and £27.5m from FY2027; exceptional cash of £24m, £15m, £10m, £7m and £5m outside the inputs; EPS 54.0p, 55.1p, 60.7p, 67.7p and 74.1p. For the memo: FY2027 is the year the thesis is tested and FY2026 the year the new team's credibility is banked; a flat H1 FY27 profit before tax in September 2027 is our base case, not a miss, and consensus implies better.

C-79

How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

B529th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: the perimeter is wide by country count and narrow by business model: one model (dedicated automated packing for a lead grocer) repeated across about ten operating countries, now with two new ones. It is narrowing in businesses (three exits) and widening in countries. Predictability is good on volume and poor on idiosyncratic events, because each country is one customer and one regulator.

  • Countries: 21 facilities in 19 countries serving about 21 markets; the operating countries that matter are about ten (UK, Ireland, Netherlands, Sweden, Denmark, Poland, Portugal, Greece, Australia, New Zealand), plus Saudi Arabia (Q4 2026) and Canada (January 2027).
  • Units: East, West, the ring-fenced seafood businesses, and the two new ventures; Dalco, Fairfax Meadow and Foods Connected gone.
  • Predictability: improved by removing volatile units and by excluding seafood from targets; impaired by single-customer exposure in each country, currency translation (about two-thirds of revenue outside the UK) and the start-up of two new plants at once.
D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

C-6815th of 35

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the expected change is plausible because about four-fifths of it rests on strengths Hilton has demonstrated repeatedly, and the one-fifth that rests on strengths it would need to acquire (a seafood turnaround, an overhead programme delivered on time, a first plant in a market with four giant primary packers) is small in the base case and dated late. Demonstrated: taking a dedicated automated plant from greenfield to mature returns in a new country (Queensland 2019; Auckland 2021 with 265 lines launched inside six months; mature Asia-Pacific returns above 30 percent on capital); holding volume through inflation (core volumes +0.2 percent in FY2025 and +2.1 percent in H1 FY26 through 30 to 50 percent beef inflation); renewing anchor contracts (Netherlands and Denmark in 2025, Tesco in 2026, Woolworths to 2033); selling non-core units at fair prices; growing with a convenience partner (Żabka volumes tripled in three years, +26 percent in H1 FY26). Not demonstrated: returning a seafood unit to sustained profit (Seachill has been a problem since 2017, Foppen since 2024), delivering an overhead programme on schedule (Genesis's benefits were re-phased, and 2025's target of 80 percent of planned benefits entering realisation was only partly met), and operating in North America. The second set's base case asks the demonstrated strengths to deliver about £20m of the £28m operating profit increase to FY2030 (existing estate £8m before overheads, Canada, Poland and NADEC £12m) and the undemonstrated ones about £8m (seafood £4.5m, overheads £3.5m), of which almost nothing falls in the next 18 months. The forecast is therefore plausible on the record; what the record does not support is speed, because every Hilton project of the last four years has been late (Canada from 2026 to 2027 and from £60m to £80m; Foppen's re-approval past every date given; Genesis re-phased).

  • The greenfield precedent is Hilton's own, and it is good. Hilton went from one plant in 1994 to 21 facilities in 19 countries without losing a core contract, and the Asia-Pacific plants reached returns above 30 percent after ramp. The peer precedents say the same with a time cost: Maple Leaf's London, Ontario poultry plant carried start-up costs of C$122m in 2023, C$20.6m in 2024 and C$3.3m in 2025 before reaching close to its C$100m EBITDA run-rate target (three years); Scandi Standard's Oosterwolde ready-to-eat plant diluted segment margin from 5.7 to 3.5 percent in 2025 and recovered to 6.2 percent by Q3 2026 (about 18 months), with "limited start-up costs" because it was a brownfield refit. Brantford is a greenfield with 150 staff, store-order-picking robotics modelled on Brisbane and a sourcing base Hilton does not control; the second set's probability-weighted path (a loss in H1 FY27, £0.5m for the year, £7.5m by FY2030) is consistent with these precedents, and Shore Capital's path (£1.5m in FY2027, over £5m by year three) is the demonstrated-strength case.
  • The strength the first set under-weighted: contractual protection of capital. The lease (20 years, £5.9m a year, indexation capped at 2.25 percent) and Shore Capital's statement that lease exposure is covered by customer agreements mean the fixed cost that most often sinks a greenfield start-up (rent and depreciation ahead of volume) is at least partly carried by Walmart. That is a demonstrated feature of the Australian contracts (interest on capital employed passes through), not a new one.
  • Seafood is the acquired-strength item, and the base needs little of it. The second set carries seafood from -£3.5m to +£1.0m by FY2030 (+£2.0m in FY2027). Evidence for even that: Seachill's layer removal and about 90 proposed redundancies (October 2026); Foppen's United States freight moved from air to sea (April 2026); a precedent (Iceland Seafood International returned to profit within 12 to 18 months of salmon prices levelling). Against: the FDA cited four outstanding issues in September 2026; Shore Capital expected no progress at the interims and expects Foppen to stop being a drag only by H2 2027; a sale under restriction would not recover the £79.8m paid in 2022 (8x to 9x EBITDA). I hold the second set's path but would place more of the improvement in FY2028 than in FY2027.
  • The "One Hilton" overheads (£3.5m retained by FY2030) have no disclosed target and a re-phased predecessor. Peers with targets delivered early (Cloetta's SEK 60m to 70m; Greencore's £15m of procurement synergies in year one against £80m by 2029); Hilton's last programme did not. The second set's haircut and late phasing are right; I would credit no overhead saving in the next 18 months.
  • The volume-with-margin strength is demonstrated, with a known exception. Profit per kilogram fell from 19.0p to 17.7p over 18 months, but almost all of that is seafood and the Scottish-beef and Irish episodes in the West; East held 2.45 percent through 50 percent beef inflation, and the first set's thesis-breaker (an Oceania margin below 3.0 percent) is far off. The strength not demonstrated is holding the West's profit per kilogram when a customer re-allocates; the base case carries that as a running 1 percent of West volume.

D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

F3627th of 35

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: narrow for FY2026 and wide for FY2027, with the skew changing sign between them. FY2026 (two months left, H1 reported, a £5m guidance range, three named H2 tailwinds and a guided tax rate) sits inside 52.5p to 55.5p of EPS with about 75 percent probability, and the residual skew is slightly up relative to consensus's 52.4p. FY2027 is the test year: I put the 80 percent range at about 47p to 61p around a central 55p (the second set's 56.6p before the finance correction), a cone of about ±13 percent, with a probability-weighted mean about 1p below the central case because the bear ingredients (Canada late or loss-making, Foppen unresolved, an Australian volume shortfall, a further West stream lost, Bank Rate 50 basis points higher) arrive inside the year, while the bull ingredients (Foppen re-approved, Canada at Shore Capital's £1.5m, a stronger Australian dollar) arrive with a lag or are binary. The predictability of the case over 18 months is therefore moderate-to-low: a 2 percent margin business whose finance charge absorbs 28 percent of operating profit turns small operating misses into large EPS misses, and Hilton's own record (misses in FY2022 and FY2025, the years of raw-material stress and project disruption) says the stress years are the ones to fear, and FY2027 contains a new plant start, an Australian cattle squeeze and a wage rise. What narrows the cone relative to the first set's view is the contract evidence (Woolworths to 2033; Tesco extended; Dutch and Danish renewals done), which removes most of the tail the first set's C-81 ranked first.

The cone, built from the drivers (FY2027 EPS, company adjusted after minorities):

  • Central case, 55p: operating profit about £101m (existing estate +3 percent, Canada +£0.5m, seafood +£2m), finance charge £30.0m, tax 28.5 percent, 90.4m shares.
  • Bear case (about 20 percent), about 47p: operating profit about £92m (Canada -£3m from a late start or a loss through H2; Australian volume -£3m as slaughter falls 6 percent and Woolworths holds price; Foppen unresolved with a further -£1m; a further West stream re-allocated, -£2m), finance charge £31.5m (rates 50 basis points higher, no reversal), tax 29 percent. The full combination is improbable; the point is that each ingredient alone costs 2p to 4p and two together (the FY2025 pattern) cost 6p to 8p.
  • Bull case (about 20 percent), about 61p: operating profit about £107m (Foppen re-approved +£3m; Canada at or above Shore Capital's £1.5m; the Australian dollar +£1.5m; interim Polish capacity earlier), finance charge £28.5m (Bank Rate reversed in 2027, as Rothschild Redburn expects), tax 28 percent.
  • The widths by line: operating profit £92m to £107m (±7 percent); profit before tax £60m to £79m (±13 percent); EPS 47p to 61p (±13 percent). The gearing from operating profit to EPS roughly doubles the width; that is the structural fact behind the low predictability.

What decides the skew:

  • Time of arrival. A volume loss (re-allocation, Australian scarcity, a late plant) hits fixed cost in the half it occurs; an efficiency gain is partly returned at the next reset; a Foppen re-approval, if it came in H1 FY27, would help H2 FY27 at most. That is the second set's P-05 asymmetry and it holds.
  • The record. Five years: two warnings in two stress years, two deliveries in two stable years, one beat on a reset bar. FY2027's conditions resemble the stress years more than the stable ones. The new team's style (a low bar, honest labelling) improves the chance that guidance survives, not that the central case is reached.
  • What has narrowed since the first set: the anchor-loss tail (C-81's first-ranked risk at 5 to 10 percent over three years) is lower on the contract evidence; FY2026's tax rate is guided; H1 FY26 was a beat; the Dutch and Danish renewals are done. What has widened: the finance charge (capitalised interest ends; rates rising), recurring exceptional cash (£14.8m in H1 FY26), the integration signals at Tesco, and Shore Capital's reminder that Walmart is only the fifth or sixth grocer in Canada and Brantford serves only the east.
  • Statutory against adjusted. The cone on statutory EPS is wider still: adjusting items were £21.0m in H1 FY26; IFRS 18 (effective 1 January 2027, though the UK Endorsement Board has not yet endorsed it and Associated British Foods targets 2028) forces management-defined measures into an audited note with no separate "exceptional" line on the face of the income statement; and a Foppen sale or a permanent restriction would trigger an impairment of goodwill (£18.3m) and intangibles (£18.6m) that the company says is sensitive to a 2.4 percent fall in Foppen's EBITDA. None of that moves adjusted EPS, but it can move the market's "adjusted" EPS if brokers re-base.
D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

F3233rd of 35

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: helpful twice a year and nearly silent in between, which is the wrong rhythm for a six-month window that contains the two most important operational events of the case. The next six months hold a January 2027 trading update (historically a one-paragraph confirmation that the year is "in line", with volumes described in an adjective), the FY2026 results on 7 April 2027 (full disclosure: volumes by region and category, East and West margins, adjusting items by line, net bank debt, capital expenditure, FY2027 guidance) and nothing scheduled on Canada's commissioning or on Foppen beyond what management chooses to say. The monitoring the thesis needs is: (1) Canada commissioned on time and ramping, observable only through management statements until September 2027; (2) Foppen's FDA position, binary and undated, and the company's past dates slipped; (3) West core-meat volumes and Irish pricing, at results only; (4) Australian cattle and Woolworths' volumes, where external data are good (MLA projections, the Eastern Young Cattle Indicator, Woolworths' quarterly sales); (5) the finance charge and net bank debt after the frozen-meat purchase, at results only, and the purchase price was not disclosed. Only (4) can be tracked monthly from outside. The company's disclosure is honest and improving (a full-time Investor Relations Director since January 2026; adjusting items itemised; the East and West restatement), but it guides none of volumes, profit per kilogram, finance cost or Canada's contribution beyond "small low-single-digit", so the Consumer must infer momentum from one half-year print and two short updates.

  • What the January 2027 update will probably say, and what it will not. The record (January 2025: "strong" Christmas, core retail meat volumes +4.0 percent in the final quarter; January 2026: FY2025 "within expectations" and a cautious FY2026 outlook) is of a brief statement that confirms the range and gives a volume adjective. It is likely to confirm FY2026 inside £66m to £71m, say whether Canada has commissioned, and describe Christmas volumes. It will not give H2 profit, the H2 tax rate, year-end net debt or Foppen's position in numbers. The May 2027 AGM update takes the same form.
  • What the April 2027 results will give, and the four lines to read first: H2 FY26 adjusted profit before tax (our £36.2m; the range implies £33.2m to £38.2m); West core-meat volume and margin (the Scottish and Irish effects annualise); H2 exceptional cash (whether the £10m a year transformation run-rate held); and year-end net bank debt (our about £192m before the frozen-meat purchase). Then FY2027 guidance: its form (a profit before tax range) and its midpoint against consensus's £74.0m.
  • Where disclosure is weakest for the thesis: Canada's economics (no fee basis, no store count, no volume commitment; the lease recovery not shown; the Canada report lists the distribution centres served, Cornwall, Moncton and Mississauga, but not stores); seafood breakeven thresholds; supply-chain finance facility sizes and rates; a "One Hilton" savings target (none published); and the Polish capital decision ("up to £30m", "bigger than originally envisaged", scoping to end-2026).
  • External monitors that substitute for disclosure over the six months: MLA's quarterly cattle projections and the Eastern Young Cattle Indicator (928.6 Australian cents a kilogram); Woolworths' Q2 FY27 sales (late January 2027) and Tesco's Q3 (January 2027) for the customers' volume and price programmes; the CMA's ABP and Dovecote Park decision (December 2026) for integration; FDA import-alert and warning-letter records for Foppen; the Bank of England on 5 November 2026 and through 2027; and the UK Endorsement Board's IFRS 18 position.
D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

C5010th of 35

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the internal transmission is clear in structure and thin in numbers; the external transmission is clear in mechanism and lagged in time. What the company says it will achieve (mid-single-digit operating profit growth on average excluding seafood; FY2026 profit before tax of £66m to £71m; a tax rate of about 28 percent; capital expenditure of about £100m then a £45m to £55m core; leverage of 1.0x to 2.0x; return on capital above 20 percent through the cycle; cash conversion of about 100 percent; dividend cover rebuilt toward 2.0x; Canada "small low-single-digit" in 2027 and "full" in 2029; NADEC break-even in year one) traces onto two operational drivers it discloses (volumes by region and category; East and West adjusted margins) and one it does not (profit per kilogram, which the Consumer derives), then through a finance charge whose components are disclosed at results (bank, lease and supply-chain finance interest; capitalised interest), a guided tax rate, undisclosed minorities of about £1m and a share count rising about 0.3 percent a year, to adjusted EPS. The chain is short and its bases are now stable (continuing perimeter; East, West and seafood), which is why the earlier sets could build a model from it. What breaks the trace is that the company guides none of the links except tax: no volume, fee, margin or finance-cost guidance, no seafood threshold, no Canada profit number, and no reconciliation of "mid-single-digit excluding seafood" to the group figure the market trades on. Externally, the chain from value chain to EPS is clear and lagged: raw-material prices pass through to revenue inside ten to twelve weeks and to profit only through mix, lag and working-capital interest; conversion-cost inflation passes through at annual resets (a drag of up to a year, which the April 2027 National Living Wage rise of 3.9 percent will test in H1 FY27); cattle availability acts on volume, not on price per kilogram, in Australia; interest rates act on the charge within the year (£2.1m per 50 basis points); currency acts on translation daily (about £2.2m per 10 percent); and customers' sourcing decisions act on volume without notice.

The trace, link by link, with what is disclosed and what must be inferred:

  1. Statement to driver. "Mid-single-digit operating profit growth excluding seafood" is about 5 percent on a base of about £99m (FY2026 excluding seafood's -£3.5m), which, with the existing estate at about 2 percent volume and 1 percent fee creep, requires Canada, Poland and NADEC to add about £2m a year on average and more from FY2028. The company does not say this, but the arithmetic is tight enough that the Consumer can.
  2. Driver to lines. Volume (disclosed by region) times profit per kilogram (derived: 18.2p in FY2025, 17.7p in H1 FY26) gives operating profit; the East margin (2.45 percent in H1 FY26) and the West core-meat margin (2.8 percent) are disclosed half-yearly; seafood's adjusted result is disclosed (-£2.2m in H1 FY26); central costs are derived (about £4.9m in the half). Canada's revenue, fee basis and fixed cost are not disclosed; the Consumer must take Shore Capital's £65m and £1.5m or the second set's haircut of it.
  3. Lines to EPS. The finance charge's components are disclosed half-yearly (H1 FY26: bank £9.8m gross less £1.6m capitalised; lease £3.8m; supply-chain finance £4.2m; other £1.4m; income £0.8m; £3.8m adjusted out); the rate sensitivity is disclosed annually; the tax rate is guided; minorities are not guided; the share count is disclosed. Every link is visible, so the trace can be audited at each results date, a strength relative to a company that guides only EPS.
  4. Milestones the company has named, and what each is worth: Canada commissioned in January 2027 (the single largest FY2027 swing); NADEC operational in Q4 2026 (nil to FY2027 EPS); Polish scoping complete at end-2026 (a capital signal; profit from FY2028); Seachill yield benefits from H2 FY26 (£1m to £2m); Foppen "all options" (±3p to 4p adjusted, £10m to £15m of cash); return on capital back above 20 percent (our FY2028); dividend cover toward 2.0x (not reached by FY2030 on our path).

Where the external transmission is least clear, and so least predictable: the share of conversion-cost inflation recovered and when (the company says partners absorb it; Deutsche Bank's 2024 work put the residual National Insurance exposure at only £1.8m; former employees say retailers delay); the reset interval and the efficiency-sharing formula (undisclosed, and the central reason the margin cannot be forecast to expand); and the elasticity of retail meat volume to the Australian price rises Morgan Stanley expects (retailers passed through 50 to 60 percent of past rises; Woolworths' Q1 FY27 fresh sales of +8.1 percent say volume is holding so far).

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

D3224th of 35

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: Hilton is not an upgrader. Over two years it has issued one upgrade (technical, and labelled so) against two downgrades. That is honest tracking, not sandbagging, and not a "beat and raise" habit.

  • Prior team: "confident FY25 outlook" (January 2025) followed by a Q3 FY2025 warning (FY2025 range lowered to £72m to £75m against about £80.5m previously expected) and a November 2025 cut to FY2026 expectations.

P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

F4226th of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the forecasts deserve high weight for FY2026, moderate weight for FY2027, and low weight for FY2028 to FY2030, and the lean should change sign over the window. FY2026 should be leaned up against consensus (by about 3 percent of EPS, because brokers carry FY2025's tax rate and sit at the bottom of a range whose upper half the named H2 drivers support). FY2027 and FY2028 should be leaned down: Hilton's record is to miss in years of raw-material stress and project start-up, and FY2027 to FY2028 contain both (Australian cattle, Canada, a new Polish build, and the National Living Wage). FY2029 to FY2030 should be held at our central case with the residual risk balanced, because the upside options (Foppen resolved, Canada at its hurdle, Walmart extending the model, a capital return) are as large as the downside. The new management team's record of honest labelling raises the weight on what it says; the organisation's record of slipping projects lowers the weight on project dates.

The record, in the form that matters for weighting:

Year Condition What happened against the bar Lesson
FY2022 Inflation spike, seafood Profit warning on seafood inflation Misses in spikes
FY2023 Stable costs £66.0m, in line Meets in stable years

  • Projects: Canada's budget rose from £60m to £80m (and bacon is extra) and the launch slipped from 2026 to January 2027; Foppen's United States re-approval has missed every date given since 2025 (FDA feedback named four outstanding issues in September 2026); the earlier "Genesis" transformation's benefits were re-phased (remuneration report); NADEC and Poland are on schedule so far.
  • Incentive signal: the July 2026 long-term incentive plan pays from 3 percent (threshold) to 8 percent (stretch) EPS growth a year. If measured from FY2025's 56.0p, our FY2028 EPS of 61.9p is 3.4 percent a year, just above threshold; consensus (62.6p) is 3.8 percent. Management is not paid to expect more, and our path does not ask for more.

Weight, by horizon:

  • FY2026 (high weight; we would expect the outcome within plus or minus 3 percent of our EPS 75 to 80 percent of the time): H1 is reported; H2 depends on Christmas trading, Foppen and currency; the guidance range is narrow (£66m to £71m) and the team has beaten its own bar. The main risks are an H2 West shortfall and the H2 interest charge after the frozen-meat purchase.

  • FY2028 (low-to-moderate weight; plus or minus 15 percent): Canada's first full year with bacon, Poland's capacity, the trough of Australian cattle supply, and the first possible renewals of anchor contracts (dates unknown).
  • FY2029 to FY2030 (low weight; plus or minus 20 percent): these years rest on two plants reaching maturity and on the absence of a contract loss; one broker forecasts FY2029 and none FY2030.

Lean, by horizon, and by how much:

  • FY2026: lean up against consensus, not against our own figure. Our 54.0p is 3 percent above the 52.4p median because of tax (28 percent against about 30 percent implied) and profit before tax (£69.0m against £67.9m). We hold our own figure at the guidance's upper-middle, not the top, because H2 must still overcome Ireland, Scotland and Foppen.
  • FY2027: lean down by about 2 percent against consensus (our 56.6p against 57.6p), and treat the residual risk around our own figure as skewed down by a further 2 to 3 percent: the record says years of start-up and raw-material stress produce misses, and our Canada figure (£0.5m) is already below Shore Capital's £1.5m.
  • FY2028: lean down by about 1 percent against consensus (61.9p against 62.6p), with a downside skew of 3 to 5 percent around our own figure. We differ from the first set, which leaned FY2028 3 to 8 percent below consensus (C-67): part of the first set's gap was its tax rate of 29.5 percent, against consensus's implied 27.8 percent and our 28.5 percent; once tax is put at our level, the remaining difference is risk skew rather than central case.
  • FY2029 to FY2030: no lean. Our 68.6p for FY2029 is 3 percent above the single broker's 66.6p because we carry Canada and Poland ramping; the upside options (Foppen resolution worth about 3p, Canada nearer its hurdle worth 5p to 8p, a buyback worth about 3p) balance the downside (a contract loss worth 10p to 20p at low probability, a Canada stall worth 4p to 6p).

What would change the weights: a January 2027 trading update showing Canada commissioned on time and H2 FY26 at or above £36m of profit before tax would raise the FY2027 weight; a slip of Canada past March 2027, West core meat volumes negative, or an Oceania margin below 3.0 percent (the first set's thesis-breaker) would lower it and justify a further 3 to 5 percent cut to FY2027 to FY2028 EPS.

Group judgement

Financial forecasts

F4029th of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The financial forecasts describe a business that is past its earnings trough but not yet on a growth path: operating profit recovers about 6 percent a year in FY2027 to FY2028 and about 7 percent in FY2029 to FY2030, EPS regains FY2024's 61p only in FY2028, and the real change is in cash, which turns from a £32m deficit to about £60m a year once Canada and Poland are built. The structure of the business explains the shape. The open-book reset is the dominant loop: it returns efficiency to the customer, holds existing-estate profit per kilogram at 18.5p to 19.2p, and makes automation defensive rather than margin-building, so the existing estate grows at about volume growth plus a little nominal creep (2.5 to 3 percent a year). Price is a revenue line, not a profit line. The growth that the open-book reset does not take back comes from new dedicated plants filling up under ten-year contracts (Canada, Poland, the NADEC venture), which supply about 40 percent of the operating profit increase to FY2030, and from removing seafood losses and overheads, which supply about 30 percent. Below operating profit, the tax rate (28 to 28.5 percent, below consensus's FY2026 to FY2027 assumption) helps FY2026; the finance charge rises in FY2027 before it falls; and there is no buyback, although the balance sheet falls below management's 1.0x leverage floor by FY2030 and will force a capital-return or second-project decision. Four corrections to the first set matter for the Consumer: Dalco's removal is already in the FY2026 base and adds nothing to FY2027; the tax rate is about 28 percent, not 29 to 30 percent; the gearing of EPS to a fixed finance charge does not operate in FY2027; and most of Foppen's cost reaches cash, not adjusted EPS. Against consensus we are above in FY2026, below in FY2027 and FY2028, and above in FY2029. For the predictability test, the case is concentrated (Canada, Poland, seafood, tax and capital expenditure explain almost all of the difference between the forecast and the record) and checkable at dated points (January and April 2027, September 2027, spring 2028). The residual skew is downward over the next 18 months (Australian cattle, Canada's start-up, customer re-allocation, the company's record of missing in years of stress) and balanced to upward beyond it (Canada's contract terms, Foppen, a second Walmart site, a capital return).

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

C+5812th of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: below operating profit, the next four years will be calmer than the last six for adjusted EPS and net income and much better for free cash flow, and they add to EPS growth only from FY2028, and modestly: tax steadies at 28 to 28.5 percent (lower than FY2025's 29.8 percent and consensus's FY2026 to FY2027 assumption, higher than consensus's FY2028), the finance charge rises in FY2027 and then stops rising and falls, minorities stay at about £1m, the share count drifts up about 0.3 percent a year, and there is no buyback. Together they take operating profit growth of 6.7 percent a year (FY2026 to FY2030) to EPS growth of 8.4 percent a year, the extra coming from a flat-then-falling charge in FY2028 to FY2030 against a depressed FY2026 base; from FY2025 to FY2028 EPS grows only 3.4 percent a year. The decisive below-the-line change is in cash and statutory profit, not adjusted EPS: exceptional cash costs fall from about £20m to about £5m, which, with capital expenditure falling from £100m to £55m, takes strict free cash flow from about minus £32m (FY2026) to about plus £66m (FY2030), against a six-year FactSet history that averaged about zero.

Tax:

  • History: adjusted effective rate 24.9 percent (FY2024), 29.8 percent (FY2025, capital-allowance true-ups; this cut FY2025 adjusted EPS by about 7 percent), 27.7 percent (H1 FY26); FactSet's reported rate ranged from 15.4 to 35.7 percent over FY2019 to FY2025.
  • Our rate: 28.0 percent (FY2026, the company's guidance as reported by the AlphaSense EPS-bridge report), 28.5 percent (FY2027 to FY2030). The mix: Australia (30 percent statutory) is the largest profit pool; the UK is 25 percent; Canada (about 26.5 percent combined) and Poland (19 percent) grow; the Netherlands is about 26 percent. The Pillar Two minimum (15 percent) does not bind in any of these. We add 0.5 points from FY2027 for the risk of further true-ups and a heavier Australian weight.
  • Against consensus: consensus implies about 29.7 percent (FY2026), 29.2 percent (FY2027) and 27.8 percent (FY2028). Our FY2026 rate is worth +1.3p of EPS against consensus; FY2027 +0.6p; FY2028 -0.6p. We disagree with the first set (C-54, C-67), which carried 29 to 30 percent and called consensus's FY2028 rate the quiet risk; the quieter error is in FY2026 to FY2027, in the brokers' favour of caution.
  • Sensitivity: 1 point of tax rate is about 0.8p to 1.0p of EPS (1.4 percent).

Interest: £26.5m, £28.0m, £28.0m, £27.5m, £27.0m (P-02). Against FactSet's history of net interest of £24.8m (FY2022), £38.1m (FY2023), £39.2m (FY2024) and £35.2m (FY2025), which is on a different and higher basis (including items the company adjusts out), the charge is stable; on the company's basis it was about £26m in FY2025.

Minorities and associates:

  • Minorities about £1.0m (FY2026) rising to £1.3m (FY2030) with Australian and Central European profit (FactSet balance-sheet minorities £5.6m). The Consumer's model has no minority input: our EPS cross-check deducts them (about 1.1p a year), so the model's EPS will be about 1p higher than ours for that reason alone.
  • NADEC (49 percent joint venture, equity-accounted): break-even in year one (FY2027), about £0.7m (FY2028) and about £1.3m (FY2029, Deutsche Bank's "£1.3m of net income by year three"), £1.5m (FY2030). We include it in operating profit and EBITDA for simplicity; it is non-cash unless distributed. Revenue is not consolidated.
  • Foods Connected (26.3 percent associate, £24.3m carrying value): immaterial to earnings; a future exit is optional cash.

One-offs and adjusting items (the largest below-the-line difference between adjusted and statutory):

  • History: adjusting items cost about £50m of statutory profit in the eighteen months to June 2026 (£27.6m Foppen, £5.0m restructuring and £2.9m reorganisation in FY2025; £21.0m of continuing items in H1 FY26, plus a £16.7m Dalco impairment in discontinued operations). FY2025 statutory profit before tax (£89.6m) was inflated by £66.5m of disposal gains.
  • Forecast: exceptional cash costs about £20m (FY2026), £12m (FY2027), £8m (FY2028), £6m (FY2029), £5m (FY2030). Statutory profit before tax therefore converges toward adjusted: about £35m to £40m in FY2026 (our estimate), about £60m in FY2027, about £72m in FY2028.
  • IFRS 18 from 1 January 2027: management-defined performance measures must be reconciled inside the audited accounts. This will not change cash, but it will make recurring "exceptional" transformation and restructuring visible every year; if brokers or FactSet begin to treat them as operating costs, the market's "adjusted" EPS could fall by 5p to 8p in FY2027 on a stricter basis. The Consumer should hold both bases.

Share count, buybacks and issuance:

  • About 90.0m shares at FY2025; incentive awards (261,883 options to the chief executive and 171,654 to the West chief operating officer in July 2026, plus the wider plan) add about 0.2m to 0.3m a year, about 0.3 percent dilution a year. No buyback since flotation; none in our base. The balance sheet creates capacity of about £30m to £35m by FY2030 at a 1.0x floor (P-02); we treat it as an option.
  • Dividend: 35.0p held for FY2026 (interim held at 10.1p), then 35.5p, 36.5p, 38.0p and 40.0p; cover 1.54x rising to 1.86x, toward but not reaching management's 2.0x. Cash cost about £31.5m rising to £35.6m.

The cone around the base case (18 months):

  • FY2026 EPS: 52.5p to 55.5p (H2 profit before tax of £34m to £38m; tax 27.5 to 28.5 percent).
  • FY2027, bear case (probability about 20 percent): EPS about 48p. Operating profit about £92m (Canada -£3m from a late or loss-making start, Australian volume -£3m, Foppen unresolved with a further £1m loss, a further West stream lost -£2m), finance cost £29.5m (rates 50 basis points higher), tax 29 percent.
  • FY2027, bull case (probability about 20 percent): EPS about 63p. Operating profit about £107m (Foppen re-approved +£3m, Canada at Shore Capital's level or better +£1.5m, Australian dollar +£1.5m), finance cost £27m, tax 27.5 percent.
  • The probability-weighted FY2027 EPS is about 56p, slightly below our central 56.6p: the residual skew is downward, as P-06 argues.

How our base case relates to company guidance: FY2026 adjusted profit before tax of £69.0m sits in the upper half of £66m to £71m; tax at 28 percent matches guidance; capital expenditure of £100m matches; operating profit growth excluding seafood (existing estate plus new plants) is about 5.6 percent a year over FY2026 to FY2030 (3.5 percent in FY2027, then about 6 to 7 percent), inside "mid-single-digit on average, not linear"; leverage stays inside 1.0x to 2.0x until FY2030; return on capital employed is below 20 percent in FY2026 (as guided) and back above it in FY2028; dividend cover rises toward but does not reach 2.0x by FY2030; Canada is below "small low-single-digit" in 2027 (£0.5m) and gives a "full" year in 2029 of only £5m, which is the one place where we are clearly more cautious than management's language.

Where our base case could be most wrong, in order of size: (1) Canada's contract terms: if capital cost passes through as Shore Capital's 2024 description of these agreements suggests, Canada's operating profit could be £10m to £15m by FY2030, adding 7p to 11p to EPS; (2) an anchor contract renewal (Tesco UK or Woolworths, dates unknown) on materially worse terms or with a re-allocation, costing 5p to 20p; (3) Foppen, worth plus or minus 3p to 4p of adjusted EPS and £10m to £15m of cash a year; (4) the Australian cattle squeeze in 2027 to 2028, worth 2p to 4p; (5) beef prices, which move revenue and the margin percentage by large amounts and profit by little.

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: there is plausible scope to lift operating profit from about £93m (FY2026) to £110m to £120m by FY2029 (5 to 9 percent a year), coming from a few big areas (seafood resolved, Canada at its hurdle) and many small ones (automation, Poland mix, secondary customers, Saudi Arabia). It is congruent with the strategy; the peer precedent (Greencore) shows self-help can work but relied on levers Hilton lacks.

  • Few big areas: (1) seafood: removing Foppen's loss and inefficiency and returning Seachill to profit, about £5m to £8m; (2) Canada: from about £1.5m in FY2027 toward £10m to £16m by FY2029 to FY2030 if the plant reaches its 20 percent hurdle; this single item is the largest swing and is not, we judge, fully in consensus (consensus operating profit rises only £2m from FY2028 to FY2029).

  • Against: Fairfax Meadow's lost contribution (about £3m to £4.6m in FY2026); Australian beef from 2027; customer resets.
  • Peer precedent: Greencore lifted its margin 110 basis points in one year through cost recovery, exits and automation (£15m of automation for £8m of savings); Cranswick's 7.9 percent came from integration Hilton will not do. Hilton's realistic ceiling is a margin per kilogram back to about 19p plus new-geography mix.
  • Congruence: every item sits inside the March 2026 framework; the target excludes seafood, so a seafood fix is upside to management's own target.

Judgement: the core growth engine has not decelerated over the last twelve months; it slowed sharply into FY2025 and is recovering, but it is still below its FY2024 rate. The input change force (portfolio repair and new geographies) is at its most intense, not slowing.

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the net impact is a business that, for the first time since FY2019, grows operating profit faster than inflation-adjusted revenue for four consecutive years, but slowly, from a trough, and with the growth back-loaded and concentrated in two new plants. Against FY2019 to FY2025 (revenue up 2.3 times on acquisitions and inflation, margin halved, EPS flat to down, free cash flow about zero on average), the forecast has revenue growing about 4 percent a year with little inflation, operating profit about 6.7 percent a year, EPS 8.4 percent a year from the FY2026 trough (only 3.4 percent a year from FY2025 to FY2028), and free cash flow turning from a large deficit to about £60m to £66m a year by FY2029 to FY2030. The quality of the improvement is mixed: of the £28m operating profit increase from FY2026 to FY2030, about 30 percent (£8m) comes from the existing estate at about volume growth plus a little nominal fee creep, about 40 percent (£12m) from Canada, Poland and NADEC (new plants absorbing fixed cost), and about 30 percent (£8m) from removing seafood losses (£4.5m) and retained overhead savings (£3.5m). The single line where the forecast differs most from the record is cash; the line where it differs least is the margin.

How the items in this set net against each other:

  • Operating leverage (P-01) and cost elimination (P-03) are the positives; together they add about £20m of operating profit by FY2030 (new plants £12m, seafood, overheads and the automation float £8.5m), against about £8m from the existing estate's volume and nominal fee creep.
  • Pricing (P-04) is neutral to profit and dominant in revenue, and its one negative (fee pressure at renewals) is a tail risk, not in the base.
  • Feedback loops (P-05) decide the shape: balancing loops act fast and cap the margin; the reinforcing partnership loop acts slowly and is what lifts FY2029 to FY2030.
  • Financial leverage and below-the-line items (P-02, P-08) are neutral to slightly positive for EPS over the window and strongly positive for cash after FY2027.
  • Track record (P-06) leans the FY2027 to FY2028 outcome down from our central case by a further 2 to 5 percent in risk terms, not in the central figures.

The pattern the Consumer should carry into the predictability test: the forecast's difference from the past rests on a small number of identifiable items (Canada's ramp, Poland's capacity, seafood, the tax rate, capital expenditure falling) rather than on a broad improvement in the existing business, which continues to earn a capped fee per kilogram. That makes the forecast checkable (each item has a date) and concentrated (two plants supply about 40 percent of the operating profit increase).

Memo topic 7, Scope for upgrades over 6 to 12 months: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

D3623rd of 35

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Guidance, consensus and the narrative reflect the repair and not yet the recovery's shape: they capture the exits, the capital expenditure peak, the cost-plus model's resilience and Canada's existence, and they do not capture the finance-charge step-up in FY2027, the recurrence of exceptional cash, the Australian cattle cycle's volume effect, the open-book cap on profit per kilogram or the contract evidence that lowers the anchor-loss tail. The gap between our assessment and what is already reflected is therefore favourable in the near term and for the outer years, and unfavourable in the middle: FY2026 is likely to come in a little above consensus on the guided tax rate and the upper half of the range (a small, mostly priced positive); FY2027 consensus (+9.9 percent EPS) is above our central case by about 4 percent and above where this management team is likely to set guidance in April 2027, so the most probable sequence over the next six months is a confirmation in January, a small FY2026 beat and a below-consensus FY2027 range in April, and a cut to FY2027 consensus of 3 to 6 percent led by Deutsche Bank, with a flat H1 FY27 print in September 2027 as the second test; FY2029 and beyond are, if anything, under-forecast (one broker; Canada and Poland not ramped), and the Canada lease and overrun sharing, the Woolworths term to 2033 and the leverage path to a capital return are unpriced positives for an 18-month-to-three-year view. The narrative is thin (two houses in the record; no investor commentary found), unanimously Buy and technically extended after a 60 percent rise, so the market's reaction to a conservative FY2027 range is the main share-price risk in the window, and it would be a reaction to consensus, not to the business. For the memo: the setup is not "conservative guidance, upgrades ahead" (topics #4 and #7) for FY2027; it is "honest guidance, consensus too high, outer years under-modelled", which belongs to a stock that is turning up with its thesis not yet broadened (topic #10), and it argues for a fair multiple near the five-year median rather than the ten-year bands.

I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

D4520th of 35

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement: FY2026 guidance (£66m to £71m) will almost certainly be met (about 90 percent) and is more likely to be confirmed in its upper half than raised; a technical nudge at the January 2027 update is possible (about 25 percent) if Christmas volumes and the Australian dollar hold, but a formal raise is unlikely because the team has already said its one raise was technical and prefers to beat. The event that matters is 7 April 2027, when FY2027 guidance is first set. On this team's record (a low bar in January 2026, held in March and May, raised once with the drivers named) and its incentives (80 percent of the annual bonus on adjusted profit before tax; long-term awards vesting from 3 percent EPS growth), the most likely form is a profit before tax range whose midpoint sits at or just below consensus's £74.0m (about 45 percent below, 40 percent in line, 15 percent above), because the framework ("mid-single-digit on average, not linear, excluding seafood") applied to a FY2026 base of about £99m excluding seafood gives about £104m of operating profit before seafood, about £102m to £103m after it, and about £72m to £73m of profit before tax on a finance charge of about £30m. Over the following events (the May 2027 AGM, the September 2027 interims, the January 2028 update, the April 2028 results) the probabilities for FY2027 guidance are: maintained about 55 percent; reduced about 30 percent (Canada's ramp, Australian volumes, a West re-allocation, rates); raised about 15 percent (Foppen re-approved or sold cleanly, Canada at Shore Capital's pace, currency). Secondarily, the mid-term framework will be maintained rather than raised or reduced (about 70 percent maintained; a raise would need a second Walmart site or a Polish project much larger than £30m; a reduction would need an anchor contract event); the one ambition that could be upgraded is the return-on-capital statement once FY2027 shows the recovery above 20 percent, and the one that could be quietly withdrawn is cash conversion at about 100 percent if exceptional cash persists.

Event by event, what drives each outcome:

  • January 2027 trading update (FY2026). Met: H1 delivered £32.8m; the range needs H2 of £33.2m to £38.2m against about £38m a year earlier on today's perimeter, with the Australian dollar, no airfreight and Seachill's cost-out as tailwinds and Ireland, Scotland and Foppen as headwinds; our £36.2m. Raised: only if the company chooses to say "top of the range", a signal rather than a number. Reduced: requires a West collapse at Christmas or a Foppen write-off presented as trading, each under 10 percent. The UK consumer backdrop has softened (Deutsche Bank cut Sainsbury's H2 like-for-like growth to 1.8 percent from 3.6 percent), but the anchor customers are fine: Tesco's H1 food like-for-like +2.4 percent, Woolworths' own-brand sales +8.5 percent in Q1 FY27.
  • 7 April 2027 (FY2026 results, FY2027 guidance, the dividend). FY2026 adjusted profit before tax most likely £68m to £70m; tax about 28 percent; EPS about 54p against consensus's 52.4p, a small beat that this team will present as in line. FY2027 guidance as above; the team's choice of a low midpoint is the main risk to the share price at this event even if the number is reachable, because consensus and the 100 percent Buy share are positioned for +9 percent profit before tax growth.
  • May 2027 AGM. Maintained (about 80 percent); a first comment on Canada's volumes and on NADEC.
  • September 2027 interims. The hinge. Our H1 FY27 profit before tax is about flat on £32.8m (Canada's H1 loss, the National Living Wage from April, a higher finance charge); consensus's +9 percent for the year implies a better first half. "Maintained" is most likely if Canada is in profit by the summer; "reduced" if Canada's H1 loss exceeds £2m to £3m or Australian volumes have turned; "raised" only with Foppen resolved.
  • January and April 2028. The FY2027 outturn and FY2028 guidance; by then the Australian cattle squeeze (slaughter -9 percent in 2028) is the external variable and Canada's bacon lines the internal one.

Why the company guides the way it does, and what that means for the gap between our view and guidance: the new team's style is to guide where it can beat and to say when a raise is technical; the auditor's control findings, the 2025 to 2026 Code breach and the remuneration structure all push toward caution; and management does not itself expect more than 8 percent EPS growth (its stretch hurdle). Our FY2026 view (the upper half) is therefore slightly above what guidance states, a favourable gap that is small and probably already in the price after a 60 percent rise from the January low. Our FY2027 view (about £71m of profit before tax) is below consensus and probably close to where guidance will be set, so guidance itself is unlikely to be the source of disappointment; consensus is.

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? D3520th of 35

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: FY2026 consensus EPS (52.4p) is more likely to rise than fall over the next two events (about 60 percent up by 2 to 4 percent, mainly tax and the upper half of the range; 30 percent held; 10 percent cut), and FY2027 consensus (57.6p median) is more likely to fall than rise over the next three events (about 55 percent cut by 3 to 6 percent; 30 percent held; 15 percent up), with FY2028 (62.6p) following FY2027 down by 2 to 4 percent and the single FY2029 estimate (66.6p) more likely to be raised than cut once Canada's ramp is visible. The reasons FY2027 falls: (1) the published median sits above two of the three visible brokers (Deutsche Bank 59.8p, Peel Hunt 54.9p, Shore Capital 54.0p; their median is 54.9p, so FactSet's 57.6p rests on an unnamed contributor at or above about 60p), which means the consensus is top-heavy and one cut moves it more than the arithmetic suggests; (2) no broker models the end of capitalised interest in 2027 (about £2.5m), a higher Bank Rate, or Canada's H1 loss explicitly, and Deutsche Bank's FY2027 carries a 5.5 percent Dalco uplift that compares with its own old number rather than with the FY2026 continuing base; (3) the company will probably set FY2027 guidance below consensus (I-01), and brokers at this company follow guidance (the January 2026 cut to £60m to £65m was matched within days by Shore Capital's 7 percent cut); (4) FY2027 consensus implies +9 percent profit before tax growth and +9.9 percent EPS growth against a framework of mid-single-digit operating profit growth excluding seafood and a finance charge that rises. The reasons it might not fall: a Foppen resolution (Deutsche Bank "expects a resolution of some sort"; Shore Capital expects Foppen to stop being a drag by H2 2027), Canada at Shore Capital's £1.5m rather than our £0.5m, and currency. Net breadth: with three to four contributors the breadth measure is coarse; I expect it to go from all-up (the three-month revisions are +8 percent with every visible broker raising) to about two of three down after April 2027, with Deutsche Bank the broker that moves, and the dashboard's revision-momentum score (15 now) to fall below zero by mid-2027, at which point the pinned B for sell-side momentum (28 October 2026) would be lost. Target prices (mean 795p; Deutsche Bank 850p, Peel Hunt 720p, Berenberg 810p) have risen 17 percent in three months and sit 11 percent above the price; they follow EPS with a lag, and Peel Hunt's 720p is already at the price.

  • FY2026: why up. Consensus profit before tax of £67.9m sits £0.6m below the guidance midpoint and implies a tax rate of about 29.7 percent against company guidance of about 28 percent and an H1 rate of 27.7 percent; at £69m and 28 percent, EPS is 54p, 3 percent above consensus. Shore Capital's and Peel Hunt's 51.2p are the lagging numbers; Deutsche Bank's 52.9p is nearer. The upgrade is small, technical and likely at the April 2027 results rather than in January; the market has paid for most of it already (the shares re-rated with the September raise).
  • FY2027: the arithmetic of the cut. Our 55.1p is 4.3 percent below the 57.6p median and almost exactly the median of the three visible brokers (54.9p). If Deutsche Bank moves from 59.8p toward 56p to 57p at the April results (its FY2027 profit before tax implies about £76m against guidance likely set at £72m to £74m) and the others hold, the published median moves to about 55p to 56p. That is a cut of 3 to 5 percent in the consensus series without any broker falling below where two already are. The Consumer should present FY2027 consensus as "three to four brokers, 54p to 60p, median 57.6p", not as a settled number.
  • FY2028 and beyond. Consensus has EPS +8.7 percent in FY2028 with an implied tax rate of 27.8 percent and capital expenditure of £58m (we carry 28.5 percent and £65m). The year contains Canada's first full year with bacon, Poland's build and the trough of Australian cattle supply; a FY2027 cut usually drags FY2028 with it. FY2029 (one broker, 66.6p) is below our 67.7p because we carry Canada and Poland ramping; if the April 2027 results confirm Canada commissioned, the outer years are more likely to rise as a second broker adds a FY2029 estimate.
  • What each broker assumes, as far as the notes show. Deutsche Bank (3 September 2026): FY2026 profit before tax £67.9m; tax 27.7 percent in H1; Canada and NADEC in the model from FY2027; "expects a resolution of some sort" at Foppen; a flat margin of about 2.1 to 2.3 percent over ten years in its discounted cash flow; target 850p. Shore Capital (3 September 2026): FY2026 £66.0m from £62.5m; Canada £65m of sales and £1.5m in FY2027 rising to over £5m by year three; bacon capital through 2027; Foppen not a drag beyond H2 2027; leverage peaking at 1.1x to December 2027 (low against our 1.4x); "not the territory for buybacks". Peel Hunt and Berenberg are not in the AlphaSense record (Peel Hunt's EBITDA basis includes right-of-use depreciation; Berenberg's 810p target is reported by Sharecast). None of the four models an explicit path for capitalised interest, the supply-chain finance cost, Australian volumes in 2027 to 2028 or IFRS 18.
  • Peer read-across for the direction of revisions. Cranswick's results in late November 2026 and Greencore's beat-and-raise record set the sector's expectation that contract makers beat; Hilton's own pattern is of meeting a low bar, and the sell-side at this company has learned to follow guidance rather than lead it. Woolworths' and Tesco's volume momentum supports the volume line; Ahold Delhaize's Dutch food deflation (-1.0 percent in August 2026) lowers West revenue without touching profit and could mislead a revenue-based revision.

Current multiple

C7610th of 36Memo topic 11, The fair multiple: the Rater's score1 answer
D-0755
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

C+5510th of 35

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the shares are overwhelmingly company-driven, and so is the multiple. Over the latest six-month window (7 May to 2 November 2026) the equal-weighted basket of 21 food-production peers explains 10 percent of Hilton's daily variance (correlation 0.31, beta 0.74); the stock returned +35.4 percent against the basket's +4.7 percent. Over 24 months the explained share has ranged from nil (November 2025 to February 2026: the profit warning, the chief executive's exit and the guidance cut, when the shares fell to 448p on company news alone) to 37 percent (July 2026, when the recovery coincided with a defensive rotation into staples), and has fallen back to 10 percent as the September results and the Dalco completion took over. The multiple tells the same story: the 24-month-forward price-to-earnings ratio went from 8.1x in January 2026 to 11.75x now on a re-rating the sector did not share (Cranswick at its 11th percentile of ten years, Greencore 30th, Bell 2nd), while Hilton's own 6th percentile of ten years says the re-rating has taken it from distressed to cheap and no further. For predictability this cuts both ways. Favourable: the multiple will respond to the dated company events the Consumer can model (Canada, Foppen, guidance), and the valuation floor is company-specific and already tested (8.1x with the chief executive gone). Unfavourable: no sector tailwind will carry the shares if delivery slips; the regime has turned against momentum (the Fitness for Fighting tilt moved to Underweight on 23 October 2026) and against small multi-part cases; and a stock that trades on its own news with a 100 percent Buy share, a relative-strength percentile of 87 and a price 25.7 percent above a 200-day average that has risen in only two of twelve months is exposed to its own disappointments without a cushion. The highest pairwise correlations are with the UK names (Associated British Foods 0.38, Premier Foods 0.34, Greencore 0.27, Cranswick 0.26), which says the residual co-movement is a UK small-cap and sterling factor rather than a protein or food-production factor; LDC, Savencia and Spadel are uncorrelated.

  • What the multiple is pricing. At 11.75x two-year-forward consensus (61.0p) the market pays near the five-year median (11.9x) for a business whose five-year history includes two warnings; the 2026 re-rating recovered the January collapse and no more. The multiple is company-driven in both directions: it fell on company news and rose on company news. The memo's fair-multiple topic (#11) should start from that fact rather than from the ten-year percentile bands (median 16.9x, 75th 19.8x) that belong to the compounder years; the guideline figure of 20.64x in the model summary sits above the ten-year 75th percentile for a stock in an early, unproven recovery, and I cannot reconcile it with anything in the three sets.
  • The industry channel that remains. The 37 percent reading of July 2026 shows the stock can trade as a staple when the market rotates defensively; with Consumer staples and Food production Overweight in the regime, a renewed rotation would lift Hilton with the sector but by less than a beta of 0.74 implies, because its own news dominates. A sector sell-off (a UK grocery price war, a food-safety event at a peer, a beef-price headline) would take 5 to 10 percent off the shares through this channel; the first set's 10 to 20 percent perception-risk estimate includes the company-specific crowding, which is the larger part.
  • For the model and the memo: the multiple's path over 18 months is a function of four company dates (January and April 2027, September 2027, spring 2028) and one external cycle (Australian cattle), not of the sector; the co-movement data give no reason to expect sector multiples to lift Hilton's, and some reason to expect the UK small-cap factor to add volatility after the 28 October 2026 Budget.
