Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

C5213th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: in this cohort (protein and chilled food makers for grocers), shares outperform when visible volume growth arrives under contract with falling margin volatility, or when a self-help programme lifts a troughed margin and returns cash; they underperform on acquisitions outside the core, on inflation spikes that expose pass-through lags, and on contract or food-safety shocks. Hilton fits the outperformance pattern on volume visibility (new partnerships) and portfolio repair, and does not fit it on margin expansion or cash return; it fits the underperformance pattern on inflation spikes, and the next spike (Australian beef, 2027 to 2028) is already visible.

  • Hilton's own history is the clearest precedent. In the 2010s it compounded by following grocers into new countries under long contracts (Australia, Central Europe, the Nordics); the shares traded at 17x to 20x (a pre-pandemic average of 20.3x; a five-year average of 17.1x quoted in 2026). It then diversified into seafood (Seachill 2017, Foppen), vegan (Dalco) and food service (Fairfax Meadow), and earned two profit warnings (2022, Q3 FY2025, the latter a 23 percent one-day fall), a de-rating to 9.8x in 2022 and a five-year low of 8.1x in January 2026. The strategy now returns to the 2010s formula. The pattern says the market pays for Hilton as a compounder only when new-geography volume is visibly adding to EPS; it pays a utility multiple when it is not.
  • Greencore (2023 to 2025) is the self-help precedent: exit of the US business (after a 2018 warning), exit of low-margin contracts, margin from 5.4 to 6.5 percent, return on invested capital from 11.5 to 15 percent, buybacks; then a transformational merger with Bakkavor and £80m of synergies by January 2029. The shares multiplied. Hilton has the exits but not the margin lever or the buyback.
  • Cranswick is the compounder precedent: capacity-led volume growth (about 10.5 percent sales growth a year over a decade), vertical integration, a margin rising for five consecutive half-years to 7.9 percent, 18.5 percent return on capital, 36 years of dividend growth, a premium multiple (14.8x now, its 11th percentile). Hilton cannot replicate the integration.
  • Underperformance precedents: Hilton 2022 and 2025 (inflation and seafood); Greencore 2018 (US volume); Cloetta's aborted factory (later reversed into a re-rating); Devro's closure; food-safety events that passed quickly at Cranswick (salmonella, 2022) and did not at Hilton (Foppen, ongoing since 2025).
  • Sector rules that apply (Assistant Portfolio Manager, October 2026): a contract maker earns on volume and factory efficiency; thin margins make small gains large; labour is the weak point; a falling commodity reaches a processor first as cash; "a share price that falls while its story strengthens can be an entry point" (that window, January to May 2026 at 447p to 540p, has passed: the shares are at 715.5p); UK food makers attract bids (Bakkavor, Devro), but Hilton's change-of-control clauses weaken that floor.
  • Fit for the next 18 months: the outperformance trigger is Canada visibly adding to EPS in FY2027 and FY2028 alongside a seafood fix; the underperformance trigger is an Australian margin squeeze or a customer sourcing shift. The base rate in the Wisdom Library ("Turnarounds take longer than you think") says to add time to the seafood fix.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

C-5215th of 35

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: on unchanged earnings, about 16 to 20 percent to a fair trough multiple and about 31 percent to the five-year low multiple; on trough earnings at a trough multiple, about 35 to 38 percent, which is back to the 52-week low. The dividend (about 4.9 percent) softens but does not change this.

  • Base: 715.5p on 29 October 2026; consensus 24-month-forward EPS 61.0p (dashboard); 11.7x.
  • Fair trough multiples on the record: the 2022 profit-warning de-rating to 9.8x (598p, -16 percent); the five-year 10th percentile of 9.4x (573p, -20 percent); Greencore today at 9.4x; the five-year low of 8.1x (494p, -31 percent) set in January 2026 with the chief executive gone and guidance cut.
  • Trough on trough: 24-month-forward EPS cut about 10 percent to about 55p (an Australian squeeze plus a Canada slip) at 8.1x to 8.5x gives 446p to 468p, -35 to -38 percent, close to the 52-week low of 447.7p.
  • Our judgement for the model of risk: the fair trough for an 18-month view is 9.4x to 9.8x on 24-month-forward earnings (about 570p to 600p); the severe case is about 450p to 490p.

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

D4620th of 35

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The narrative ahead is a dated staircase of company-delivered steps (Saudi Arabia, Poland, Canada, results, dividend cover), strong on near-term density and weak on novelty. The market has already paid for the repair (the shares are up about 60 percent from the January low, 30 percent in three months, to 11.7x 24-month-forward earnings, near the five-year median and at the 6th percentile of ten years), and every broker models the next steps. The novel, price-moving steps are three: a Foppen outcome (upside on re-approval, downside on a distressed sale), Canada's first-year profit relative to the cautious end of the range, and, further out, a second Walmart site or a first capital return that would reopen the question of whether Hilton can compound again. For the Consumer's purposes, the staircase gives the model dated checkpoints at which to test the operating assumptions: January 2027 (Christmas and Canada's start), April 2027 (FY2026 delivery and FY2027 guidance), September 2027 (Canada's first half and West volumes) and spring 2028 (Canada's first full year and seafood).

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–62

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the two sets are congruent in substance and jar in three places that matter, each of which the Consumer can now resolve. The first set's foundations (a contractually capped fee per kilogram, absolute buyer power, a repair team with modest ambition, risks that are sudden rather than gradual) and the second set's forecast (a slow, back-loaded recovery in which the existing estate grows at about volume, two new plants and the removal of seafood losses supply most of the increase, and cash improves more than earnings) describe the same business. The second set's four corrections to the first (Dalco already out of the FY2026 base; tax about 28 percent, not 29 to 30; no finance-cost gearing in FY2027; Foppen's cost reaching cash more than adjusted EPS) are all right, and the new evidence confirms the tax point directly (company guidance of about 28 percent for 2026; 27.7 percent in H1). The places where the sets jar or where new evidence changes an answer are: (1) Canada's contract terms, where the first set's expert testimony (Hilton bore the build inflation) is contradicted by the company's own September 2025 call ("both parties participate in managing budget overruns") and by Shore Capital's description of lease recovery under customer agreements, so the second set's flagged upside is better founded than its base; (2) the finance charge, where neither set saw the £1.6m per half of capitalised interest that reverses in FY2027, so the second set's FY2027 EPS was about 1.5p too high; (3) the largest unknown the first set named, the anchor contract dates, is now half closed: the Woolworths agreements are 15-year contracts from July 2018 running to 2033, the Tesco partnership was extended in early 2026 for an undisclosed term, and the Dutch and Danish renewals completed in late 2025, which lowers the probability the first set gave to a material anchor loss within three years. One further incongruence sits outside the sets: the model's guideline multiple of 20.64x is irreconcilable with both sets' valuation reasoning, and the Consumer should treat the summary's +95.5 percent return as a mechanical output, not a judgement.

Where the sets align, and why that matters for predictability:

  • The unit of account. Both sets model tonnes and profit per kilogram and treat revenue as pass-through; the AlphaSense driver report and Deutsche Bank's long-run model (a flat margin of about 2.1 to 2.3 percent for a decade) use the same frame. There is no competing view inside the work that margins can expand, which removes one source of forecast dispersion.
  • The shape. The first set's "trough in FY2026, turning, but the turn technical in the profit line" (C-06) and the second set's "past its earnings trough but not yet on a growth path" are the same reading; consensus's +9.9 percent FY2027 EPS rebound is the outlier, not either set.
  • The risk map. The first set's three sudden risks (customer re-allocation, food safety, Canada start-up) are the second set's bear-case ingredients (a further West stream lost, Foppen unresolved, Canada late). Nothing in the second set's forecast depends on an item the first set rated weak (pricing power 12, cuttable cost 40, out-execution 38).

Where they jar, and my resolution:

  1. Dalco and the self-help pool. The first set's "about £8m of losses to remove" was halved by the second set to £4m to £5m (seafood only). I agree with the second set; the brokers' notes confirm Dalco's £3.7m is inside the FY2026 guidance raise. Deutsche Bank's statement that the disposal lifts FY2027 EPS by 5.5 percent compares against its own pre-disposal FY2027, not against the FY2026 continuing base; the Consumer should not read it as a FY2027 growth driver.
  2. Tax. First set 29 to 30 percent; second set 28.0 then 28.5. The company guides about 28 percent for 2026; unused tax losses of £90.9m (£68.8m recognised) at end-2025 and Pillar Two charges of nil support a rate at or below 28.5 percent in the outer years, with the risk in mix (Australia at 30 percent is the largest pool). The second set is right.
  3. Supply-chain finance interest. The first set said it rises with meat prices; the second set said it had plateaued (£4.2m in H1 FY26 against £4.4m). The tax-and-finance report reconciles the two: the rate is the retailer's credit spread over a variable benchmark (Tesco's annual report), so the cost moves with Bank Rate and the ECB, and the scale moves with invoice values, so with meat prices. Both sets are partly right; the H1 fall reflects the disposals shrinking the base. The second set's flat £8.5m is reasonable at stable rates; a 50 basis-point rise adds about £0.75m on this component, inside the company's £2.1m total sensitivity.
  4. Canada's terms (the material one). First set: Hilton bears inflation, starts at a 10 to 15 percent sourcing disadvantage, and a 20 percent return needs £16m of profit. Second set: carried the cautious path (£0.5m rising to £7.5m) but flagged capital pass-through as a £5m to £10m upside. New evidence: the September 2025 call said both parties share overruns; Shore Capital says lease exposure is covered by customer agreements; the building lease runs 20 years with rent indexation capped at 2.25 percent; Ontario granted C$1.5m; Shore Capital budgets start-up costs at £1m to £2m. Against this, Walmart ranks only fifth or sixth in Canadian grocery and Brantford serves Eastern Canada only (Shore Capital), and the bacon lines slip to late 2027. My resolution: the second set's base path stands for the next 18 months (the ramp, not the contract, decides FY2027), but the probability that Canada reaches its hurdle by 2029 to 2030 is higher than the second set implied, and the upside is more likely to show in FY2029 than in FY2027.
  5. Walmart replication. The first set treated a second Walmart site as "the largest optional upside". The Canada report finds no dated evidence of any plan, and documents Walmart's own-and-operate model in the United States (three milk plants at over $350m each; case-ready beef plants at Thomasville, Georgia, in 2020 and Olathe, Kansas, in 2025, sourcing from Sustainable Beef LLC). That cuts two ways: Walmart's wish for case-ready capacity is real, but its United States habit is to own it. The option is weaker than the first set said, and it carries a long-dated risk that Walmart Canada insources at the end of the ten-year term. For the 18-month window neither matters; for the memo's lifecycle judgement (#10) it does.
  6. Anchor contract dates. The Woolworths term to 2033 and the 2026 Tesco extension lower the first set's 5 to 10 percent three-year probability of a material anchor loss to, I judge, 3 to 5 percent, concentrated in Tesco (term undisclosed) and in partial re-allocations of the Scottish-beef kind, which the second set already carries at about 1 percent of West volume a year. One caution on the contracts report: its item on Woolworths acquiring In2food (October 2026) comes from a South African source that pairs it with Shoprite, so it almost certainly concerns Woolworths Holdings of South Africa, not Hilton's partner Woolworths Group of Australia; the Consumer should not use it.

The two incongruences to carry into the memo: the second set's FY2027 EPS is about 1.5p too high for the capitalised-interest reversal (corrected above); and the sets' scores jar more than their reasoning (internal change 66 and the staircase 60 against economic strength 40, value chain 40 and risks 37). The second set reconciles them correctly as a strong input producing a modest output, and that is the frame to keep.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

D4218th of 35

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: thinly. The sell-side narrative on Hilton is two houses deep in the research record (Deutsche Bank and Shore Capital; Peel Hunt and Berenberg are not held by AlphaSense), and it articulates well the dynamics that are already in the price: the cost-plus model's resilience, the Dalco and Fairfax Meadow exits, the 2026 capital expenditure peak and net debt, the technical nature of the September raise, Foppen as an undated drag, and Canada as a "growth supercharger" with a 20 percent return hurdle. It does not articulate, or mentions in one line without modelling, the dynamics that will move FY2027 and FY2028 earnings against those expectations: the open-book reset as a cap on profit per kilogram (Deutsche Bank assumes a flat margin for a decade without saying why); the Walmart contract's capital and lease terms (nobody); the end of capitalised interest and the finance-cost path through rising rates (nobody); the adjusted tax path beyond FY2026 (flat assumptions only); the recurrence of "exceptional" transformation and Foppen cash (both houses add everything back, while Deloitte flagged the classification as a key audit matter with a fraud-and-bias risk); IFRS 18 (neither house has modelled it); Australian cattle availability in 2027 to 2028 (in Morgan Stanley's protein research and in Woolworths' and NH Foods' coverage, not in Hilton's); retailers' migration of volume to integrated processors (Tesco's Scottish beef is reported; Tesco's Foyle and Kepak contracts and the CMA's ABP and Dovecote Park review are not connected to Hilton by anyone); the £150m of customer-provided supply-chain finance (mentioned as cheap funding, never as off-balance-sheet leverage or a rate-sensitive cost); and the leverage path toward a capital return (Shore Capital: "not the territory for buybacks", nothing on timing). Investor commentary is effectively absent from the record: no fund-manager letter, forum thread or social-media commentary on Hilton surfaced, which fits a £650m company with about $2.7m of daily turnover, a passive register (the co-founders at 4.7 and 3.2 percent; Western Standard's 5.0 percent since July 2026 the only new concentrated holder; no activist) and a 100 percent Buy share. The investor narrative, as far as price behaviour and the dashboard reveal it, is "repair done, cheap, recovery coming", which is the first set's "Major Corporate Change" input without the second set's modest-output conclusion. For the next 18 months the well-known dynamics are the positives of the staircase; the unknown ones are three negatives (the finance charge, Australian volumes, recurring exceptional cash) and two positives (the FY2026 tax rate; Canada's lease and overrun sharing). Secondarily, for 18 months to three years out, the narrative holds Canada's 20 percent return as management states it and does not question the sourcing gap, Walmart's Canadian market rank or Walmart's United States habit of owning case-ready plants; nor does it hold the open-book cap, so the market's long-run expectation of margin is probably above both sets'.

Dynamic by dynamic, who says what:

  • Widely articulated (both houses, since 2024 to 2025): cost-plus pass-through protecting cash margin (Deutsche Bank's initiation of 30 April 2024; Shore Capital throughout); the portfolio exits and their profit effect (Shore Capital, 31 July 2026: Dalco's removal worth about 5 percent of profit before tax); the capital expenditure peak of about £100m and net bank debt rising in H2 FY26 (both, 3 September 2026); the technical nature of the FY2026 raise (Shore Capital's own words); Canada's timetable and bacon lines (both); seafood as a drag with "all options" open (both); the dividend-cover rebuild (Shore Capital).
  • Articulated by one house, briefly: the tax rate (Deutsche Bank notes 27.7 percent in H1 and a higher rate than prior years; no path); the supply-chain finance cost advantage of 0.5 to 1.5 points (Shore Capital, April 2025); Walmart's Canadian market rank and the Eastern-Canada scope (Shore Capital, April 2025); the sourcing gap of 10 to 15 percent a pound (an expert call of December 2025, not a broker); leverage peaking at 1.1x (Shore Capital, May 2026).
  • Articulated by no covering house: the reset mechanism as a structural cap (the expert record has it; Deutsche Bank's flat-margin assumption embeds it without stating it); the Walmart contract's allocation of the £20m overrun and of the £5.9m-a-year lease (Companies House has the lease; Shore Capital's 2024 note has the principle; no note applies it to Brantford); capitalised interest and its reversal; IFRS 18's effect on the presentation of adjusted profit; the recurrence of about £10m a year of transformation cash as a structural cost; Australian cattle availability as a volume risk to the East in 2027 to 2028; Tesco's integration signals as a volume risk to Hilton; the Woolworths contract term to 2033 (public since 2018, never cited as a support); the timing of a capital return at a 1.0x floor.
  • Investor narrative and positioning. The price has risen 60 percent from the January low and 35 percent in six months against the sector's 5 percent; relative strength is at the 87th percentile; technical momentum is pinned B and sell-side momentum B; the 200-day average has risen in only two of twelve months, an early uptrend with little institutional confirmation. There is no evidence of investor commentary that goes beyond the sell-side's; the first set's finding that the register is passive stands. The narrative is sell-side-led, and the sell-side is two houses and a house broker.
  • Peers' narratives, for contrast. Greencore's is a synergy and consolidation story with a disclosed £80m target and a beat-and-raise record; Cranswick's a compounder story with capacity-led volume and integration; Bell's a stable pass-through story; Maple Leaf's a start-up-cost-unwind story with a 13 percent EBITDA margin; Premier Foods' a deleveraging and distribution story. Hilton's is the only one without a numerical self-help target or a margin lever, and the sell-side fills the gap with "growth supercharger" language about Canada that the company has not quantified.

Each layer's own material

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 30 October 2026, 00:10 to 00:29 UK, Opus, richie login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Hilton Food Group (HFG-GB): first set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Written 29 October 2026 at a share price of 715.5p (dashboard, 29 October 2026). Money in pounds sterling; per-share figures in pence.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Orientation for the Consumer: six anchors to carry into the model

These are not answers to a question; they are the frame every answer below relies on, set out once so the Consumer does not have to rebuild them.

  1. Revenue is the wrong unit; model tonnes and profit per kilogram. Raw meat is about 80 percent of the packed cost and over 90 percent of raw-material cost passes through to the retailer, so reported revenue moves with cattle and pig prices, not with the business. H1 FY26 revenue grew 15.3 percent (11.5 percent at constant currency) on volume growth of 2.1 percent. The unit that carries the earnings is adjusted operating profit per kilogram packed: about 19.0p in FY2024, 18.2p in FY2025 (£95.1m on 523,379 tonnes), 18.7p in H1 FY25 and 17.7p in H1 FY26 (£45.8m on 258,729 tonnes). Our arithmetic shows FactSet consensus operating profit (£93.1m, £99.6m, £107m for FY2026 to FY2028) implies about 17.5p, 18.3p and 19.1p a kilogram if volumes grow 2 to 3 percent a year (the brokers' operating profit basis may differ slightly from the company's): that is, consensus assumes profit per kilogram recovers to its FY2024 level by FY2028.
  2. The business underneath is a £0.77bn value-added business earning about 12 percent on it. FY2025 revenue of £4,214.6m less raw proteins and consumables of £3,443.5m leaves about £771m of value added (our derivation), out of which labour costs £295.9m and the adjusted operating profit is £95.1m. A 1p-per-kilogram change on about 530,000 tonnes is about £5.3m of operating profit, about 4p or 8 percent of earnings per share; a 0.5-percentage-point change in the revenue margin is about £23m, a third of earnings per share. Percentage margins are therefore a misleading signal: a fall in beef prices raises the percentage margin with no change in cash profit, as happened at Cranswick when pig prices fell 15 percent.
  3. Earnings per share are geared below operating profit. On consensus, net finance cost absorbs about £25m to £27m of a £93m to £107m operating profit (about 27 percent), the adjusted tax rate is about 28 to 30 percent (29.8 percent in FY2025, 27.7 percent in H1 FY26), minorities are about £1m, and the share count is about 90m rising slowly from incentive awards. Every £2m of extra finance cost is about 3 percent of earnings per share.
  4. Fix the perimeter before computing growth. Continuing operations now exclude Fairfax Meadow, Foods Connected and Dalco. The FY2025 adjusted profit before tax of £73.2m includes discontinued units; continuing was £69.0m as reported in March 2026, but that still carried Dalco's FY2025 loss of slightly over £4m (Shore Capital). On today's perimeter FY2025 is therefore about £73m (our reconstruction), H1 FY25 is £34.6m and H2 FY25 about £38m. The raised FY2026 guidance of £66m to £71m is therefore still a decline of about 3 to 10 percent on a like-for-like perimeter, and implies an H2 FY26 of £33m to £38m against about £38m a year earlier.
  5. Four bases exist for earnings per share and two for net debt; do not mix them. Company adjusted EPS was 56.0p in FY2025 (brokers' basis 55.7p; FactSet adjusted diluted 60.3p; reported basic 53.3p). Company net bank debt excludes £198.1m of leases (£126.7m at FY2025, £194.4m at H1 FY26, 1.4x); FactSet's net debt includes them (3.3x on its reported EBITDA). Free cash flow for FY2025 ranges from minus £31m (FactSet) to plus £53.6m (company, before growth capital) across sources; the Consumer should fix one definition: operating cash flow after leases and all capital expenditure, before disposals.
  6. Consensus is three brokers (Deutsche Bank, Peel Hunt, Shore Capital in FactSet; Berenberg publishes outside it), and FY2029 is one broker. Peel Hunt's EBITDA (£165m to £188m) is on a different basis from the other two (£140m to £162m), so read operating profit (EBIT), not EBITDA, as the consensus line.

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: External change forces · Group: Internal change forces · Group: Industry and Value Chain · Group: Company - Competitive advantage · Group: Company - Economic strength · Group: Company - Management · Group: Risks · Group: Guidance · Group: Sell-side estimates · Group: Stock market narrative

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Hilton Food Group plc (HFG-GB): an international secondary packer of retail meat, seafood and fresh prepared food for leading grocers under long-term open-book cost-plus or fee-per-kilogram partnerships, reported in an East region (Australia, New Zealand, Central Europe, the NADEC joint venture in Saudi Arabia) and a West region (UK, Ireland, Netherlands, Sweden, Denmark, Portugal, the new Walmart Canada plant), with the seafood businesses Seachill and Foppen held outside both regions.

Request: T-P1

Peers: Cranswick (listed UK pork and poultry processor using cost-of-production contracts and heavy automation; the nearest UK comparator on plant economics); Greencore (listed UK own-label chilled convenience contract maker, now combined with Bakkavor; discloses labour-cost recovery and synergy delivery); LDC (listed French integrated poultry processor running the NIAGARA plant-modernisation programme; a precedent for capacity start-up costs); Bell Food Group (listed Swiss meat processor that passes raw material through one-to-one; a close cost-structure analogue); Scandi Standard (listed Nordic chicken processor; plant-level margin and utilisation disclosure); Maple Leaf Foods (listed Canadian protein processor; precedent for greenfield plant start-up costs in Ontario); ABP Food Group, Kepak and Dawn Meats (private Irish and UK beef processors moving into retail packing; the direct competitors for Hilton's UK and Irish volume). Inputs: What is the split of Hilton's cost base between raw materials passed through, labour, energy, packaging, depreciation, logistics and central overheads, and which of these are fixed at plant level in the short run? How are processing fees set in each main contract (open-book cost-plus in the UK, fee per kilogram in Australia, others), and how often are they reset? What happened to segment margins and profit when volumes fell at Seachill (UK seafood volumes down 6.8 percent in FY2025, company annual report) and when Hilton stopped supplying Tesco's Scottish stores with beef in H1 2026 (interim results, 3 September 2026)? What happened when volumes rose sharply (Seachill volumes up over 17 percent in H2 2024, company commentary)? Under open-book audit, what share of savings from automation, machine-learning line control and yield improvement does Hilton keep, and for how long before they are passed back at the next reset? What has the "One Hilton" transformation programme cost (£4.6m of transformation costs in H1 2026, interim results) and what savings has it targeted and delivered? What did the Seachill cost-reduction programme deliver, and which Foppen costs (airfreight, production inefficiencies of £3.0m in H1 2026, company disclosure) end if the US Food and Drug Administration re-approves the Greek plant or if Foppen is sold? For the Walmart Canada plant in Brantford, Ontario (budget raised from £60m to £80m; launch January 2027; bacon lines added later in 2027, company disclosures), what are the expected start-up costs and ramp profile, does the contract pass capital or interest costs through as the Australian contracts reportedly do, and how did comparable greenfield protein plants ramp? How much UK National Living Wage and employer National Insurance inflation have cost-plus contract makers recovered, and with what delay? What did peer cost programmes deliver against promise: Greencore's Bakkavor synergies (£80m targeted by January 2029, company), Cranswick's automation (headcount and line-speed gains at Preston, company), Cloetta's "One Cloetta" savings (SEK 60m to 70m, company)? Is the interest on customer-provided supply-chain finance (reported by Shore Capital as £9.6m in FY2025 against £4.5m in FY2024) a cost that rises with raw-material prices and working-capital values? Focus: How much of any change in volume, and of any cost saving, actually reaches Hilton's operating profit, and for how long, under open-book cost-plus contracts that are reset with the customer; and how quickly the new Canadian plant can move from start-up to its stated return hurdle given that Hilton bore the build-cost increase.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Cranswick (listed; cost-of-production pricing reset quarterly with no lag on feed and utilities; the benchmark for pass-through speed in UK protein); Greencore (listed; reports about 75 percent of raw ingredient spend under automatic cost models and labour harder to pass through); LDC (listed; French EGalim indexation passes grain costs to retailers, a regulated comparator); Bell Food Group (listed; passes raw material on one-to-one with a slight delay); Maple Leaf Foods (listed; Canadian pricing environment for Hilton's new Walmart plant); Almarai (listed Saudi food group; pricing and cost environment for the NADEC venture); ABP Food Group, Kepak and Dunbia (private; integrated beef processors competing for Tesco and Irish retail volume). Operating conditions: rapid raw-material inflation (beef in 2025 and 2026; white fish after quota cuts); raw-material deflation (Australian prices in 2023 and 2024; European pork oversupply); the loss or re-allocation of a volume stream by an anchor customer (Tesco's move to Scottish beef for Scottish stores in 2026); consumer trade-down within beef (steak to mince, lighter packs); contract renewal or re-tender; UK labour and energy cost inflation; a new plant serving a single anchor customer (Walmart Canada); and currency translation of Australian profits. Inputs: What are the term, renewal dates and pricing mechanics of Hilton's main agreements: Tesco in the UK and Ireland (described by former employees as an open-book model with processing costs fixed annually), Woolworths in Australia and New Zealand (fee per kilogram, with capital costs reportedly passed through), Albert Heijn, ICA, Coop Danmark and Salling, Żabka, Walmart Canada and NADEC? When do the Tesco UK and Woolworths agreements next renew, and what do they contain on benchmarking, volume guarantees and the right to source elsewhere? How did Hilton's margins behave in the 2022 and 2025 inflation spikes and recover in 2023 and 2024, and how long did the lag last? Are labour, energy, packaging and financing costs recovered, and how quickly? What happened to Hilton's profit when Australian raw-material prices fell in 2023 and 2024? How large is Tesco within Hilton's UK sales (expert calls cite 85 to 90 percent), and how often does Tesco move volume between suppliers (expert calls cite 5 to 7 percent tactical shifts)? What do Tesco, Woolworths, Ahold Delhaize and Walmart say publicly about own-label sourcing, supplier consolidation and insourcing of meat processing? What do Lidl Ireland's price-gap policy (Irish Times, 26 September 2025) and the Dutch promotional pull-back imply for Hilton's West volumes? How did Seachill price white fish to Tesco through the haddock and cod spikes, and how is Foppen's US salmon business priced? How are Hilton's processing fees likely to behave as Australian cattle availability falls in 2027 and 2028 (Morgan Stanley, 19 August and 2 September 2026)? Focus: Whether Hilton can hold its processing fee per kilogram across inflation, deflation, volume re-allocation and contract renewal, and how long the lags last in each; above all, the renewal timetable, terms and competitive threat for the Tesco UK and Woolworths agreements.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: Hilton's earnings path depends on whether open-book resets pull its margin per kilogram back to a contractual norm (a balancing loop) or whether its record of new partnerships, filled plants and automation compounds (reinforcing loops). Peers: Cranswick (listed; a reinforcing loop of reinvestment, vertical integration and share gain over a decade); Greencore (listed; consolidation and synergy loop after Bakkavor); LDC (listed; capacity and upstream-integration loop); Bell Food Group (listed; contract maker for Swiss grocers Coop and others); Scandi Standard (listed; Nordic chicken, utilisation loop); ABP Food Group and Kepak (private; integrated processors whose captive raw material may draw retailers away from independent packers). Inputs: Open-book audit and annual fee resets returning efficiency gains to the retailer, so that margins revert after both savings and cost shocks; raw-material inflation raising retail prices, triggering consumer trade-down and lower volumes, which raises cost per kilogram on a fixed plant base; raw-material inflation raising the value of inventory and receivables, which raises interest on customer-provided supply-chain finance; a customer's volume re-allocation (Tesco's Scottish beef in 2026) lowering utilisation and weakening Hilton's cost position at the next tender; a record of successful single-retailer partnerships (Australia, Europe) attracting new partners (Walmart Canada; NADEC; Żabka into Romania) and Walmart's reported wish to replicate the dedicated-plant model elsewhere in North America; secondary customers (Salling, Netto, Dunnes Stores, Burger King in Sweden) filling existing plants and lowering unit costs; retailers' post-pandemic preference for suppliers with captive raw material, and primary processors moving into packing; automation and technology rolled site by site across the group. Focus: Which of these loops is strongest over the next 18 months to four years: whether Hilton's margin per kilogram is held to a contractual norm by resets, and whether the new-partnership loop (especially a second Walmart site) is real and dated.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Net finance cost, tax and minorities absorb a large share of Hilton's thin operating profit, so its earnings per share depend heavily on below-operating-profit items, currency translation and a few discrete projects whose sizes are not guided. Peers: Cranswick (listed; UK tax and finance-cost profile for a protein processor); Greencore (listed; UK contract maker with acquisition-related finance cost and leases); LDC (listed; French processor with net cash, a contrast in financing); Bell Food Group (listed; Swiss contract maker); Cloetta (listed; a Nordic food maker whose leverage fell sharply, a contrast in capital allocation). Inputs: The adjusted effective tax rate (29.8 percent in FY2025 against 24.9 percent in FY2024, attributed to capital-allowance true-ups, company annual report; 27.7 percent in H1 2026, interim results) and its likely level by country mix; the composition of net finance cost between bank debt, lease interest and customer-provided supply-chain finance, and its sensitivity to Bank of England and Reserve Bank of Australia rates and to meat prices (a 50 basis-point rise cited as about £2.1m, company annual report); net bank debt and leverage through the 2026 capital-expenditure peak (£194.4m and 1.4 times at June 2026, interim results) and the effect of the tactical frozen-inventory purchase in H2 2026; capital expenditure for 2027 and 2028 including the Polish expansion (up to £30m, possibly larger, company) and the Canadian bacon lines (later in 2027), against management's core range of £45m to £55m; share count and dilution from July 2026 long-term incentive awards; minority interests; the translation effect of the Australian and New Zealand dollars (36.78 percent of revenue in FY2025, derived from the FY2025 annual report); the Walmart Canada profit contribution from 2027 to 2029 (Shore Capital models about £1.5m on £65m of sales in 2027; management guides a small low-single-digit contribution in 2027 and full benefits in 2029); the NADEC share of profit (Deutsche Bank estimates about £1.3m of net income by year three); the profit path of Seachill and Foppen and the US re-approval timeline; the earnings effect of the Fairfax Meadow and Dalco disposals; recurring adjusting items (transformation and restructuring) and the effect of IFRS 18 from 2027; the dividend policy (progressive, cover rebuilt toward 2.0 times). Focus: Which of tax, finance cost, currency, share count, Canada, NADEC and seafood will move earnings per share most between 2026 and 2029, and where management guidance and the published forecasts of Deutsche Bank, Peel Hunt, Shore Capital and Berenberg differ on each.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 31 October 2026, 09:53 to 10:12 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Hilton Food Group (HFG-GB): second set of questions, the operating and financial model

Written 31 October 2026 at a share price of 715.5p (dashboard, 29 October 2026). Money in pounds sterling millions; per-share figures in pence unless stated. "Operating profit" means the company's adjusted operating profit (EBIT) on the continuing perimeter (excluding Fairfax Meadow, Foods Connected and Dalco). "EBITDA" in this document is on the company's IFRS 16 basis (adjusted operating profit plus all depreciation, including right-of-use depreciation, but excluding amortisation of acquired intangibles); it is about £24m higher than the brokers' EBITDA, which excludes right-of-use depreciation (see the bridge in the forecast inputs).

"Free cash flow, our strict definition" is operating cash flow after cash interest (including lease interest), tax, lease principal repayments, all capital expenditure (core and growth), working-capital movement and exceptional (restructuring, transformation and Foppen) cash costs; before dividends, acquisitions and disposals. This is the definition the first set recommended (orientation note 5); it is lower than FactSet's free cash flow definition (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capital expenditure) mainly by the lease principal, about £25m to £29m a year, and by capital expenditure on intangible assets; exceptional cash is in both.

Five places where this set changes the picture the first set handed over:

  1. Dalco's removal does not lift FY2027 on the continuing perimeter. The first set (C-15, C-21 and the Internal change forces group judgement) told the Consumer to "carry Dalco's removal into FY2027". We disagree: Dalco was moved to discontinued operations in the H1 FY26 accounts, and the September 2026 guidance raise to £66m to £71m was largely its exclusion. Its loss of about £3.7m to £4m a year is therefore already absent from the FY2026 continuing base, so it adds nothing to FY2027 growth. The "about £8m of losses to remove" from a FY2026 base is therefore really about £4m to £5m (seafood only). This halves the self-help pool that the first set and the September memo rely on for FY2027 to FY2028 growth.
  2. The tax rate is the cleanest identifiable source of FY2026 upside against consensus. Consensus EPS (52.4p) on consensus profit before tax (£67.9m) implies tax and minorities of about 30 percent, which is FY2025's 29.8 percent rate carried forward. The H1 FY26 rate was 27.7 percent and the company guides about 28 percent for the full year (AlphaSense EPS-bridge report, citing the interim results; to be verified against the interim text). At 28 percent and £69m of profit before tax, FY2026 EPS is 54.0p, 3 percent above consensus. The first set carried 29 to 30 percent (C-54, Economic strength group judgement); we think that is wrong for FY2026 and too high for the outer years by about one point.
  3. The FY2027 shape is flatter than consensus. Consensus has EPS falling 5.9 percent in FY2026 and rising 9.9 percent in FY2027. Part of that rebound is an artefact of brokers' too-high FY2026 tax rate. On our numbers FY2026 EPS falls 3.6 percent and FY2027 rises 4.8 percent, because finance cost rises about £1.5m in FY2027 (net debt does not fall, Bank Rate rises), Canada contributes little net of start-up costs, the Australian cattle squeeze begins, and there is no Dalco benefit. Our FY2027 EPS (56.6p) is 1.7 percent below consensus (57.6p), inside the first set's 55p to 58p range.
  4. Foppen's cost-out reaches cash and statutory profit far more than it reaches adjusted EPS. Of Foppen's H1 FY26 costs, £7.0m sat in adjusting items (including £2.1m of airfreight, ended in April 2026, and £3.0m of production inefficiencies) and only £2.2m in adjusted operating profit. A Foppen fix or sale therefore moves adjusted EPS by about 3p to 4p at most but moves free cash flow and statutory profit by £10m to £15m a year. The adjusted-EPS case for seafood is small; the cash case is not.
  5. Free cash flow is the line where the forecasts and the record most disagree. On the strict definition, FY2026 free cash flow is about minus £32m and FY2027 about plus £20m, against consensus FactSet-basis free cash flow of £10.6m and £45.0m. On FactSet's own definition our FY2027 figure (about £46m) matches consensus; the difference is the lease principal (about £26m), real cash that the FactSet line omits. FY2026 is lower than consensus on either definition because we carry the H2 frozen-meat purchase and about £20m of exceptional cash. The dividend (about £32m a year) is not covered by strict free cash flow until FY2028.

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Conventions used in Parts 1 to 3 (read first).

  • EBITDA basis: the company's adjusted operating profit (continuing operations: excluding Fairfax Meadow, Foods Connected and Dalco) plus all depreciation, including right-of-use (IFRS 16) depreciation, excluding amortisation of acquired intangibles. EBITDA less the depreciation and amortisation line equals our adjusted operating profit: £95.5m, £101.0m, £108.0m, £116.5m, £123.9m. This EBITDA is about £24m a year higher than the brokers' EBITDA (see the bridge below).
  • Revenue base: FY2026 growth of 9.6 percent is measured on FactSet's FY2025 revenue of £4,214.6m (the figure in the model's history), giving FY2026 revenue of about £4,619m. FY2025 restated for Dalco (moved to discontinued operations in the H1 FY26 accounts) is about £4,108m on our derivation (FactSet's H1 FY25 £1,986m plus H2 FY25 £2,122m), on which FY2026 growth is about 12.5 percent. If the model's FY2025 base is the restated £4,108m, the FY2026 input should be 12.4 percent to reach the same £4,619m. Absolute revenue: £4,619m, £4,790m, £4,991m, £5,191m, £5,373m.
  • Net financial items: the company's adjusted net finance cost, including lease interest (about £7.6m a year), bank interest and interest on customer-provided supply-chain finance (about £8.5m a year), net of interest income.
  • Lease payments: the principal element only. Lease interest is already inside net financial items; entering total lease cash would double count it.
  • Dividend per share: in pounds (0.350 = 35.0p), as FactSet holds UK per-share figures.
  • Not in the inputs, but in our cross-check: minorities of £1.0m, £1.0m, £1.1m, £1.2m and £1.3m (the model's EPS will be about 1.1p higher than ours for this reason), and exceptional cash costs (restructuring, transformation, Foppen disruption) of £20m, £12m, £8m, £6m and £5m (the model's free cash flow will be higher than ours by these amounts).
  • NADEC (49 percent Saudi joint venture, equity-accounted): its share of profit (£0, £0, £0.7m, £1.3m, £1.5m) is included in EBITDA and operating profit for simplicity; it is non-cash unless distributed.

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

The company reports East (Australia, New Zealand, Central Europe including Poland's fresh prepared food, and the NADEC joint venture), West (UK, Ireland, Netherlands, Sweden, Denmark, Portugal and, from January 2027, Walmart Canada) and the seafood businesses (Seachill and Foppen) separately; FactSet holds only one segment. H1 FY26 (company, interim results 3 September 2026): East revenue £1,014.9m (+21.6 percent reported, +15.3 percent at constant currency), adjusted operating profit about £24.9m (2.45 percent margin), volume +3.2 percent; West revenue £1,274.2m (+10.6 percent, including seafood on our reading), West core meat adjusted operating profit £28.0m (-20.5 percent; 2.8 percent margin on about £1,000m of core meat revenue), volume +0.6 percent; seafood adjusted operating loss £2.2m (from a £2.8m profit); central costs about £4.9m (our derivation). Our forecast by segment (adjusted operating profit, £m; derived, the company does not guide segments):

East includes Poland's new capacity (£0.8m, £2.0m, £3.0m from FY2028) and NADEC; FY2028 East carries a £1.5m probability-weighted drag from the Australian cattle squeeze. Central costs fall in FY2028 on "One Hilton" savings and rise thereafter with inflation.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Volume: existing estate about +2 percent a year (East about 3 percent; West about 1 percent after an allowance of about 1 percent a year for re-allocation to integrated processors, as Tesco's Scottish beef in H1 FY26); Canada adds about 8 thousand tonnes in FY2027 rising with bacon; Poland's capacity from about 2028. Group tonnes about 534k, 552k, 571k, 589k, 604k (FY2025 523k).
  • Profit per kilogram: the existing estate is held to a contractual norm by open-book resets: 18.5p (FY2026), 18.75p, 18.85p, 19.0p, 19.2p (about 1 percent a year of nominal creep, below conversion-cost inflation). The group figure rises from 17.9p to 20.5p only through mix (Canada, Poland, seafood recovery).
  • Price: raw materials are passed through; price and mix add about 7 points to FY2026 revenue growth, about 0.5 points in FY2027 and about 1 point a year thereafter. Price contributes nothing to profit except the lagged recovery of conversion-cost inflation (about £1m to £2m in FY2027).
  • Operating leverage: new plants carry fixed cost before volume: Canada loses money in H1 FY27 and earns £0.5m in FY2027, rising to £7.5m by FY2030 (probability-weighted for a 20 to 30 percent chance of a slip; Shore Capital models £1.5m in FY2027 and over £5m by year three). The 20 percent hurdle management claims would need £16m to £19m; we flag that as upside dependent on contract terms we cannot see.
  • Cost elimination: seafood losses fall from £3.5m to a £1m profit (+£4.5m); retained "One Hilton" overhead savings +£3.5m by FY2030; automation savings in pass-through plants are a float of £1m to £2m returned at resets. Dalco adds nothing to FY2027: it is already outside the FY2026 continuing base.
  • Depreciation: about £72m in FY2026 on the continuing IFRS 16 basis (H1 FY26 about £36m), rising with Canada (about £4m a year from FY2027) and Poland.
  • Finance cost: rises in FY2027 (average net bank debt about £26m higher, Bank Rate about 4.25 percent by mid-2027), then falls as net bank debt falls from about £204m (FY2027) to about £133m (FY2030).
  • Tax: 28.0 percent in FY2026 (company guidance about 28 percent, H1 27.7 percent), 28.5 percent thereafter (Australia, the largest profit pool, at 30 percent; risk of true-ups).
  • Capital expenditure: about £100m in FY2026 (guided); £80m in FY2027 (core about £50m, Poland about £20m, Canada bacon about £10m) and £65m in FY2028, both above consensus (£70m and £58m); £55m from FY2029 (core range £45m to £55m).
  • Working capital: year-end FY2025 0.2 percent of revenue (FactSet, £9.3m); FY2026 0.4 percent after the H2 frozen-meat purchase for Christmas 2026 and Easter 2027; normalising to 0.2 percent and then nil as that stock unwinds (FY2023 and FY2024 year-ends were about -1.5 percent; we do not assume a return there).
  • Acquisitions less disposals: FY2026 Dalco proceeds +£5.4m (completed 9 October 2026) less the NADEC equity and other small items (about -£6.4m, of which £3.1m in H1). No acquisitions or disposals thereafter in the base; a Foppen sale is a possible event not modelled.
  • Dividend: progressive and slow while cover rebuilds toward 2.0x: 35.0p (held), 35.5p, 36.5p, 38.0p, 40.0p.
  • Shares: about 90.0m at FY2025, rising 0.2m to 0.3m a year from incentive awards; no buyback (leverage reaches 1.0x only in FY2029).

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

  • FY2026 adjusted profit before tax £66m to £71m (raised 3 September 2026): ours £69.0m (operating profit £95.5m less net financial items £26.5m), in the upper half; H2 FY26 about £36.2m against about £38m a year earlier on today's perimeter.
  • FY2026 tax rate about 28 percent: ours 28.0 percent.
  • FY2026 capital expenditure about £100m: ours £100m. Core capital expenditure £45m to £55m a year: our totals of £80m and £65m in FY2027 to FY2028 include Poland (up to £30m, "bigger than originally envisaged") and Canada's bacon lines, which sit outside the core range.
  • Mid-single-digit adjusted operating profit growth on average, excluding seafood, not linear: ours, excluding seafood, about 5.6 percent a year over FY2026 to FY2030 (3.5, 5.9, 6.9 and 5.9 percent): consistent with the average, and below it in FY2027, which the "not linear" language allows.
  • Net bank debt 1.0x to 2.0x EBITDA: ours peaks at about 1.4x (FY2026 to FY2027) and falls to 1.0x in FY2029 and 0.8x in FY2030, below the range, which implies surplus capital management has said it would return; we do not assume a buyback.
  • Return on capital employed above 20 percent through the cycle, below 20 percent in FY2026: ours about 19 percent (FY2026), 19.8 percent (FY2027), about 21 percent (FY2028).
  • Canada "a small low-single-digit profit contribution in 2027" and "full profit benefits in 2029": ours £0.5m in FY2027 (below the guided language) and £5.0m in FY2029.
  • NADEC break-even in year one: ours nil in FY2027, £0.7m in FY2028.
  • Progressive dividend, cover rebuilt toward 2.0x: ours 1.54x rising to 1.86x by FY2030.
  • Cash conversion about 100 percent of net income excluding growth capital: reached on the company's definition by about FY2028; on our strict definition (after all capital expenditure and exceptional cash) only by FY2030.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • EBITDA: consensus EBITDA (£141m, £153m, £162m for FY2026 to FY2028; median of three brokers) is on the brokers' adjusted basis with depreciation of about £48.5m to £50.7m, which excludes right-of-use depreciation of about £24m a year (Peel Hunt, at £165m to £188m, appears to include it). Our IFRS 16 EBITDA less about £24m to £26m of right-of-use depreciation gives a brokers'-basis EBITDA of about £143m, £153m and £162m: FY2026 £2m above consensus (our operating profit is £2.4m higher), FY2027 to FY2028 in line. FactSet's history line (operating EBITDA, £138m in FY2025) is after adjusting items and is not comparable with either.
  • Operating profit (EBIT): consensus £93.1m, £99.6m, £107m, £109m (FY2029, one broker) against ours £95.5m, £101.0m, £108.0m, £116.5m. FactSet's history EBIT (£61.1m in FY2025) is after adjusting items; the company's continuing adjusted operating profit was £95.1m.
  • Profit before tax: consensus £67.9m, £74.0m, £79.6m, £84.8m against ours £69.0m, £73.0m, £80.0m, £89.0m. The company's figure is adjusted profit before tax on continuing operations; FY2025 was £73.2m (group, including discontinued units), £69.0m (continuing as reported in March 2026, still including Dalco's loss of about £4m) and about £73m on today's perimeter.
  • EPS: company adjusted EPS 56.0p (FY2025) against the brokers'-basis actual of 55.7p in FactSet's estimates database; FactSet's own "diluted before unusual items" EPS (60.3p) and reported basic EPS (53.3p) are different bases and must not be used for growth. Our EPS is on the company's adjusted basis after minorities: 54.0p, 56.6p, 61.9p, 68.6p, 74.5p against consensus 52.4p, 57.6p, 62.6p, 66.6p. The FY2026 gap is mainly tax (consensus implies about 29.7 percent of tax and minorities against our 28 percent plus £1m).
  • Revenue: the brokers'-basis FY2025 actual is £4,346m (as first reported, before the restatement for disposals); FactSet's fundamentals give £4,214.6m; today's perimeter is about £4,108m. Consensus revenue (£4,589m, £4,701m, £4,904m) is therefore on a higher base than ours; growth rates are not comparable across bases.
  • Net debt: the company's net bank debt excludes leases (£126.7m at FY2025, £194.4m at June 2026); FactSet's net debt includes them (£325m and £389m). Neither includes about £150m of customer-provided supply-chain finance.
  • Free cash flow: consensus (£10.6m, £45.0m, £66.6m, £72.1m) is FactSet's definition: operating cash flow less fixed-asset capital expenditure, before lease principal. The company's "adjusted free cash flow" (£53.6m in FY2025; +£10.4m in H1 FY26) excludes growth capital expenditure and, it appears, exceptional cash. Ours, below, deducts everything: interest, tax, lease principal, all capital expenditure, working capital and exceptional cash. On FactSet's definition ours would be about -£7m, £46m, £75m, £87m and £95m.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: Hilton Food Group plc (HFG-GB): an international secondary packer of retail red meat, seafood and fresh prepared food for leading grocers under long-term open-book cost-plus or fee-per-kilogram partnerships, reported as an East region (Australia, New Zealand, Central Europe including Polish fresh prepared food for Żabka, and a 49 percent equity-accounted joint venture with NADEC in Saudi Arabia), a West region (UK, Ireland, Netherlands, Sweden, Denmark, Portugal, and from January 2027 a dedicated Walmart Canada plant in Brantford, Ontario) and seafood businesses (Seachill in the UK and Foppen in the Netherlands and Greece) held outside both regions; Fairfax Meadow, 65 percent of Foods Connected and Dalco were sold in 2025 and 2026.

Request: T-D1

Peers: Cranswick (listed UK pork and poultry processor; the benchmark for capacity-led growth and automation in UK protein); Greencore (listed UK own-label chilled contract maker; a self-help and synergy precedent after exiting its US business and buying Bakkavor); Maple Leaf Foods (listed Canadian protein processor; the closest precedent for a greenfield protein plant start-up in Ontario, its London poultry plant, and for Canadian retail pricing); Scandi Standard (listed Nordic chicken processor; start-up and under-utilisation precedent at its Oosterwolde ready-to-eat plant); Bell Food Group (listed Swiss contract maker passing raw material through one-to-one; a close cost-structure analogue); LDC (listed French integrated poultry processor; NIAGARA capacity programme as a capacity start-up precedent); ABP Food Group, Kepak, Foyle Food Group and Dunbia (private integrated UK and Irish beef processors competing for Tesco and Irish retail volume); Danish Crown and Vion (private European primary processors used as price benchmarks by Nordic and Dutch retailers) Operating conditions: a new dedicated plant serving a single anchor retailer in a new country (Walmart Canada, January 2027); a capacity expansion in a fast-growing convenience category (Polish fresh prepared food); a turnaround of loss-making seafood units under a United States export restriction; a group overhead and operating-model transformation ("One Hilton"); beef price inflation in 2025 and 2026 followed by a possible plateau; falling Australian cattle availability in 2027 and 2028; UK National Living Wage and employer National Insurance rises; Bank of England rate rises in late 2026 and 2027; grocery price competition in the UK, Ireland, the Netherlands, Canada and Australia Inputs: Has Hilton previously taken a new dedicated plant in a new country (for example in Australia, New Zealand, Central Europe or Portugal) from start-up to its target return, and how long did each take, with what start-up losses? What does the Walmart Canada contract say, or what have the company and brokers said, about who bears the increase in the build budget from £60m to £80m (company, 2025 and 2026), whether capital or interest costs are passed through as Shore Capital (4 September 2024) described for these agreements, and how management reconciles a return above its 20 percent hurdle with Shore Capital's published model of about £65m of sales and £1.5m of operating profit in 2027 rising to over £5m by year three? How did Maple Leaf's London, Ontario plant and Scandi Standard's Oosterwolde plant ramp, and what did their start-up costs and duration turn out to be against plan? What has the Seachill cost and yield programme delivered so far, and what precedents exist for a UK white-fish processor returning to sustained profit after quota-driven price spikes? What savings target, if any, has Hilton attached to "One Hilton", and why were the benefits of its earlier "Genesis" transformation re-phased (remuneration report)? Can Hilton grow volume about in line with its grocers while holding processing profit per kilogram, given the record of 19.0p (FY2024), 18.2p (FY2025) and 17.7p (H1 2026) in company disclosures? What did Greencore, Cloetta and Cranswick deliver against their cost programmes and over what time? Focus: Whether Hilton's record shows it can bring a greenfield dedicated plant in a new country (Walmart Canada) to its stated return within about two to three years, and whether its seafood businesses can be returned to profit, on strengths it already has rather than strengths it would need to acquire.

Request: T-D2

Peers: Cranswick (listed; cost-of-production pricing and integration, a contrast in earnings volatility); Greencore (listed; a contract maker whose forecasts rested on a few large drivers, the Bakkavor synergies); Maple Leaf Foods (listed; dispersion of outcomes through a large plant start-up and network consolidation); Bell Food Group (listed; one-to-one pass-through contract maker); Scandi Standard (listed; utilisation-driven margin swings); LDC (listed; volume-led integrated processor with net cash); Cloetta (listed; deleveraging by postponing a greenfield plant); ABP Food Group and Kepak (private; integrated beef processors whose volume gains come at independent packers' expense) Operating conditions: beef price inflation and its reversal; Australian cattle supply contraction in 2027 and 2028 (Morgan Stanley, August and September 2026); a greenfield plant start-up in Canada; a United States Food and Drug Administration export restriction on a Greek smoked-salmon plant; anchor-customer volume re-allocation (Tesco's move to Scottish-sourced beef for Scottish stores in 2026); rising UK interest rates; a capital-expenditure peak (about £100m in 2026, company guidance); currency translation of Australian and New Zealand dollar profits (about 37 percent of revenue) Inputs: Which items separate Hilton's earnings in 2027 to 2029 from 2020 to 2025: the Walmart Canada ramp, Polish capacity, the seafood profit path, the NADEC joint venture's share of profit (Deutsche Bank: about £1.3m of net income by year three), the adjusted tax rate (29.8 percent in FY2025, 27.7 percent in H1 2026, company guidance about 28 percent for 2026), net finance cost (H1 2026: £8.2m bank interest, £3.8m lease interest, £4.2m customer-provided supply-chain finance; a 50 basis-point rate rise cited as about £2.1m), capital expenditure above the £45m to £55m core range (Poland up to £30m, Canadian bacon lines), and Australian volumes? Request: T-I1

Layer 3 and Layer 4: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months / Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

Written 2 November 2026, 22:51 to 23:10 UK, Fable, ricardinho login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Hilton Food Group (HFG-GB): third and fourth sets of questions, the predictability of the forecasts and what the market already reflects

Written 2 November 2026 at a share price of 715.5p (dashboard, 29 October 2026; the Financial Analyst's model used 726p on 28 October). Money in pounds sterling; per-share figures in pence. "Operating profit" is the company's adjusted operating profit on the continuing perimeter (excluding Fairfax Meadow, Foods Connected and Dalco); EPS is company adjusted after minorities unless stated; consensus is the FactSet median of 28 October 2026 (three visible brokers: Deutsche Bank, Peel Hunt, Shore Capital; FactSet's median rests on at least one more contributor).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Orientation: the model's cross-check gaps explained, the two inputs changed, and two things the Consumer must know before reading the model

The one-page summary shows the model's EPS 1p to 3p below the second set's cross-check in every year (53p, 54p, 59p, 66p, 72p against 54.0p, 56.6p, 61.9p, 68.6p, 74.5p) and its free cash flow above the cross-check by £19m, £9m, £4m, £2m and £1m. Each gap has an identifiable cause; one is an input error, which I correct.

  1. The FY2026 revenue base (an input error, corrected). The model measures FY2026 growth on the sum of its two restated FY2025 half-years (£1,986m plus £2,122m, £4,108m, which excludes Dalco), not on FactSet's annual £4,214.6m on which the second set built its 9.6 percent. 9.6 percent on £4,108m gives the model's £4,503m rather than the intended £4,619m, which is why the summary records that Part 3's half-year rates "were moved by -5.5 points to match the year". Because the EBITDA margin is a percentage input, every later year's revenue, EBITDA, operating profit and EPS is about 2.5 percent too low (operating profit £91.4m against £95.5m in FY2026; £96.7m against £101.0m in FY2027). The second set anticipated this ("if the model's FY2025 base is the restated £4,108m, the FY2026 input should be 12.4 percent"). I change the FY2026 revenue growth input to 12.4 percent. With it, the reported H1 FY26 (£2,289m) plus H2 at Part 3's 9.8 percent (£2,330m) equals £4,619m, 12.4 percent on £4,108m, so the Part 3 rates reconcile without being moved. This alone lifts the model's EPS by about 2.4p in FY2026 and about 2.9p by FY2030.
  2. Minorities (definitional). The model carries no minority interest; the cross-check deducts £1.0m to £1.3m (1.1p to 1.4p). After the revenue correction the model's EPS will sit about 1.2p above the cross-check, as the second set said it would. Not an error.
  3. Exceptional cash costs (definitional, and a model limitation). The model's free cash flow is before the restructuring, transformation and Foppen cash costs the cross-check deducts (£20m, £12m, £8m, £6m, £5m). The gaps of £19m, £9m, £4m, £2m and £1m are those amounts less the EBITDA shortfall from the revenue error (£4m to £5m a year), so they reconcile. The AlphaSense adjusting-items report shows cash exceptional costs of £14.8m in H1 FY26 alone (Foppen £7.0m, transformation £4.6m, restructuring £3.2m), management's guide of about £10m a year of transformation cash, and Seachill's consultation on about 90 redundancies begun in late October 2026. I therefore judge the second set's £20m for FY2026 and £12m for FY2027 too light and carry £24m, £15m, £10m, £7m and £5m. The model has no input for them. The Financial Analyst should add a line for exceptional cash costs of £24m, £15m, £10m, £7m and £5m for FY2026 to FY2030, deducted from free cash flow and from year-end net cash but not from adjusted earnings, so that the model's cash lines match the Analyst's cross-check.
  4. The finance charge (an input changed on new evidence). The interim results show £1.6m of bank interest capitalised into the Brantford plant in H1 FY26 (gross bank interest £9.8m, £8.2m charged); nothing was capitalised in H1 FY25. The second set's H1 charge of £13.0m, and its path from there, is net of that capitalisation. Capitalisation stops when the plant starts in January 2027, so about £3.2m a year of interest returns to the income statement in FY2027, less perhaps £0.7m of new capitalisation on the Polish build and the Canadian bacon lines. The second set's FY2027 rise of £1.5m (higher average debt, Bank Rate about 4.25 percent) did not include this. I raise net financial items to £30.0m (FY2027), £29.5m (FY2028), £28.5m (FY2029) and £27.5m (FY2030) from £28.0m, £28.0m, £27.5m and £27.0m, and leave FY2026 at £26.5m because H2 still capitalises. The rate path is two-sided (ING expects Bank Rate at 4.00 percent on 5 November 2026; Rothschild Redburn expects that reversed in 2027), so I take about £2m of the £2.5m step-up. The change costs about 1.6p of FY2027 EPS.

My cross-check after the two changes (company adjusted EPS after minorities; strict free cash flow after exceptional cash): EPS 54.0p, 55.1p, 60.7p, 67.7p and 74.1p for FY2026 to FY2030 (second set 54.0p, 56.6p, 61.9p, 68.6p, 74.5p; consensus 52.4p, 57.6p, 62.6p, 66.6p); free cash flow about -£36m, +£16m, +£45m, +£57m and +£66m. Against consensus: FY2026 +3 percent, FY2027 -4 percent, FY2028 -3 percent, FY2029 +2 percent. The model's EPS should read about 1.2p above these figures.

A presentation point for the IFRS 16 lines from FY2027. Companies House filings show Hilton Foods Canada signed a 20-year building lease in 2024 (annual payments £5.9m, indexed to CPI and capped at 2.25 percent; £19.1m of rent prepaid in 2025), to be recognised as a right-of-use asset and lease liability when it commences in 2027. That adds roughly £60m to lease liabilities, about £4m a year of right-of-use depreciation and about £2.5m of lease interest from FY2027, with the cost recovered through the Walmart fee if, as Shore Capital says of these agreements, "the vast majority of Hilton's lease exposure is directly covered under its long-term customer agreements". The EPS effect is nil; IFRS 16 EBITDA, depreciation and lease interest rise by similar amounts; FactSet's net debt (which includes leases) rises by about £60m without cash leaving. I have not put this into the inputs, because the net EPS and cash effects are close to zero and neither the recognition date nor the pass-through is disclosed; the Consumer should not read a jump in FactSet net debt or IFRS 16 EBITDA in FY2027 as a change in the business.

The return at the guideline fair multiple. The summary's total shareholder return of +95.5 percent to December 2027 rests on a guideline multiple of 20.64x two-year-forward earnings. Nothing in the three sets supports a multiple of that order for this phase: the five-year range of the 24-month-forward multiple is 8.1x to 18.1x with a median of 11.9x, today is 11.75x, and the first set judged the fair range low-to-mid teens (C-50), with the 17x to 20x of the compounder years belonging to a faster-growing business. The guideline multiple is topic #11 for the memo Analyst; I note only that the earnings component of the return (+10.5 percent to December 2027 on the model's EPS) is what my answers test, and that it falls slightly after my changes.

Co-movement of HFG-GB with its dashboard peers (computed 2026-11-02 from the master dashboard's daily prices)

What this is: daily close-to-close returns over rolling windows of 126 trading days (about six months). Correlation: how closely the daily moves line up (1 = always together, 0 = unrelated). Beta: how far the stock moves for a 1% move in the basket. R-squared: the share of the stock's daily price variance the peer basket explains; the remainder is the stock's own (idiosyncratic) movement. Basket: the peers below, equal-weighted. Prices only; the multiple's own history is in the financials block.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for the memo

Research requests for the next set

Company: Hilton Food Group plc (HFG-GB): an international secondary packer of retail red meat, seafood and fresh prepared food for leading grocers under long-term open-book cost-plus or fee-per-kilogram partnerships, reported as an East region (Australia, New Zealand, Central Europe including Polish fresh prepared food for Żabka, and a 49 percent equity-accounted joint venture with NADEC in Saudi Arabia), a West region (UK, Ireland, Netherlands, Sweden, Denmark, Portugal, and from January 2027 a dedicated Walmart Canada plant in Brantford, Ontario) and seafood businesses (Seachill in the UK and Foppen in the Netherlands and Greece) held outside both regions; Fairfax Meadow, 65 percent of Foods Connected and Dalco were sold in 2025 and 2026.

Request: OWN-1

Question: What is the term and expiry date of Hilton Food Group plc's supply agreement with Tesco for the UK and Ireland following the extension the company reported with its interim results on 3 September 2026, and how has the share of Tesco's fresh beef, lamb, pork and poultry supplied by Hilton Food Group changed since 2023 as Tesco has moved its Scottish stores to Scottish-sourced beef (reported by Hilton in September 2026) and signed long-term contracts with Foyle Food Group and Kepak for its Sustainable Beef Group (Tesco press release, 31 July 2026); what do Tesco's own statements, the Groceries Code Adjudicator's annual survey, Kantar or Worldpanel market data, the farming and grocery trade press, and former Tesco or Hilton Food Group employees say about any further beef, lamb or pork volume Tesco intends to move to integrated processors (ABP Food Group, Dunbia, Kepak, Foyle) or to source directly, and what has the Competition and Markets Authority's inquiry into ABP Food Group's stake in Dovecote Park (initial enforcement order 21 September 2026; commencement notice 28 October 2026) revealed about the structure and concentration of UK retail beef supply?

Request: OWN-2

Question: For Hilton Food Group plc's dedicated Walmart Canada plant in Brantford, Ontario (launch planned for January 2027; cumulative spend of £80.7m at June 2026 with about £25m more in the second half of 2026 and bacon lines added in 2027, per the interim results of 3 September 2026; a 20-year building lease with annual payments of £5.9m and £19.1m of prepaid rent, per Companies House filings of August 2026), what do Hilton's filings and calls, Walmart Canada's statements, Ontario government records, Canadian trade press and former Walmart Canada or Hilton Foods Canada employees say about the fee mechanism, the recovery of lease and capital costs through the Walmart fee, how the budget increase from £60m to £80m was shared between the parties, how much interest Hilton capitalised on the project in 2026 and when capitalisation ends, the number of Walmart stores and distribution centres served and the planned volume ramp through 2027; and, given that Walmart owns and operates its own case-ready beef plants in Thomasville, Georgia (opened 2020) and Olathe, Kansas (opened 2025) and three milk plants in the United States, what dated evidence is there of whether Walmart intends to extend the Hilton partnership to other regions, to own case-ready capacity in Canada itself, or to re-tender the Brantford supply at the end of the ten-year term?

Request: OWN-3

Question: What do investors, as distinct from the sell-side, say about Hilton Food Group plc since its interim results of 3 September 2026: the published commentary of institutional holders and UK small-cap investment trusts that hold the shares (including Western Standard, which notified a 5.01 percent holding on 22 July 2026, and the co-founders Philip and Robert Heffer), fund-manager letters and factsheets, retail-investor forums and social media, the financial press and podcasts; which parts of the case do they stress (the portfolio exits, Walmart Canada, the dividend, the valuation, Foppen, Australian beef, contract renewals, the new management team under Mark Allen) and which do they not mention; how has the shareholder register changed during 2026 (new holders, sellers, short positions disclosed to the Financial Conduct Authority); and what do the published notes or press reports of Peel Hunt and Berenberg, which cover the stock alongside Deutsche Bank and Shore Capital, say about FY2027 and the drivers of their forecasts?
